A new gauge for gravitational perturbations of Kerr spacetimes I: The linearised theory
Gabriele Benomio

Abstract
We propose a new geometric framework to address the stability of the Kerr solution to gravitational perturbations in the full sub-extremal range | a | < M |a|<M . Central to our framework is a new formulation of nonlinear gravitational perturbations of Kerr, whose two novel ingredients are the choice of a geometric gauge and non-integrable null frames both tailored to the outgoing principal null geodesics of Kerr. The vacuum Einstein equations for the perturbations are formulated in our gauge as a system of equations for the connection coefficients and curvature components relative to the chosen frames. When renormalised with respect to Kerr, the null structure equations with the form of outgoing transport equations do not possess any derivatives of renormalised connection coefficients on the right hand side. In this work, we derive the linearised vacuum Einstein equations around Kerr in the new framework. The system of linearised gravity exhibits two key structural properties. The first is well-known and consists of the exact decoupling of two gauge invariant linearised quantities in the system, satisfying two decoupled spin ± 2 \pm 2 Teukolsky equations. The second is a new , gauge dependent structure in the outgoing transport equations for the linearised connection coefficients inherited from the nonlinear system: Unlike previous works, these equations do not contain any derivatives of linearised connection coefficients on the right hand side and induce a new hierarchy of only outgoing transport equations including all gauge dependent linearised quantities. Our new framework is designed to effectively capture the stabilising properties of the red-shifted transport equations, thereby isolating one of the crucial structures of the problem. Such a feature is suggestive of future simplifications in the analysis. As an illustration, our companion work [ 6 ] employs the system of linearised gravity and its enhanced red-shifted transport equations, specialised to the | a | = 0 |a|=0 case, to produce a new simplified proof of linear stability of the Schwarzschild solution. The full linear stability analysis in the full sub-extremal range | a | < M |a|<M is deferred to future work. As already apparent from [ 6 ] , our framework will allow to combine the new structure in the transport equations with the elliptic part of the system to establish a linear orbital stability result without loss of derivatives , indicating that the framework may be well suited to address nonlinear stability in the full sub-extremal range | a | < M |a|<M .



1
Introduction

The two-parameter family of Kerr spacetimes
[
37
]
is widely believed to be the unique family of stationary, asymptotically flat black hole solutions to the vacuum Einstein equations

\ric(g)=0

(1)

and thus to characterise all the possible stationary endstates of vacuum black hole dynamics
[
38
]
.

A fundamental open problem in general relativity is to formulate a mathematical proof of nonlinear stability for the Kerr exterior region, in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
, as a solution to the Cauchy problem
[
12
]
for (
1
):

Open Problem
.

For all vacuum initial data sufficiently close to the initial data corresponding to a Kerr exterior solution with parameters
(a_{0},M_{0})
,
|a_{0}|<M_{0}
, the maximal solution to (
1
) asymptotically settles down to a nearby Kerr exterior solution with parameters
a\approx a_{0}
and
M\approx M_{0}
.

See Conjecture IV.1 in
[
18
]
for a more precise formulation.

The nonlinear stability of the trivial solution to (
1
), Minkowski space, has first been proven in the monumental work
[
14
]
. The breakthrough work
[
18
]
is the first nonlinear stability result, without symmetry assumptions, for a black hole solution to (
1
), namely the Schwarzschild solution.
1
1

1

The work
[
18
]
characterises the dynamics of all vacuum black holes which arise from initial data in a neighbourhood (in moduli space) of the initial data of a given Schwarzschild solution and asymptote a (possibly different)
Schwarzschild
solution.
The series of works
[
41
,
42
,
26
,
40
,
53
,
27
]
prove the nonlinear stability of the Kerr solution in the slowly rotating regime
|a|\ll M
. Related works are discussed in Section
1.4
of the present introduction.

1.1
Linear stability as a building block for nonlinear stability

Proving nonlinear stability for a solution to (
1
) poses a number of both conceptual and technical difficulties. Most of the key conceptual difficulties, however, already appear, and can be understood, in the context of
linear
stability, namely the study of the stability of solutions to the system of linearised vacuum Einstein equations (or, for brevity, system of
linearised gravity
). This fact has motivated the growing activity around the mathematical study of the linear stability of black holes, although the subject has a long (and, to some extent, independent from the interest in nonlinear problems) tradition in the physics literature (see Section
1.1.1
).

To be employed as a
direct building block in a proof of nonlinear stability
, the mathematical formulation of a linear stability result should include some essential ingredients, or
Steps
:

•

Step 1: Nonlinear gauge

As a first step, one has to break the general covariance of the equations (
1
) by choosing a gauge in which the nonlinear vacuum Einstein equations can be formulated as a well-posed initial value problem for a system of nonlinear partial differential equations.

•

Step 2: Linearisation

To move from the nonlinear to the linear theory, one has to implement a linearisation procedure to linearise the vacuum Einstein equations in the gauge chosen in Step 1.
2
2

2

Our prescription here should be contrasted to an approach which linearises “ungauged” equations and later employs so-called “linear gauges”.
The linearised system obtained should come with a natural notion of solutions and initial data and inherit well-posedness from the nonlinear equations.

Ideally, once the linearised system of equations is derived, one wishes to never refer back to the nonlinear theory. In fact, one should be able to handle the final linearised system of equations without any knowledge about its derivation and phrase all linear stability statements as statements for solutions to the linearised system.

•

Step 3: Orbital stability

An essential stability result to be proven should be a uniform boundedness statement for solutions, and derivatives of solutions, to the linearised system of equations in terms of an explicit initial data (energy) quantity.
3
3

3

Orbital stability is a weaker notion of stability than
asymptotic
stability (see Step 4), in that it does not require that solutions to the linearised system decay. However, we should point out that, in general, one does not expect to be able to achieve an orbital stability result without proving decay for at least some of the quantities in the system.
To be a true orbital stability result, and be applicable to nonlinear problems, it is important that such a statement
does not lose derivatives
at the top-order energy level.

•

Step 4: Asymptotic stability

The core of a linear stability result should be a uniform decay statement for all solutions to the linearised system of equations in terms of an explicit initial data (energy) quantity. The decay statement will necessarily lose derivatives, but the loss should be
quantifiable
and, at least in principle, compatible with nonlinear applications. One also has to ensure that the decay statement obtained is
quantitative
and again, at least in principle, sufficient for nonlinear applications. In this sense, the decay rates of certain quantities need to be sufficiently fast.

1.1.1
The Schwarzschild solution

An important milestone in the mathematical study of linear stability is the work
[
17
]
proving the linear stability of the Schwarzschild solution.
4
4

4

The work
[
17
]
is more general than the direct linear analogue of
[
18
]
, in that it allows convergence to a linearised
Kerr
solution.
The linear stability result
[
17
]
is carried out in line with the prescription of Steps 1-4 and ultimately serves as the fundamental building block in the nonlinear stability result
[
18
]
. Related linear results for the Schwarzschild solution and other solutions to (
1
), including the Kerr solution, have recently appeared and are discussed in Section
1.4
of the present introduction.

The work
[
17
]
is centered around long celebrated ideas and contributions from the physics literature on black holes. Among these, particularly prominent is the realisation that one can derive decoupled equations for some of the quantities in the system of linearised gravity. Indeed, it has been known since the original work
[
5
]
that the system of linearised gravity on Schwarzschild allows, if suitably formulated, the
exact decoupling
of two
gauge invariant
linearised quantities into two decoupled equations, known as
spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations
.
5
5

5

The Teukolsky equations can be formulated for general spin
s\in\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{Z}
. For
s=0
, the Teukolsky equations (both) coincide with the wave equation, for
s=\pm 1
they govern the dynamics of the extremal components of electromagnetic perturbations. For the sake of the present introduction, we only refer to the case
s=\pm 2
. We note that some of the results cited hold for general spin.
These quantities correspond to the linearised extremal curvature components in the Newman–Penrose formalism
[
51
]
. Decoupled
wave-like
equations, known as
Regge–Wheeler

[
52
]
and
Zerilli

[
63
]

equations
, have been previously derived following a different approach to linearised gravity, based on metric perturbations (see also
[
58
,
48
]
). Work of Chandrasekhar
[
10
]
developed a transformation theory mapping fixed-frequency
mode
solutions to the spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations to solutions to the Regge–Wheeler equation, connecting the two perspectives.

Crucially, the approach to Steps 1 and 2 of the work
[
17
]
in a
double-null gauge
yields a system of linearised gravity which admits the well-known decoupling of two gauge invariant quantities into Teukolsky equations. To exploit this decoupling in the stability analysis of Steps 3 and 4, the work
[
17
]
preliminarily addresses the asymptotic properties of
general
solutions to the spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations on Schwarzschild. As a first step, work
[
17
]
generalises Chandrasekhar’s theory to a transformation theory for
general
solutions to the Teukolsky equations and establishes uniform boundedness and decay for the transformed Regge--Wheeler quantities.
6
6

6

The
mode
stability analysis of the Regge–Wheeler equation already appears in the original work
[
52
]
.
The transformation theory of
[
17
]
is formulated in physical space and allows, as a second step, to translate the decay for the Regge–Wheeler quantities into decay for the original Teukolsky quantities. As an application to the linear stability problem, uniform decay for the decoupled gauge invariant quantities is established
independently from the other quantities in the system
. We point out that the asymptotic analysis of the Regge–Wheeler equation and, in turn, of the Teukolsky equations builds upon a series of works, culminating in
[
20
,
19
]
, developing important robust methods in the study of the scalar linear wave equation

\Box_{g}\,\varphi=0

on black hole exterior spacetimes.

The proof of stability for the gauge invariant quantities captures an essential feature of the dynamics of the problem, namely the
hyperbolic
character of the system of linearised gravity, and, in fact, already controls
all
the dynamical degrees of freedom independent of the choice of gauge
[
59
]
. In view of this, it has been a common (mis)belief in the physics literature that a stability result
7
7

7

In fact, a
mode
stability result.
for the gauge invariant quantities constitutes a conclusive linear stability result. However, to successfully apply the linear theory to the nonlinear stability problem, one needs a
full
linear stability result, namely a stability statement for the
full
system of linearised gravity. The remaining quantities to control in the system are
gauge dependent
. Hence, the structure of the remaining part of the system and its stability analysis are very much sensitive to the approach adopted in Step 1 of the problem, where a choice of gauge is made.

By adopting a double-null gauge, work
[
17
]
is the first to resolve the gauge dependent part of the analysis for the system of linearised gravity on Schwarzschild. After gaining control over the gauge invariant quantities, the proof of
[
17
]
exploits a
hierarchical structure
in the transport equations in the system to prove decay for
all
the gauge dependent quantities.

1.1.2
The Kerr problem

As discovered by the seminal work
[
57
]
, a suitable formulation
8
8

8

We already point out that such a formulation cannot be seen as the Kerr analogue of the approach to Steps 1 and 2 of
[
17
]
, namely a formulation in a double-null gauge. See the end of Section
1.3.2
.
of the system of linearised gravity on Kerr still admits the exact decoupling of two gauge invariant linearised quantities into decoupled spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations. The
mode
stability analysis of these equations is, in the case of Kerr, already highly non-trivial. Relying on the crucial fact that, in analogy with the wave equation
[
8
]
, the spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations on Kerr fully separate, the celebrated work
[
61
]
proves the non-existence of exponentially growing mode solutions. As key steps towards addressing the stability of
general
solutions, work
[
4
]
extends the mode stability result
[
61
]
to the real axis and work
[
56
]
achieves a
quantitative
mode stability statement. The asymptotic analysis of general solutions is considerably more complicated. The results
[
47
,
16
]
establish uniform boundedness and decay for
general
solutions to the Teukolsky equations in the slowly rotating regime
|a|\ll M
. The work
[
54
]
resolves the frequency space analysis of the equations without any restriction on the spacetime parameters, to be seen as the main missing ingredient towards proving uniform boundedness and decay for the Teukolsky quantities in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
. We remark that all these works address the spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations on Kerr and their asymptotic properties
independently
from the full linear stability problem. In fact, a system of linearised gravity on Kerr is
not
derived in these works.

As in the Schwarzschild case, one desires
a formulation of Step 1 leading to a system of linearised gravity
both
amenable to the decoupling of the gauge invariant quantities
and
possessing a hierarchical structure sufficient to prove stability for
all
the quantities in the system
. However, exploiting the decoupling and decay of the gauge invariant quantities in the context of the full linear stability problem for Kerr is more subtle. Indeed, regardless of the formulation chosen, the equations in the gauge dependent part of the system present a stronger coupling, due to the fact that the Kerr solution is only stationary. Furthermore, for reasons discussed in Section
1.3.2
, a formulation of the problem capturing the decoupling of the gauge invariant quantities is significantly more difficult to implement than in the Schwarzschild case, and necessarily yields a system of linearised gravity with new, more intricate structures in its gauge dependent part. These additional complications are related to the use of
non-integrable frames
and the appearance of the
antitraces
of the second fundamental forms in the equations.

We point out that instances of systems of linearised gravity on Kerr possessing a hierarchical structure compatible with the decoupling of the gauge invariant quantities have been noted, and exploited, in a considerable number of works in the physics literature. Examples of such works are those concerning metric reconstruction problems
[
62
]
, in which, starting from the decoupled gauge invariant quantities, one wants to reconstruct the corresponding linearised metric perturbations. The metric perturbations are typically obtained by descending a hierarchy of transport equations in the system, in which each equation is sourced by one or more of the previously computed quantities
[
15
,
11
,
46
]
. However, it is important to note that employing this type of hierarchies in a proof of linear stability would
not
lead to a full stability analysis
in line with the prescription of Steps 3 and 4
,
9
9

9

We also note that the systems of linearised gravity on Kerr in which these hierarchies appear typically fail to be derived as prescribed by Steps 1 and 2. See the related footnote
2
.
one typical obstruction being the wasteful loss of derivatives required to establish uniform decay statements. Finding a hierarchy which is suitable to our objective is a much more delicate task.

The purpose of the present work is to develop a
new geometric framework to address the
full
linear stability of the Kerr solution to gravitational perturbations in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
. In particular, this work resolves Steps 1 and 2 of the linear stability problem
10
10

10

The well-posedness of the vacuum Einstein equations in our gauge, which is stated as a feature of Step 1 in Section
1.1
, is not addressed in the present work.
by (1)
introducing a
new
formulation of nonlinear gravitational perturbations of the Kerr solution
and (2)
deriving a
new
system of linearised gravity on Kerr exterior spacetimes
. The new linearised system reconciles the two aforementioned aspects of the problem. Indeed, as a result of the choices made in the formulation of nonlinear gravitational perturbations, the linearised system obtained allows for the well-known decoupling of two gauge invariant linearised quantities and, at the same time, exhibits a
new hierarchical structure
in its gauge dependent part making the stability analysis of the full system tractable.

In the future, we intend to perform the full stability analysis of our system of linearised gravity in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
and,
for the first time
, in line with the prescription of Steps 3 and 4. Our resolution of the linear stability problem will provide the fundamental ingredient towards a proof of nonlinear stability of the Kerr solution in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
. Although the nonlinear stability problem presents a number of additional (and daunting) technical difficulties, the transition from the linear to the nonlinear analysis in our framework is not expected to present any new conceptual elements.

1.2
The new system of linearised gravity

To implement our framework and derive a system of linearised gravity with the features described, some specific
choices
in the formulation of the problem are to be made. In this section, we give a brief outline of the derivation of our new system of linearised gravity and the choices that it entails. Further motivation and technical comments on our choices appear in Section
1.3
.

As we shall see in the present section, all our choices are made in the formulation of nonlinear perturbations (Section
1.2.2
). The result of our choices is a new nonlinear
geometric
framework tailored to the
outgoing principal null geodesics
of Kerr. The fact that our framework privileges the outgoing direction reflects in a new convenient form of the outgoing transport equations in both the nonlinear and linearised systems of equations (Section
1.2.3
) and in the enhanced role played by the event horizon and red-shift effect in the future analysis (see the later Section
1.5.1
).

1.2.1
Preliminaries: The Kerr exterior manifold

We start with some preliminaries. Let
a
and
M
be real parameters, with
|a|<M
. Through a procedure already sketched in Section
3.2.1
, we define the
Kerr exterior manifold

(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
, with ambient manifold

\mathcal{M}:=(-\infty,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\,.

(2)

We refer to the boundary

\mathcal{H}^{+}:=\partial\mathcal{M}

as the
(future) event horizon
. We define the
algebraically special

\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}=(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}},e_{3}^{\text{as}},e_{4}^{\text{as}})

1.2.2
Nonlinear perturbations

To formulate nonlinear perturbations of the Kerr exterior manifold, we start by choosing real parameters
a
and
M
, with
|a|<M
. We fix the manifold-with-boundary
\mathcal{M}
, which we identify with the Kerr ambient manifold (
2
) with star-normalised, outgoing principal differentiable structure
(s,\underline{s},\theta^{A})
. We introduce the fixed smooth scalar functions
\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}
and
k_{a,M}
of the fixed coordinates on
\mathcal{M}
.

Once the background structure is fixed, we prescribe a one-parameter family of smooth Lorentzian metrics

\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon):=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\epsilon)\,ds^{2}+2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)\,ds\,d\underline{s}+2\,\boldsymbol{b}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,ds\,d\theta^{A}+2\,\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,d\underline{s}\,d\theta^{A}+\,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)\,d\theta^{A}d\theta^{B}

(9)

on
\mathcal{M}
, with

\displaystyle\partial_{\underline{s}}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{\underline{s}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0

(10)

on
\mathcal{M}
for all
\epsilon
. For each
\epsilon
, the metric quantities
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)
are smooth scalar functions on
\mathcal{M}
, the metric quantities
\boldsymbol{b}(\epsilon)
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}(\epsilon)
are one-tensors on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-spheres and the metric quantity
\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\epsilon)
is a symmetric two-tensor on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-spheres. All quantities are regular on the whole
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. We assume that
\boldsymbol{g}(0)=g_{a,M}
with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{a}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(0)

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{b}_{\theta^{A}}(0)

\displaystyle=b_{\theta^{A}}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(0)

\displaystyle=\underline{b}_{\theta^{A}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}(0)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}\,,

i.e. the metric coefficients of
\boldsymbol{g}(0)
relative to the fixed differentiable structure coincide with the Kerr metric coefficients relative to the star-normalised, outgoing principal differentiable structure of Kerr.

The form (
9
) of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, together with the additional identities (
10
), correspond to a choice of gauge for the family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, called
outgoing principal gauge
. See Section
1.3.1
.

To the one-parameter family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, we associate a one-parameter family of local frames

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)=(\eo(\epsilon),\etw(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon))

on
\mathcal{M}
such that
13
13

13

A choice of the frame vector fields
(\eo(\epsilon),\etw(\epsilon))
is not required.

\displaystyle\ef(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=k_{a,M}\,\partial_{\underline{s}}

\displaystyle\et(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{s}+\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\underline{s}}+\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\theta^{A}}\,.

(11)

For each
\epsilon
, the frame quantities
\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)
are smooth scalar functions on
\mathcal{M}
and the frame quantity
\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}(\epsilon)

, including on

. The frame quantities are

)-(

.

As a result of their definition, the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
satisfy the following properties:
14
14

14

The proof of some of the properties is not immediate from the definition of the frames. See already the related comments in Section

.

•

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are null frames

The frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are null relative to the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon
.

•

\ef(\epsilon)
and
\et(\epsilon)
are global, regular and non-degenerate

The frame vector fields (

) are global, regular and non-degenerate vector fields on the whole
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

•

\ef(\epsilon)
is fixed

The frame vector field

\mathcal{M}
for all

and satisfies the identity

\ef(\epsilon)=e_{4}^{\text{as}}

(12)

for all
\epsilon
.

•

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)
is the algebraically special frame

We have

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)=\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}

with

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(0)

\displaystyle=\underline{j}_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(0)

\displaystyle=\underline{k}_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(0)

\displaystyle=\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}_{a,M}\,,

i.e. the frame coefficients of
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{3}}(0)
relative to the fixed differentiable structure coincide with the frame coefficients of the vector field
e_{3}^{\text{as}}
relative to the star-normalised, outgoing principal differentiable structure of Kerr.

•

\boldsymbol{\omegah}(\epsilon)
,
\boldsymbol{\xi}(\epsilon)
and “
\boldsymbol{\etab}(\epsilon)
” are fixed

The perturbed connection coefficients and curvature components are defined as the connection coefficients and curvature components of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
. For
\epsilon=0
, all the perturbed geometric quantities coincide with the corresponding connection coefficients and curvature components of the Kerr metric
g_{a,M}
relative to the algebraically special frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
.

As a result of our choices, a selected set of perturbed connection coefficients remain fixed on
\mathcal{M}
for all
\epsilon
and coincide, in a suitable sense, with their respective Kerr quantities.
15
15

15

The statement that the perturbed connection coefficient
\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\epsilon)

and coincides with the connection coefficient
\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)

.
These connection coefficients are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omegah}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{4}}\et(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon))\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{4}}\ef(\epsilon),\ea(\epsilon))\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{4}}\et(\epsilon),\ea(\epsilon))

(13)

and dictate how the frame vector fields
\et(\epsilon)
and
\ef(\epsilon)
are transported along the integral curves of

.

Our choice of frames is
new
. In particular, the property that the perturbed connection coefficient
\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\epsilon)
remains fixed has no analogue in previous works.

This concludes the formulation of nonlinear perturbations of the Kerr exterior manifold. We emphasise that the formulation involves
two choices
, namely the choice of gauge for the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
and the choice of frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
. The
choice of frames is the most important novelty of our framework
, as it is ultimately responsible for the new structural properties of our system of linearised gravity. See Sections
1.2.3
and
1.3
.

1.2.3
From nonlinear perturbations to the system of linearised gravity

The derivation of the system of linearised gravity on the Kerr exterior manifold then follows three more steps and does not involve any additional choices:
16
16

16

Strictly speaking, there is some freedom in setting up the linearisation procedure. However, all the possible linearisation procedures are equivalent from the point of view of the key structures of the linearised system obtained.

(i)

A comparison procedure

We first establish a geometric procedure to compare the perturbed quantities to the corresponding Kerr quantities on the fixed manifold
\mathcal{M}
.
17
17

17

Such a geometric procedure is needed because the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
induce orthogonal distributions which
vary
with
\epsilon
(see Appendix
A
and Section
1.3.2
for the meaning of these words). For a brief discussion of the geometric procedure, see already Section
3.4
of the overview. Although we do not elaborate on this step of the problem in the present introduction, implementing this geometric procedure represents a major technical ingredient of our framework.

(ii)

The vacuum Einstein equations

Secondly, we assume that the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
solve the vacuum Einstein equations for all
\epsilon
, which are formulated as a nonlinear system of equations for the perturbed connection coefficients and curvature components of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
. Once the vacuum Einstein equations are assumed to hold, we refer to nonlinear perturbations as nonlinear
gravitational
perturbations of the Kerr exterior manifold. By exploiting our comparison procedure, one can take geometric differences of perturbed and Kerr quantities and
renormalise
the vacuum Einstein equations relative to the Kerr background.

Our choices in the formulation of nonlinear perturbations enforce a
new
structure
in the renormalised vacuum Einstein equations. The new structure characterises the renormalised
null structure equations
. In our renormalised system, these equations are of three different types: Outgoing transport equations of the schematic form

\nablasl_{4}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(\epsilon)-\widetilde{\Gamma}_{a,M})=(\boldsymbol{\psi}(\epsilon)-\psi_{a,M})\,,

(14)

ingoing transport equations of the schematic form

\nablasl_{3}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(\epsilon)-\widetilde{\Gamma}_{a,M})=\nablasl(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\epsilon)-\Gamma_{a,M})+(\boldsymbol{\psi}(\epsilon)-\psi_{a,M})

(15)

and elliptic equations of the schematic form

\nablasl(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(\epsilon)-\widetilde{\Gamma}_{a,M})=\nablasl(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\epsilon)-\Gamma_{a,M})+(\boldsymbol{\psi}(\epsilon)-\psi_{a,M})\,,

(16)

where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(\epsilon)
,
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\epsilon)
denote distinct perturbed connection coefficients,
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\epsilon)
perturbed curvature components and
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{a,M}
,
\Gamma_{a,M}
,
\psi_{a,M}
their corresponding Kerr quantities. The schematic equations (
14
)-(
16
) only include the top-order terms, with
\nablasl
denoting the Kerr projected covariant derivative (see Section
1.3.2
). Crucially, as a result of our choice of frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
, all the
outgoing
transport equations do
not
contain any derivatives of renormalised connection coefficients on the right hand side. Indeed, all the derivatives of renormalised connection coefficients that would appear on the right hand side of such equations are derivatives of one of the fixed connection coefficients (
13
). A similar structure is not present in the ingoing transport and elliptic equations.

(iii)

The system of linearised gravity

Finally, we linearise the system of renormalised vacuum Einstein equations around the Kerr exterior manifold, i.e. around
\epsilon=0
. The final system of linearised gravity can be handled without any knowledge about its derivation or reference to the nonlinear formulation of the problem. The unknowns of the system of linearised gravity are the linearised induced metric, frame coefficients,
18
18

18

See Section
1.3.2
for the meaning of the induced metric and frame coefficients.
connection coefficients and curvature components, denoted by the superscript
\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{}
. The system consists of three sets of equations, namely a set of equations for the linearised frame coefficients, the linearised null structure equations and the linearised Bianchi equations. The equations for the linearised frame coefficients are (both ingoing and outgoing) transport and elliptic equations.

Two of the linearised curvature components in our system, namely

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\alpha}}\,,

(17)

are
gauge invariant
linearised quantities and
exactly decouple
into spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations.

The new structure of the nonlinear null structure equations directly transfers to the linearised system: The vanishing of the linearised quantities

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\hat{\omega}}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\xi}\,,

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\eta}}

(18)

implies that
all
the
outgoing
transport equations for the linearised connection coefficients do
not
possess any derivatives of linearised connection coefficients on the right hand side
(see, for instance, the equations (
19
)-(
22
) below). Furthermore, these equations exhibit an overall
weak coupling
with the rest of the system. Some examples of weakly coupled equations are the linearised second variational formula

\nablasl_{4}\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\chi}}+(\text{tr}\chi)\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\chi}}-\omegah\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\chi}}=-\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\alpha}\,,

(19)

which only couples with the linearised curvature component
\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\alpha}
, the transport equation
19
19

19

The linearised equations (
20
)-(
22
) are obtained by linearising the equations (
29
)-(
31
) appearing in Section
1.3.2
. The reader should refer to Section
1.3.2
for a preliminary discussion of the meaning of the antitraces of the second fundamental forms appearing in the equations (

)-(
23
).

\displaystyle\nablasl_{4}\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}+\chi\times\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}+\omegah\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\underline{\chi})\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\chi}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\,{{}^{\star}\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\chi}}}

(20)

\displaystyle-\widehat{(\nablasl\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\not{g}}})^{\sharp_{3}}\cdot\underline{\eta}}+(\nablasl\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\not{g}}})^{\sharp_{1}}\cdot\underline{\eta}-\frac{1}{2}\,\underline{\eta}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\nablasl(\textup{tr}\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{g}})-(\not{\textup{div}}\,\underline{\eta}+(\underline{\eta},\underline{\eta}))\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\not{g}}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)\,{}^{\star}\underline{\eta}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,(-2\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{3}+\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}})+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})\,{}^{\star}\underline{\eta}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{4}

\displaystyle+(\nablasl_{3}\underline{\eta}-\underline{\chi}^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\underline{\eta})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{4}+(\nablasl_{4}\underline{\eta}-\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\underline{\eta})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{3}

for the linearised ingoing shear, which decouples from any linearised connection coefficient other than the linearised outgoing shear, and the linearised Raychaudhuri equation

\nablasl_{4}(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\chi})+(\text{tr}\chi)(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\chi})-\omegah\,(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\chi})=(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})

(21)

and transport equation for the linearised antitrace of the outgoing second fundamental form

\nablasl_{4}(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})+(\text{tr}\chi)(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})-\omegah\,(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})=-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\chi})\,,

(22)

which form a completely decoupled pair of equations. The absence of linearised frame coefficients on the right hand side of the equations (
19
) and (
21
)-(
22
) originates from the frame identity (
12
) and contributes to the weak coupling.

Similarly,
all
the
outgoing
transport equations for the linearised induced metric and frame coefficients do
not
possess any derivatives of the linearised induced metric or frame coefficients on the right hand side and are weakly coupled with the rest of the system.

Analogous structures are not present in the linearised transport equations in the
ingoing
direction. In fact, all the ingoing transport equations for the linearised connections coefficients couple with a derivative of a linearised connection coefficient on the right hand side. An example is the second variational formula

\nablasl_{3}\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}+(\text{tr}\underline{\chi})\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}=-2\,\not{\mathcal{D}}{}_{2}^{\star}\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\xi}}+(\underline{\eta}-\eta)\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\xi}}-\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\alpha}}\,,

to be contrasted with the equation (
19
). Similarly, all the ingoing transport equations for the linearised induced metric and frame coefficients couple with a derivative of a linearised induced metric or frame coefficient on the right hand side. These equations also exhibit a stronger coupling with the rest of the system than their outgoing counterpart. In this regard, one may compare the equation (
21
) with the corresponding linearised Raychaudhuri equation in the ingoing direction

\displaystyle\nablasl_{3}(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\underline{\chi}})+(\text{tr}\underline{\chi})(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\underline{\chi}})=

\displaystyle\,2\,\not{\text{div}}\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\xi}}+2\,(\underline{\eta}-\eta,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\xi}})+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}})+(\text{tr}\underline{\chi})\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\hat{\underline{\omega}}}

(23)

\displaystyle-(\nablasl(\text{tr}\underline{\chi}),\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}})-\nablasl_{4}(\text{tr}\underline{\chi})\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}_{3}-\nablasl_{3}(\text{tr}\underline{\chi})\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}_{4}\,,

where some extra linearised frame and connection coefficients now appear on the right hand side.

The new structure of the outgoing transport equations in the linearised system allows, crucially, to order these equations into a
new
hierarchy
including
all
linearised quantities (see Appendix
B
). The new hierarchy has the potential to unlock the stability analysis of the full system, which could be carried out in line with the prescription of Steps 3 and 4 of Section
1.1
. See Section
1.5.1
.

1.3
Technical features and challenges of our framework

In this section, we elaborate on the choices encountered in the implementation of our framework of Section
1.2
and briefly discuss the technical difficulties that they pose. As we shall see, the main technical difficulties arise from our choice of frames.

1.3.1
A geometric framework

We start with a brief comment on the geometric nature of our framework.

As outlined in Section
1.2
, our formulation of nonlinear gravitational perturbations involves the choice of a new
geometric
gauge for the vacuum Einstein equations, called
outgoing principal gauge
. In an outgoing principal gauge, the fixed curves of constant
(s,\theta^{A})
on
\mathcal{M}
are (i) integral curves of the vector field
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
and (ii)
null geodesics
of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)

for all
\epsilon
. Indeed, the gauge identities (
10
) imply the identity

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\underline{s}\,\underline{s}}(\epsilon)=-\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}\,\delta_{\underline{s}}^{\mu}

(24)

for the Christoffel symbols of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon
, with
x^{\mu}=(s,\underline{s},\theta^{A})
. By inspecting the geodesic equation, it is an easy check that the identity (
24
) suffices to establish that curves of constant
(s,\theta^{A})
are geodesics of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon
, which are moreover null in view of the metric form (
9
) (in particular, one exploits
\boldsymbol{g}_{\underline{s}\,\underline{s}}(\epsilon)=0
).

As part of the fully rigorous implementation of our framework in the bulk of the paper, we will prove that assuming the metric form (
9
) and the identities (
10
) for all
\epsilon
can indeed be legitimately viewed as a choice of gauge for the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
. See already Section
3.3.3
.

Other instances of geometric gauges which have been employed to formulate the vacuum Einstein equations are double-null gauges
[
13
,
18
]
, constant mean curvature (and maximal) gauges
[
14
]
, Bondi gauges
[
39
]
and radiation gauges
[
2
]
. Geometric gauges may be contrasted to
wave
gauges
[
43
,
44
]
, for which the coordinates are solutions to a system of wave equations.

In our framework, the vacuum Einstein equations are formulated as a nonlinear system of equations for
geometric quantities
, corresponding to the connection coefficients and curvature components of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the null frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
. These quantities have a manifest geometric meaning and some of the equations in the system can be identified as classical equations from differential geometry (e.g. the Codazzi equation (
32
)). In addition to the advantages that we shall describe in the present section, the use of frame quantities directly relates with the so-called
tetrad formalisms
, such as the aforementioned Newman-Penrose formalism, which have found extensive application in the treatment of the black hole stability problem in the physics literature.

Proving that our frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are well-defined and satisfy the properties listed in Section
1.2.2
requires some care. In particular, an abstract local existence result for the system of ODEs defining the frames will account for their existence, whereas our choice of gauge for the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
will suffice for the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
to satisfy the desired properties. See already Section
3.3.4
.

The geometric character of the nonlinear equations transfers to the linearised system. Indeed, within our framework, the linearisation procedure is performed
geometrically
and yields a linearised system of equations
in geometric form
for the linearised connection coefficients and curvature components.

1.3.2
The divorce of frames and spacetime foliation

The exact decoupling of the gauge invariant quantities (
17
) into spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations is a significant advantage guaranteed by our system of linearised gravity, in that it allows to address the analysis of these quantities independently from the other quantities in the system (see Section
1.5.1
). However, as we shall now explain, it comes at the price of dealing with new technical difficulties in the formulation of nonlinear perturbations and with new intricacies in the (linearised) system of vacuum Einstein equations.

The gauge invariance and exact decoupling of two linearised curvature quantities are fundamentally tied to some delicate algebraic properties of the Kerr exterior manifold. In loose terms, these algebraic properties correspond to the existence of exactly two null directions, known as outgoing and ingoing
principal null directions
, which are shear-free geodesic directions “diagonalising” the Riemann curvature tensor. For such algebraic properties to become manifest in our problem, a specific choice of frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)
in the formulation of nonlinear perturbations is
necessary
. Such a choice corresponds to the
algebraically special frame
of Kerr

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)=\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}\,,

(25)

as outlined for our frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
in Section
1.2
, and is a choice of a null frame whose null frame vector fields are aligned with the two principal null directions of Kerr. Relative to the frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
, the connection coefficients

\displaystyle{\widehat{\chi}}_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}_{a,M}\,,

(26)

\displaystyle{\xi}_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle{\underline{\xi}}_{a,M}

(27)

and curvature components

\displaystyle{\alpha}_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle{\underline{\alpha}}_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle{\beta}_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle{\underline{\beta}}_{a,M}

(28)

of the Kerr metric identically
vanish
. The vanishing of these quantities, encoding the shear-free (i.e. (
26
)), geodesic (i.e. (
27
)) and “curvature-diagonalising” (i.e. (
28
)) nature of the frame, is what ultimately enforces the gauge invariance and exact decoupling of two linearised curvature quantities.

Crucially,
for
|a|>0
,
the algebraically special frame of Kerr is a
non-integrable
null frame
,
20
20

20

In stark contrast, the algebraically special frame of the Schwarzschild exterior manifold (
|a|=0
) is
integrable
. See Section
1.3.3
.
in fact forcing our frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
to be non-integrable frames
for all
\epsilon
.
21
21

21

We think of non-integrability of a frame as an open condition: Given a one-parameter family of frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
, if the frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)
is non-integrable, then the frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
is non-integrable for
\epsilon
sufficiently small.
See Appendix
A
for an introduction to the notion of non-integrable frames.

Being non-integrable, the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
cannot be possibly
adapted
to
any
foliation of the ambient manifold
\mathcal{M}
, meaning that the induced orthogonal (relative to
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
) distributions

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}:=\left\langle\et(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon)\right\rangle^{\perp}

cannot be possibly identified (even locally) with the tangent bundles to the two-dimensional leaves of any foliation of
\mathcal{M}
.
22
22

22

This fact follows from Frobenius theorem. See Appendix
A
.
In particular,
the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are
not
adapted to the fixed foliation of
\mathcal{M}
induced by the fixed
(s,
,
θ
^A)
{-differentiablestructure}.~Theframes
\boldsymbol{$}{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are nonetheless tailored to the fixed null geodesics of constant
(s,\theta^{A})
(or, equivalently, to the fixed hypersurfaces of constant
s
) on
\mathcal{M}
, which are generated by the frame vector field
\ef(\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon
.

Non-integrable frames are, in general, more complicated to handle than frames adapted to a spacetime foliation. Some instances of complications, which do not arise for adapted frames, are the following:

•

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors

The connection coefficients and curvature components of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are defined as
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors
, meaning as smooth sections of the abstract tensor bundle

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}\otimes\ldots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)})^{\star}\otimes\ldots\otimes(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)})^{\star}

on
\mathcal{M}
. Being the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
non-integrable, these quantities cannot be interpreted as tensors on a sub-manifold of
\mathcal{M}
, as it is, on the other hand, possible for adapted frames. One related limitation is that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors cannot be written in coordinate form, in that non-integrable distributions do not admit coordinate (co-)bases.

•

Second fundamental forms

A crucial role in the problem is played by the outgoing and ingoing second fundamental forms

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\ef(\epsilon),\eb(\epsilon))\,,

\displaystyle{\underline{\boldsymbol{\chi}}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\et(\epsilon),\eb(\epsilon))\,.

The non-integrability of the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
implies that the second fundamental forms possess non-vanishing outgoing and ingoing
antitraces

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}})(\epsilon)

(see already Section
3.1.2
), which complicate the treatment of these geometric quantities. In the case of adapted frames, the second fundamental forms are
symmetric
, i.e. their antitraces identically vanish.

•

Induced connection

The definition of induced geometric quantities over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
involves new technicalities. The spacetime metric
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
and Levi-Civita connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\epsilon)
naturally induce a metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
and a linear connection

\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\epsilon)

over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
. For adapted frames, the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\epsilon)
is
Levi-Civita
relative to the induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
. In our framework, being
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
a non-integrable distribution, the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\epsilon)
has
torsion
(see already Section
3.1.3
), as it is the case for
any
linear connection over a non-integrable distribution. Thus, the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\epsilon)
is
not
Levi-Civita relative to the induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
. Differential operators acting on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors are defined relative to
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\epsilon)
and require some extra care to take the torsion of
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\epsilon)
into account.

•

Vacuum Einstein equations

To formulate the vacuum Einstein equations for the connection coefficients and curvature components of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
, one has to derive a new system of vacuum Einstein equations. Our nonlinear system of equations differs from the one in
[
14
]
, in that the system of
[
14
]
is formulated for connection coefficients and curvature components relative to an adapted frame.
23
23

23

For
\epsilon=0
, one can easily check that the connection coefficients and curvature components of the Kerr exterior manifold relative to the algebraically special frame do
not
solve the system of vacuum Einstein equations of
[
14
]
.
The appearance of the antitraces of the second fundamental forms in both the null structure and Bianchi equations is the main novelty of our system. Some examples of equations where the antitraces appear are the outgoing Raychaudhuri equation
24
24

24

We remove the
\epsilon
in the equations (
29
)-(
32
) to keep the notation lighter.

\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})^{2}-\boldsymbol{\omegah}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})=-(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\chi}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\chi}})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})^{2}\,,

(29)

the outgoing transport equation for the antitrace of the outgoing second fundamental form

\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})+(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})-\boldsymbol{\omegah}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})=0\,,

(30)

the outgoing transport equation for the ingoing shear

\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\underline{\widehat{\chi}}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\underline{\widehat{\chi}}}+\boldsymbol{\omegah\underline{\widehat{\chi}}}=-2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}{}_{2}^{\star}\,\underline{\eta}}+\boldsymbol{\underline{\eta}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\underline{\eta}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\underline{\chi}})\,\boldsymbol{\widehat{\chi}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}})\,{}^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,{}^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\underline{\widehat{\chi}}}

(31)

and the Codazzi equation

\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\,\widehat{\chi}}=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})-\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\nablasl}(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})-\boldsymbol{\widehat{\chi}^{\sharp}\cdot\zeta}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\zeta}-(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\eta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\,.

(32)

Dealing with the complications coming from the use of the non-integrable frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
is one of the main themes and technical challenges of our framework. Several of these complications transfer to the system of linearised gravity, one example being the appearance of the linearised antitraces of the second fundamental forms (see already the equations (
21
)-(
23
)).

We point out that, to enforce the gauge invariance and exact decoupling of two linearised curvature quantities, it is not only necessary, but also
sufficient
that the frames adopted in the formulation of nonlinear perturbations satisfy the property (
25
). The other properties satisfied by our frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
(see Section
1.2.2
) are necessary to produce the new hierarchical structure in the linearised system and mitigate the complications introduced in the equations by non-integrability. Such properties can only be achieved by exploiting wisely the freedom
25
25

25

To enforce the gauge invariance and exact decoupling of two linearised curvature quantities, one has the freedom to choose the frames among the infinite one-parameter families of null frames such that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)=\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
. In particular, being the frames inevitably non-integrable, the freedom in the choice of frames is not limited by any of the constraints coming from possibly adapting the frames to the fixed spacetime foliation.
in the choice of frames, as done by our implicit definition of the frames through the system of ODEs (
41
)-(
43
).

We mention an additional technical difficulty arising from our choice of frames which is not directly ascribable to non-integrability:

•

Frame coefficients

The frame vector field
\et(\epsilon)
is only defined implicitly, meaning that the frame quantities
\boldsymbol{\underline{j}}(\epsilon)
,
\boldsymbol{\underline{k}}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{\underline{\Lambda}}(\epsilon)
from (
11
) are only defined implicitly and cannot be explicitly related to the metric quantities from (
9
). It is thus convenient to introduce the
frame coefficients
of
\et(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{f}}}_{4}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(\et(\epsilon),e_{4}^{\text{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{f}}}_{3}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(\et(\epsilon),e_{3}^{\text{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{f}}}_{A}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=g_{a,M}(\et(\epsilon),e_{A}^{\text{as}})\,.

(33)

The frame coefficients (
33
), together with additional frame coefficients defined in the bulk of the paper, appear extensively in the problem and replace, in most cases, the use of the metric quantities from (
9
) and frame quantities from (
11
). In fact, the set of all frame coefficients, along with the induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
, constitute the full set of perturbed quantities at the level of the metric appearing in the equations.

The linearised frame coefficients are unknowns of the system of linearised gravity (see already the equations (
20
) and (
23
)). The set of equations for the linearised frame coefficients that we alluded to in Section
1.2.3
is obtained by suitably linearising the commutators of the
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
-frame vector fields. For the convenient structure of the outgoing transport equations for the linearised frame coefficients discussed in Section
1.2.3
, it is crucial that the frame vector field
\ef(\epsilon)
remains fixed for all
\epsilon
.

We conclude with a remark about the potential use of adapted frames in the problem. By Frobenius theorem (see Appendix
A
),
all
one-parameter families of frames adapted to a fixed foliation of
\mathcal{M}
are necessarily families of
integrable
frames, and thus fail at including the algebraically special frame of Kerr as a member of the family. For this reason, the use of adapted frames is
in
compatible with the gauge invariance and exact decoupling of two linearised curvature quantities, for which the inclusion of the algebraically special frame is necessary.
26
26

26

The use of adapted frames
is
compatible with the exact decoupling of two gauge invariant linearised quantities in the special case
|a|=0
, in that the algebraically special frame of Schwarzschild is integrable. In fact, as we shall see in Section
1.3.3
, frames adapted to suitable foliations of the Schwarzschild exterior manifold do guarantee the exact decoupling of two gauge invariant linearised quantities.

1.3.3

The
|a|=0
case

We briefly describe our framework in the Schwarzschild
|a|=0
case.

For what concerns the star-normalised, outgoing principal differentiable structure
(s,\underline{s},\theta^{A})
and the algebraically special frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
of the Schwarzschild exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{0,M})
, two special properties arise. The first is that the coordinate
s
is null (see Figure
2
). The second is that the algebraically special frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
is
integrable
and
adapted
to the foliation induced by the star-normalised, outgoing principal differentiable structure.

\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}
\mathcal{I}^{+}
\mathcal{M}^{*}

\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}
\mathcal{I}^{+}

\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}
\mathcal{I}^{+}

Figure 2
:
The figure depicts the Penrose diagram of the manifold
(\mathcal{M}^{*},g_{0,M})
and the foliation of
(\mathcal{M}^{*},g_{0,M})
induced by the coordinates
s
(in blue) and
\underline{s}
(in red).

In the formulation of nonlinear perturbations, our framework retains the use of non-integrable frames. In fact, the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are non-integrable
for all
|\epsilon|>0
.
27
27

27

The retention of non-integrability follows from choosing frames such that all the perturbed connection coefficients (
13
) remain fixed for all
\epsilon
. In particular, to achieve the property that the perturbed connection coefficient
\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\epsilon)
remains fixed, the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are, in general, to be non-integrable for
|\epsilon|>0
.

By setting
|a|=0
in our linearised system of equations on Kerr, one obtains a
new
system of linearised gravity on Schwarzschild exterior spacetimes. Compared to the Kerr case, many more background quantities in the system identically vanish, including the background antitraces of the second fundamental forms (since
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
is an integrable frame). However, as in the Kerr case, the use of non-integrable frames in the formulation of nonlinear perturbations introduces new intricacies in the gauge dependent part of the linearised system, such as the presence of the linearised antitraces of the second fundamental forms. One instance is the linearised Codazzi equation

\not{\text{div}}\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\chi}}+\ldots=\frac{1}{2}\,\nablasl(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\chi})-\frac{1}{2}\,{}^{\star}\nablasl(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})-\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\beta}+\ldots\,,

in which the linearised antitrace of the outgoing second fundamental form appears on the right hand side.

The system admits the exact decoupling of the gauge invariant quantities (
17
) into spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations. The outgoing transport equations for the linearised induced metric, frame coefficients and connection coefficients present the new structure and weak coupling that we discussed in Section
1.2.3
for the Kerr case. In fact, the essence of the new structure of these equations can be more easily appreciated in the Schwarzschild case, where the additional coupling terms arising from linearising around a (only) stationary spacetime are absent. The equations (
20
)-(
22
) now assume an even simpler form: The transport equation

\nablasl_{4}\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}+\chi\times\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}+\omegah\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\underline{\chi}}}=-\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\underline{\chi})\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\chi}}

(34)

for the linearised ingoing shear
only
couples with the linearised outgoing shear and the linearised Raychaudhuri equation

\nablasl_{4}(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\chi})+(\text{tr}\chi)(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\chi})-\omegah\,(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\chi})=0

and transport equation for the linearised antitrace of the outgoing second fundamental form

\nablasl_{4}(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})+(\text{tr}\chi)(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})-\omegah\,(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})=0

are
completely
decoupled. As in the Kerr case, the outgoing transport equations can be ordered into a
new hierarchy
including
all
linearised quantities in the system.

Our new system of linearised gravity on Schwarzschild can be employed in a
new
proof of full linear stability which revisits, and significantly simplifies, some of the key aspects of the analysis from previous works. See the companion work
[
6
]
by the author.

To conclude, it is important to point out that, in the Schwarzschild
|a|=0
case, the use of non-integrable frames in the formulation of nonlinear perturbations is
not
necessary for the gauge invariance and exact decoupling of two linearised curvature quantities. Indeed, in an outgoing principal gauge, a choice of frames
adapted
to the fixed spacetime foliation
for all
\epsilon
would still allow the decoupling of two gauge invariant linearised quantities and avoid the complications of non-integrability altogether. In fact, the use of non-integrable frames is a new element of our framework which is absent in previous works on the linear stability of Schwarzschild. The work
[
17
]
, for instance, considers nonlinear perturbations in a double-null gauge and adopts a family of frames adapted to a fixed double-null foliation of the Schwarzschild exterior manifold for all
\epsilon
.

1.4

Related works

We give a brief account of the most recent developments in the study of both linear and nonlinear stability of black hole solutions to gravitational perturbations.

As discussed in Section
1.1.1
, the breakthrough work
[
17
]
is the first to establish the linear stability of the Schwarzschild solution to gravitational perturbations. Subsequent work
[
36
]
proves the linear stability of Schwarzschild in a generalised harmonic gauge (see also
[
35
,
33
,
34
]
). Work
[
23
]
proves the linear stability of the Reissner–Nordström solution in the full sub-extremal range.

For what concerns the linear stability of the Kerr solution, two works carry out the analysis of the full system of linearised gravity:

•

The work
[
1
]
establishes a uniform decay statement for solutions to linearised gravity in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
. The result assumes integrated decay estimates for the gauge invariant linearised quantities outside the slowly rotating regime
|a|\ll M
and requires a sufficiently high loss of derivatives. An improved control of the regularity loss seems necessary to directly apply the result to the nonlinear problem.

The system of linearised gravity is formulated for spin-weighted (tetrad) quantities in the Geroch–Held–Penrose (GHP) formalism and assumes that the linearised perturbations satisfy a set of “linear gauge conditions”, known as (linear)
outgoing radiation gauge
conditions. In the follow-up paper
[
2
]
, such gauge conditions are shown to arise from a choice of (nonlinear) outgoing radiation gauge for sufficiently small nonlinear perturbations of Kerr. The outgoing radiation gauge is tailored to the
ingoing
principal null geodesics of Kerr. In
[
2
]
, the choice of gauge is combined with a choice of null tetrads similarly tailored to the
ingoing
principal null geodesics of Kerr. The vacuum Einstein equations for the tetrad quantities in the outgoing radiation gauge are proven to be well-posed. By linearising the nonlinear system of
[
2
]
, one obtains a system of linearised gravity which, although different from the one presented in
[
1
]
, can be controlled by relating its linearised quantities to the ones in
[
1
]
.

For what concerns the analysis, a key role is played by a hierarchical structure present in the
ingoing
transport equations in the linearised system.

•

The work
[
29
]
establishes a uniform decay statement for solutions to linearised gravity in the slowly rotating regime
|a|\ll M
. An improved control of the regularity loss seems necessary to directly apply the result to the nonlinear problem.

The system of linearised gravity is formulated for metric perturbations in a harmonic gauge. The analysis employs tools from microlocal analysis and spectral theory which allow to take a perturbative (relative to the
|a|=0
case) approach to the proof. In particular, the decoupling of gauge invariant linearised quantities into spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations for
|a|>0
is not exploited.

Recent work
[
3
]
presents a mode stability analysis of the system of
[
29
]
in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
, providing one of the missing ingredients towards the extension of
[
29
]
to the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
. Work
[
3
]
exploits the seminal mode stability result
[
61
]
for the Teukolsky equations.

The work
[
18
]
and the series of works
[
41
,
42
,
26
,
40
,
53
,
27
]
resolve the nonlinear stability problem for Schwarzschild and slowly rotating Kerr black holes respectively. See also prior work
[
39
]
for a proof of nonlinear stability of Schwarzschild black holes in polarised axisymmetry and recent work
[
45
]
as a step towards proving the nonlinear stability of Kerr black holes in harmonic gauge. We point out that the work
[
26
]
presents a geometric framework and a system of vacuum Einstein equations which make use of various non-integrable frames. The works
[
25
,
24
]
initiate the study of the linear stability problem for Kerr–Newman black holes.

For positive cosmological constant, the nonlinear stability of slowly rotating Kerr-de Sitter black holes is proven by the work
[
30
]
. See also the recent works
[
22
,
21
]
proving the linear and nonlinear stability of the slowly rotating Kerr-de Sitter solution with a different approach. Without smallness assumption on the rotational parameter, the work
[
9
]
proves a mode stability result for the Teukolsky equations on Kerr-de Sitter black holes in a partial range of spacetime parameters (and, in fact, provides a revisited proof of the original result
[
61
]
for Kerr black holes).

For negative cosmological constant, the work
[
28
]
proves mode stability for the Teukolsky equations on Kerr–anti-de Sitter black holes (satisfying the Hawking–Reall bound), initiating the study of linear stability for these spacetimes. The nonlinear dynamics of Kerr–anti-de Sitter black holes remains an open problem and may lead, for certain boundary conditions, to instabilities
[
31
]
. See the related breakthrough works
[
49
,
50
]
for pure AdS space as a solution to the Einstein equations with matter.

1.5

Outlook

We describe some open directions related to the present work.

1.5.1

Linear stability of Kerr

Future work by the author will perform the stability analysis of the system of linearised gravity in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
. As in the
|a|=0
case of
[
6
]
, the analysis will produce uniform boundedness and decay statements for solutions to the linearised system in line with the prescription of Steps 3 and 4 of Section
1.1
. In particular, the linear stability result will include top-order uniform boundedness and (degenerate) integrated decay statements for all linearised curvature components which
do not lose derivatives
, indicating that the framework may be well suited to address nonlinear stability in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
.

By virtue of the structure of the system of linearised gravity, one may implement a full stability analysis divided into two parts. The first part of the analysis is already
known
and exploits the exact decoupling of the
gauge invariant
quantities (
17
) into decoupled spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky equations. Indeed, one can directly transfer the result
[
54
]
(and upcoming companion work by the authors) to establish uniform decay for these quantities independently from the other quantities in the system. For the second part, the new hierarchical structure of the system suggests the possibility to implement a
new scheme
to prove uniform decay for all the remaining
gauge dependent
quantities in the full sub-extremal range
|a|<M
and, for the first time, in line with the prescription of Steps 3 and 4 of Section
1.1
.

We expect the outgoing transport equations in the system to play a more prominent role in the gauge dependent analysis than in previous works. In particular, a crucial role would be played by the
red-shifted
transport equations for the linearised connection coefficients available in the scheme (see Appendix
B
), as for instance the equation (
20
) for the linearised ingoing shear. The red-shifted transport equations can be employed to prove local integrated decay by direct, forward-in-
\underline{s}
integration from the initial data.
28
28

28

One would also need to exploit that the right hand side of the equation suitably decays.
One such instance, which can already be appreciated in the
|a|=0
analysis of
[
6
]
, would be the proof of local integrated decay for the linearised ingoing shear by direct integration of the equation (
20
).

The red-shift effect at the event horizon has been exploited in a large number of works on the stability analysis of sub-extremal black hole exteriors. The fact that our new framework effectively isolates this important structure in the analysis is suggestive of potential simplifications in the nonlinear problem in our framework.

1.5.2

Further directions in linearised gravity

Beyond its primary application to the linear stability of Kerr, our system of linearised gravity can be employed in a number of related problems. One interesting example could be the study of conservation laws for solutions to our system of linearised gravity on Kerr. Work
[
32
]
derives conservation laws and flux bounds for solutions to the system of linearised gravity on Schwarzschild of
[
17
]
. For
|a|=0
, our system may be used to present a different approach to deriving conservation laws and flux bounds.

From a more general perspective, our framework may be adapted to derive systems of linearised gravity on other black hole exterior spacetimes. Some natural examples would be
Kerr–(A)dS
exterior spacetimes, as solutions to the vacuum Einstein equations with cosmological constant, and
Kerr–Newman
exterior spacetimes, as solutions to the Einstein–Maxwell equations. One may expect the systems to enjoy similar features to the ones characterising our system, including the exact decoupling of two gauge invariant linearised quantities and a new hierarchical structure in its gauge dependent part. Linear stability, a scattering theory and conservation laws for linearised gravity on these spacetimes could then be addressed. In the particular case of Reissner–Nordström exterior spacetimes, a new system of linearised gravity may be employed to revisit (and possibly simplify) the proof of linear stability of
[
23
]
in the same fashion as described in Section
for the Schwarzschild case.
