2

Structure of the paper

The bulk of the present paper is divided into seven sections, namely Sections
4
-
10
. The content of each of the sections is summarised in Section
3
, which serves as an overview. In this section, we give a brief outline of the paper, which accounts for its overall structure and logic.

The first two sections of the bulk of the paper are preliminary sections:

•

In Section
4
, we develop the necessary geometric formalism to formulate the vacuum Einstein equations as a system of equations for the connection coefficients and curvature components of a spacetime
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
relative to a general null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
. Crucially, the frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is allowed to be a non-integrable frame. The technical difficulties related to non-integrable frames are all addressed in this section. The equations are derived in Section
4.8
.

See Section
3.1
for an overview to this section of the paper.

•

In Section
5
, we define the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
and the (non-integrable) algebraically special frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
and outgoing principal differentiable structures of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
. By making the identification
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})=(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}=\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
, we exploit the formalism of Section
4
to treat the connection coefficients and curvature components of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
.

See Section
3.2
for an overview to this section of the paper.

At this point, one is ready to start the formulation of the problem proper:

•

In Section
6
, we formulate nonlinear perturbations of the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
. The formulation employs both the algebraically special frame and an outgoing principal differentiable structure of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
from Section
5
. This section is the core of the paper and includes the main elements of our new geometric framework.

In Sections
6.2
and
6.4
, we prove technical statements concerning our choice of gauge and frames respectively. These two sections are independent modules and can be skipped by the reader without affecting the overall logic of the paper.

See Section
3.3
for an overview to this section of the paper.

Before proceeding with the formulation of the problem, a first technical digression is required:

•

In Section
7
, we prescribe a geometric procedure to compare the perturbed connection coefficients and curvature components with their corresponding Kerr background quantities. The need for such a procedure is related to certain technical complications arising from our choice of frames.

See Section
3.4
for an overview to this section of the paper.

One can now formulate the (renormalised) vacuum Einstein equations for the perturbations:
29
29

29

The vacuum Einstein equations are nowhere assumed in Sections
6
and
7
.

•

In Section
8
, we assume that the perturbations of Section
6
solve the vacuum Einstein equations. To formulate the equations for the perturbed connection coefficients and curvature components, one employs the formalism and system of equations of Section
4
. By taking differences of the perturbed and corresponding Kerr quantities as prescribed in Section
7
, we renormalise the system of vacuum Einstein equations relative to the Kerr background.
30
30

30

More conveniently, we will present a slightly different system of equations, dubbed
reduced
vacuum Einstein equations, which can be easily used by the reader to derive the renormalised vacuum Einstein equations.

See Section
3.5
for an overview to this section of the paper.

Section
8
concludes the nonlinear formulation of the problem. To derive the system of linearised gravity, a second (and last) technical digression is required:
31
31

31

Section
9
is the first section which departs from the nonlinear theory. The treatment of Sections
6
-
8
is fully nonlinear.

•

In Section
9
, we linearise the renormalised quantities obtained by taking geometric differences of perturbed and corresponding Kerr background quantities via the procedure of Section
7
. The linearisation formulae derived in Section
9
are general and could, in principle, have been derived immediately after Section
7
.

See Section
3.6
for an overview to this section of the paper.

In the last section, we achieve the formulation of the system of linearised gravity on Kerr exterior spacetimes:

•

In Section
10
, we apply the linearisation formulae of Section
9
to linearise the system of renormalised vacuum Einstein equations and derive the system of linearised gravity on the Kerr exterior manifold. This section is entirely self-contained and allows to handle the system of linearised equations without knowledge of the rest of the paper.

See Section
3.7
for an overview to this section of the paper.

To summarise, the paper presents three systems of equations. The general vacuum Einstein equations appear in Section
4.7
. The reduced vacuum Einstein equations appear in Section
8.1
. The system of linearised gravity on Kerr exterior spacetimes appears in Section
10
.

3

Overview

We give an overview of the present work. Each of the sections in the overview corresponds to a section of the bulk of the paper.

3.1

The vacuum Einstein equations

In Section
4
, we start by considering a
3+1
-dimensional, smooth, time-oriented Lorentzian manifold
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
, endowed with the Levi-Civita connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
of the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
. We consider a general, possibly
non-integrable
, local null frame on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
, meaning a local null frame

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}=(\eo,\etw,\et,\ef)

whose associated local
horizontal distribution

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}:=\left\langle\et,\ef\right\rangle^{\boldsymbol{\perp}}

is possibly a
non-integrable
distribution on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. The frame vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
are assumed to be
orthonormal
relative to
\boldsymbol{g}
and form a local frame of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
. See Section
4.1
.

In Section
4
and throughout the paper, we adopt the bolded notation of
[
17
]
in the nonlinear setting.

3.1.1

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors

In Section
4.1
, we define
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(k,q)
-tensors
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
as smooth sections of the abstract
horizontal
(k,q)
-tensor bundle

\otimes_{k}\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\otimes_{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})^{\star}

on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. We refer to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(1,0)
-tensors as
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
and to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(0,q)
-tensors as
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

q
-tensors
.

In Section
4.2
, the frame connection coefficients and curvature components of the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
relative to the frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
are defined as smooth scalar functions and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

q
-tensors on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
.

In Section
4.3
, we define contractions and products of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors.

3.1.2

Decomposition of the second fundamental forms

The second fundamental forms

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi}{}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\ef,\eb)\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\chi}}{}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\et,\eb)

are
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
two-tensors and can be decomposed into
symmetric traceless
,
pure trace
and
antisymmetric parts
as follows

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\chi}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\chi}}

\displaystyle=\underline{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\chi}}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\underline{\boldsymbol{\chi}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
and
\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}
are the metric and volume form induced over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
by the spacetime metric
\boldsymbol{g}
and spacetime standard volume form
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
respectively. We refer to the smooth scalar functions

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}})

as the
antitraces
of the second fundamental forms. The antitraces of the second fundamental forms both identically vanish if and only if
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable. Equivalently, the antitraces of the second fundamental forms both identically vanish if and only if the commutator

[\boldsymbol{e_{A}},\boldsymbol{e_{B}}]=({}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\boldsymbol{\not{H}{}_{B}^{C}}-{}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{\not{H}{}_{A}^{C}})\,\boldsymbol{e_{C}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,\boldsymbol{e_{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,\boldsymbol{e_{4}}

(35)

of the frame vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
is generated by the frame vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
. See Section
4.2.1
.

3.1.3

The induced connection and its torsion

The spacetime Levi-Civita connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
induces a canonical linear connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors. Crucially, the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
is the
Levi-Civita
connection of the induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is
integrable
.
32
32

32

As we shall prove in Section
4.4
, a stronger statement holds true: There
exists
a Levi-Civita connection of
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors if and only if
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.
In fact, for non-integrable distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
, the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
has non-trivial
torsion
.
33
33

33

We note that, on the other hand, the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
is always compatible with the induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
, regardless of the integrability of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
. See Section
4.4
.
The torsion of
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
arises from the failure of the commutator (
35
) to be generated by the frame vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
, which implies the relation

\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\eb-\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\ea\neq[\ea,\eb]\,,

where the left hand side is, by definition of
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
, a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field. See Section
4.4
.

In Section
4.4
, we define a set of differential operators acting on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors in terms of the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
. The formal definition of such operators is, in most part, analogous to that from previous works
[
17
,
18
]
. However, to handle some of these operators, the torsion of
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
has to be taken into account. One relevant example is the second covariant derivative

\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}}\,,

which appears in the definition of the
induced Riemann curvature
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
. Due to the properties of the second covariant derivative
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}
when the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
has torsion, it turns out that the induced Riemann curvature
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
is a
tensorial

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
quantity if and only if
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is
integrable
. Similarly, the symmetries of
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
depend on the (non-)integrability of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
. See Section
4.5
.

3.1.4

The nonlinear system of equations

In Section
4.7
, we assume that the Lorentzian manifold
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
solves the vacuum Einstein equations

\boldsymbol{\ric}(\boldsymbol{g})=0\,.

The nonlinear system of equations is formulated as a system of equations for the connection coefficients and curvature components of the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
relative to the frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
, and thus it is a system for
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors. The complete system of equations appears in Sections
4.7.1
-
4.7.2
. The full system of equations is divided into two coupled sets of equations, namely the
null structure equations
and the
Bianchi equations
. The Bianchi equations encode the
hyperbolic
character of the vacuum Einstein equations. Our nonlinear system of equations reduces to that of
[
14
]
when the frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is integrable.

3.1.5

Formal derivation of the equations

In Section
4.8
, we present the formal derivation of the nonlinear system of equations of Section
3.1.4
. The main new technical ingredient is the presence of the antisymmetric parts of the second fundamental forms in the computations. Another new technicality arises in the derivation of the
Gauss equation
. As already mentioned, due to the torsion of the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
, one has to deal with
non-tensorial

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
curvature quantities. The Gauss equation is written in terms of a
corrected Gauss curvature

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{K}}}
, which we introduce to treat the curvature of non-integrable distributions. See Section
4.5
.

3.2

The Kerr exterior manifold

In Section
5
, we define the
Kerr exterior manifold
and the
algebraically special frame
and
outgoing principal differentiable structures
of the Kerr exterior manifold.

3.2.1

Definition of the Kerr exterior manifold

In Section
5.1
, we define the manifold-with-boundary

\mathcal{M}:=(-\infty,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}

(36)

with coordinates
\bar{t}^{*}\in(-\infty,\infty)
,
y^{*}\in[0,\infty)
and standard spherical coordinates
(\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
. As usual, the coordinates
(\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})
only cover a subset of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. We define the
(future) event horizon
as the boundary

\mathcal{H}^{+}:=\partial\mathcal{M}

of
\mathcal{M}
and
(future) null infinity
as the formal hypersurface

\mathcal{I}^{+}:=\left\{\bar{t}^{*}=\infty\right\}\,.

Given real parameters
a
and
M
, with
|a|<M
, we define two positive constants
r_{\pm}
and a new regular coordinate
\bar{r}_{a,M}=\bar{r}_{a,M}(y^{*})
(see Section
5.1
). We will simply denote
\bar{r}_{a,M}
by
\bar{r}
and refer to the coordinates
(\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r},\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})
as
Kerr coordinates
. We note that
\mathcal{H}^{+}=\left\{\bar{r}=r_{+}\right\}
.

We define the
Kerr family of metrics
as the two-parameter family of Lorentzian metrics
g_{a,M}
on
\mathcal{M}
such that

\displaystyle g_{a,M}=

\displaystyle-\left(1-\frac{2\,M\,\bar{r}}{\Sigma}\right){d\bar{t}^{*}}^{2}+2\,d\bar{t}^{*}\,d\bar{r}+\Sigma\,{d\bar{\theta}}^{2}+\frac{(\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta\,a^{2}\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}\,{d\bar{\phi}^{*}}^{2}

(37)

\displaystyle-2\,a\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}\,d\bar{r}\,d\bar{\phi}^{*}-\frac{4\,a\,M\,\bar{r}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}\,d\bar{t}^{*}\,d\bar{\phi}^{*}\,,

where the smooth scalar functions
\Delta(\bar{r})
and
\Sigma(\bar{r},\bar{\theta})
are defined in Section
5.1
. The metric (
37
) is manifestly smooth on
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
can be checked to be a null hypersurface relative to
g_{a,M}
. See Figure
3
.

The smooth Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
will be referred to as the
Kerr exterior manifold
. One can check that
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
solves the vacuum Einstein equations.

\mathcal{H}^{+}
\mathcal{I}^{+}

Figure 3
:
Penrose diagram of the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
.

In Section
5.2
, we introduce the auxiliary
Kerr-star differentiable structure

(t^{*},r,\theta,\phi^{*})

on
\mathcal{M}
. These coordinates are regular on the whole manifold
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and primarily differ from Kerr coordinates in that the level sets of
t^{*}
are everywhere spacelike, horizon-penetrating and asymptotically flat hypersurfaces. The definition of Kerr-star coordinates requires the use of a radial cut-off function, which prevents an
explicit
form of the Kerr metric in these coordinates. For this reason, Kerr-star coordinates are not employed in the first place to
define
the Kerr metric. On the other hand, Kerr-star coordinates are directly employed to
define
the
star-normalised, outgoing principal differentiable structure
. See Section
3.2.3
.

3.2.2

The algebraically special frame

In Section
5.3
, we define the
algebraically special
(local) null frame

\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}=(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}},e_{3}^{\text{as}},e_{4}^{\text{as}})

of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
(see already Section
1.2.1
). The associated horizontal distribution

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}:=\left\langle e^{\text{as}}_{3},e^{\text{as}}_{4}\right\rangle^{\perp}

is globally well-defined on
\mathcal{M}
. For
|a|>0
, the frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
is
non-integrable
, with
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
a non-integrable distribution.

The induced metric, connection coefficients and curvature components of
g_{a,M}
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
are
global
and
regular

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors on
\mathcal{M}
and are explicitly computed in Section
5.4
. We point out the following identities for the connection coefficients

\displaystyle\omegabh_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\eta_{a,M}-\zeta_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{a,M}=\underline{\xi}_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\widehat{\chi}{}_{a,M}=\underline{\widehat{\chi}}{}_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

the
non
-vanishing of the antitraces of the second fundamental forms
(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)_{a,M}
and
(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi})_{a,M}
and the vanishing of the curvature components

\displaystyle\alpha_{a,M}=\underline{\alpha}_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{a,M}=\underline{\beta}_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,.

The connection coefficients and curvature components of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
solve the nonlinear system of null structure and Bianchi equations of Section
3.1.4
. The formalism developed in Section
3.1
is already needed at this stage in its full generality, in that
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
is a non-integrable frame.

3.2.3

Outgoing principal differentiable structures

In Section
5.6
, we define
outgoing principal differentiable structures
of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
as (local) differentiable structures
(x^{1},x^{2},\vartheta^{A})
on
\mathcal{M}
, with
(\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, such that

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\text{as}}(x^{1})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\text{as}}(\vartheta^{A})

\displaystyle=0\,.

Relative to an outgoing principal differentiable structure, the Kerr metric satisfies the identities

\displaystyle(g_{a,M})_{x^{2}x^{2}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{x^{2}}(g_{a,M})_{x^{2}\mu}+\mathfrak{h}_{a,M}\,(g_{a,M})_{x^{2}\mu}

\displaystyle=0

for some smooth scalar function
\mathfrak{h}_{a,M}
on
\mathcal{M}
, with
x^{\mu}=(x^{1},\vartheta^{A})
.

Then, we introduce the
star-normalised
, outgoing principal differentiable structure

(s,\underline{s},\theta^{A})

(38)

on the spacetime region

\mathcal{M}^{*}\subset\mathcal{M}

as in Figure
, with

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\in[r_{+},r_{+}+1)\,,

\displaystyle\underline{s}

\displaystyle\in[0,\infty)\,,

\displaystyle(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\,.

The coordinates (
38
) are
regular
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}
. The coordinates
(s,\underline{s})
are
global
, whereas the angular coordinates
(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})
are subject to the usual degeneration on two-spheres.

The coordinate
\underline{s}
is such that

\underline{s}=t^{*}

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, with
t^{*}
the Kerr-star time coordinate on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. To construct the other coordinates, we first consider the hypersurface

\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}:=\left\{\underline{s}=0\right\}

and define a coordinate function
s_{0}
on
\mathcal{S}_{0}^{*}
such that
s_{0}=r|_{\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}}
, with
r
the Kerr-star (or, equivalently, Boyer–Lindquist) radius. We define arbitrary angular coordinates
(\theta^{1}_{0},\theta^{2}_{0})
on the two-spheres of constant
s_{0}
foliating
\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}
. We then transport the coordinate
s_{0}
along the integral curves of the vector field
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, i.e. we define the coordinate
s
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
such that

e_{4}^{\text{as}}(s)=0

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and
s|_{\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}}=s_{0}
. We define angular coordinates
(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})
on the two-spheres

\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}:=\left\{s,\underline{s}\right\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}

such that

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\text{as}}(\theta^{1})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\text{as}}(\theta^{2})

\displaystyle=0

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and
(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})|_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,0}}=(\theta^{1}_{0},\theta^{2}_{0})|_{\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,0}}
.

We note that constructing
global
coordinates
(s,\underline{s})
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
requires to carefully check that the vector field
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
is a
complete
vector field on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. See Section
5.6
.

The coordinates
(s,\underline{s})
induce a
global
foliation of
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. The level sets of
areeverywhere\emph{spacelike},horizon-penetratingandasymptoticallyflathypersurfaces.~Wehavetheformal\emph{null}hypersurface\begin{equation*}\mathcal{I}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}:=\left\{\underline{s}=\infty\right\}\,.\end{equation*}Thelevelsetsof
r_+¡s¡ r_+ +1
are\emph{timelike}hypersurfacesintersecting
I
^+
.~Suchhypersurfacesare\emph{asymptoticallynull}astheyapproach
I
^+
.~Thelevelset
\left\{s=r_{+}\right\}=\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}
is\emph{null}.~SeealreadyFigure\ref{fig:intro_foliation}.\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\par\par\begin{figure}\par\centering\par\begin{subfigure}[155.24895pt]\par\centering\par\hbox to108.49pt{\vbox to104.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.0816pt\lower-17.27138pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

3.3

Nonlinear perturbations of the Kerr exterior manifold

In Section
6
, we formulate nonlinear perturbations of the Kerr exterior manifold. The formulation is the core of our new geometric framework.

3.3.1

Family of metrics

In Section
6.1
, we start by choosing real parameters
a
and
M
, with
|a|<M
. We fix the manifold-with-boundary
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, which we identify with the subset of the Kerr ambient manifold of Figure
with star-normalised, outgoing principal differentiable structure
(s,\underline{s},\theta^{A})
. We introduce the fixed smooth scalar functions
\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}
,
k_{a,M}
and
\omegah_{a,M}
and the fixed one-tensor
\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{a,M}
of the fixed coordinates on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, with

\omegah_{a,M}:=\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(\nabla_{e_{4}^{\text{as}}}e_{3}^{\text{as}},e_{4}^{\text{as}})

and
\underline{\eta}_{a,M}^{\circ}\in\Gamma((T\mathcal{M}^{*})^{\star})
such that

\displaystyle\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{a,M}(e_{A}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(\nabla_{e_{4}^{\text{as}}}e_{3}^{\text{as}},e_{A}^{\text{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{a,M}(e_{3}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=0\,.

Once the background structure is fixed, we prescribe a one-parameter family of smooth, non-degenerate Lorentzian metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
as already outlined in Section
1.2.2
. We further assume that

\sum_{0\leq|I|\leq 10}|\partial^{I}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{g}}(\epsilon)-\partial^{I}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{g}}(0)|\leq\epsilon

(39)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, with

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{g}}(\epsilon)=\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{b}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)\right\}

and
I
a multi-index for partial derivatives relative to the fixed differentiable structure.

We remark that there exists a sufficiently small constant
\epsilon_{0}>0
, depending only on the fixed background parameters
a
and
M
, such that, for all
0\leq|\epsilon|\leq\epsilon_{0}
, the form (
9
) of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, together with the additional identities (
10
), correspond to a choice of gauge for the family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, called
outgoing principal gauge
. The assumption (
39
) is relevant for this fact. See Section
3.3.3
.

From now on, without loss of generality,
we restrict to
0\leq|\epsilon|\leq\epsilon_{0}
. When we write
for all
\epsilon
, this restriction is implicitly assumed.

3.3.2

Family of frames

In Section
6.3
, to the one-parameter family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, we associate a one-parameter family of local frames

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)=(\eo(\epsilon),\etw(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon))

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
such that

\displaystyle\ef(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=k_{a,M}\,\partial_{\underline{s}}

\displaystyle\et(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{s}+\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\underline{s}}+\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\theta^{A}}\,.

(40)

For each
\epsilon
, the frame quantities
\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)
are smooth scalar functions on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and the frame quantity
\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}(\epsilon)
is a vector field tangent to the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-spheres. All quantities are regular on the whole
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}
. For each
\epsilon
and any
(s,\theta^{A})
, the frame quantities are
defined
implicitly as solutions to the system of ODEs
34
34

34

We note that the only unknown quantities on the right hand side of the system of ODEs (
41
)-(
43
) are the frame quantities
(\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon),\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon),\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}(\epsilon))
. All the other quantities are either background quantities or known quantities depending on the given metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
. We denote by
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\epsilon)
the Christoffel symbols of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
.

\displaystyle k\,\frac{d\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)}{d\underline{s}}=

\displaystyle\,-2\left(\frac{\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{A}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)}{\Omega^{2}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{B}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)}\right)(\Omega^{2}\boldsymbol{g}^{ss}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{C}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{s\theta^{C}}(\epsilon))

(41)

\displaystyle-(\omegah+k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{s\underline{s}}^{s}(\epsilon))\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\underline{s}\theta^{A}}^{s}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{ss}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{A}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{s\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle k\,\frac{d\,\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)}{d\underline{s}}=

\displaystyle\,-2\left(\frac{\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{A}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)}{\Omega^{2}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{B}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)}\right)(\Omega^{2}\boldsymbol{g}^{s\underline{s}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{C}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\underline{s}\theta^{C}}(\epsilon))

(42)

\displaystyle+(k\,\mathfrak{k}-\omegah)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{s\underline{s}}^{\underline{s}}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\underline{s}\theta^{A}}^{\underline{s}}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{s\underline{s}}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{A}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\underline{s}\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle k\,\frac{d\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)}{d\underline{s}}=

\displaystyle\,-2\left(\frac{\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{B}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)}{\Omega^{2}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{C}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{C}}(\epsilon)}\right)(\Omega^{2}\boldsymbol{g}^{s\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{D}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\theta^{A}\theta^{D}}(\epsilon))

(43)

\displaystyle-\omegah\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\underline{s}\theta^{B}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{s\underline{s}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{s\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{B}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)

in the variable
\underline{s}
, with initial data

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)|_{\underline{s}=0}=\left.-\frac{2\,\underline{j}}{k\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon))}\,\right\rvert_{\underline{s}=0}\,,

(44)

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)|_{\underline{s}=0}=\left.\frac{\underline{j}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{j}\,\underline{k}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{j}\,\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\boldsymbol{b}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{k}\,\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+|\underline{\Lambda}|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\epsilon)}}{k\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon))^{2}}-\frac{2\,\underline{k}}{k\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon))}\,\right\rvert_{\underline{s}=0}\,,

(45)

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)|_{\underline{s}=0}=\left.-\frac{2\,\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}}{k\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon))}\,\right\rvert_{\underline{s}=0}\,.

(46)

A choice of the frame vector fields
(\eo(\epsilon),\etw(\epsilon))
is
not
required. For further comments on the definition of the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
, see Section
3.3.4
.

As a result of their definition, the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
satisfy the properties already listed in Section
1.2.2
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, whose proof is addressed in Section
3.3.4
. In particular, the one-parameter family of horizontal distributions

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}:=\left\langle\et(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon)\right\rangle^{\boldsymbol{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)}}

is globally well-defined on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and such that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)}=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
.

The connection coefficients and curvature components of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are global and regular
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. We have the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omegah}(\epsilon)-\omegah_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(47)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(48)

\displaystyle\underline{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\epsilon)-\underline{\eta}{}_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0

(49)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}

for all
\epsilon
. The geometric quantity
\underline{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\epsilon)
in the identity (
49
) is a global, regular
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-tensor on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
that one can uniquely associate to the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensor
\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\epsilon)
(see Section
3.4
).
35
35

35

Consistently, we will have
\underline{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(0)=\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)
, and thus
\underline{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(0)=\underline{\eta}{}_{a,M}
.
The identity (
49
) is thus an identity for the geometric difference of global, regular
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-tensors on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

Two additional properties of the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are particularly relevant for the problem:

•

Non-integrable horizontal distributions

The frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are non-integrable for all
\epsilon
,
36
36

36

For
|a|=0
, the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are non-integrable for all
|\epsilon|>0
, with
\epsilon=0
excluded. See already Section
1.3.3
.
with
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
non-integrable horizontal distributions. See already Section
1.3.2
.

•

Variable horizontal distributions

The one-parameter family of distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
is a family of
variable distributions
relative to the fixed distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. This, in particular, means that

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}\neq\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}

(50)

for all
|\epsilon|>0
. The property persists for
|a|=0
. For a rigorous definition of variable distributions, see Section
6.3
.

The horizontal distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
are
both
non-integrable
and
variable. We point out that the two properties are independent and could have, in principle, occurred separately.
37
37

37

One could, for instance, associate to the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
a one-parameter family of null frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
such that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
for all
\epsilon
. Such frames would not, however, satisfy all the properties listed in Section
1.2.2
.
The variability of the distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
gives rise to some technical difficulties when one wants to renormalise the perturbed quantities relative to their corresponding Kerr background quantities (see Section
3.4
). We also point out that a choice of frames
adapted
to the fixed differentiable structure would have induced
fixed
,
integrable
horizontal distributions, and thus neither of these two properties would have occurred.

In Section
6.3
, we also define the
frame coefficients
of
\et(\epsilon)
, together with additional frame coefficients which are convenient for the problem. See already Section
1.3.2
.

3.3.3

Outgoing principal gauge

In Section
6.2
, we
prove
that the form (
9
) of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
of Section
3.3.1
, together with the additional identities (
10
), correspond to a choice of gauge for the family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, called
outgoing principal gauge
. See Proposition
6.2
.

We emphasise that the gauge statement is
nonlinear
and requires a closeness assumption to the Kerr exterior manifold, whereas the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
are
not
required to satisfy the vacuum Einstein equations or to possess any special Petrov-type property for
|\epsilon|>0
. We also note that the gauge identities depend on the Kerr parameters.

The closeness assumption in the gauge statement involves an analogue of condition (
39
) (see the inequality (
180
)). The condition is employed in various steps of the proof of the statement. Although a
C^{0}
-version of the condition (i.e.
|I|=0
) would suffice for some of these steps, we do need the assumption on derivatives of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
to establish that certain geometric quantities in the proof are sufficiently regular (see Section
6.2.1
). The number of derivatives required by the condition is not sharp.

3.3.4

Frame construction

In Section
6.4
, we consider the set-up of Section
3.3.1
and prove that the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
introduced in Section
3.3.2
are well-defined and satisfy the properties stated.

We emphasise that the frame construction is
nonlinear
. As for the gauge construction of Section
3.3.3
, the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
are
not
required to satisfy the vacuum Einstein equations or to possess any special Petrov-type property for
|\epsilon|>0
. We also point out that the frame construction exploits
all
the gauge identities for the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
.

3.4

A morphism between horizontal tensor bundles

Let us consider the fixed ambient manifold
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and the one-parameter family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
and associated frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
introduced in Sections
3.3.1
-
3.3.2
. Since the distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
are variable relative to the fixed distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
, the horizontal tensor bundles

\displaystyle\otimes_{k}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}\otimes_{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)})^{\star}\,,

\displaystyle\otimes_{k}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}\otimes_{q}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})^{\star}

(51)

are
distinct
abstract tensor bundles on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for all
|\epsilon|>0
(see already the relation (
50
)). In particular,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

(k,q)
-tensors and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}

(k,q)
-tensors are smooth sections of distinct tensor bundles.

In Section
7
, we prescribe, for each
\epsilon
, a smooth
bundle morphism
38
38

38

We remark that, a priori, there is no canonical bundle morphism between the horizontal tensor bundles (
51
).

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{q}(\epsilon):\otimes_{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)})^{\star}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\otimes_{q}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})^{\star}

\displaystyle(p,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{p}(\epsilon))

\displaystyle\mapsto(p,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}_{p}(\epsilon))\,.

For
\epsilon=0
, the bundle morphism
\mathfrak{B}_{q}(0)
coincides with the
identity map
over
\otimes_{q}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})^{\star}
. For the tensor bundle
\otimes_{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)})^{\star}
equipped with the metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\epsilon)
, the bundle morphism
\mathfrak{B}_{q}(\epsilon)
is an
isometry
for all
\epsilon
(see Section
7.5
). See Figure
5
.

\otimes_{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)})^{\star}
\otimes_{q}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})^{\star}
\mathcal{M}^{*}
\mathfrak{B}_{q}(\epsilon)
\boldsymbol{\pi}(\epsilon)
\pi

:
Commutation diagram of the bundle morphism
\mathfrak{B}_{q}(\epsilon)
. The maps
\boldsymbol{\pi}(\epsilon)
and
\pi
