.

The bundle morphism
\mathfrak{B}_{q}(\epsilon)
induces an isometry between
sections
of tensor bundles, i.e. between
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

q
-tensors and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}

q
-tensors. In loose terms, the morphism
\mathfrak{B}_{1}(\epsilon)
maps any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
to a
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-tensor
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}(\epsilon)
obtained by first extending
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
to a spacetime one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\epsilon)\in\Gamma((T\mathcal{M}^{*})^{\star})
such that
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\ef(\epsilon))=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\et(\epsilon))=0
and then projecting (and restricting)
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\epsilon)
onto
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})^{\star}
. Higher order
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

q
-tensors are mapped accordingly. We point out that, when applied to the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensor
\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\epsilon)
, the morphism
\mathfrak{B}_{1}(\epsilon)
yields the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-tensor
\widetilde{\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\epsilon)
appearing in the identity (
49
).

The geometric formalism of Section
7
is
nonlinear
and does
not
assume that the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
solve the vacuum Einstein equations. In fact, in Section
7
we only treat general
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors, without identifying them with the connection coefficients and curvature components of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
. Section
7
is the most technical part of the paper and contains an additional overview to its content. See Section
7.1
.

3.5

The renormalised vacuum Einstein equations

Let us consider the fixed ambient manifold
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and the one-parameter family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
and associated frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
introduced in Sections
3.3.1
-
3.3.2
. In Section
8
, we assume that the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
solve the vacuum Einstein equations

\boldsymbol{\ric}(\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon))=0

for all
\epsilon
. The nonlinear system of null structure and Bianchi equations for the connection coefficients and curvature components of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
is a nonlinear system of equations for
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors and coincides, for each
\epsilon
, with the system of equations described in Section
3.1.4
.

For later convenience, we
renormalise
the vacuum Einstein equations for the perturbations relative to the Kerr background quantities
in geometric form
. To this end, one needs to take
geometric differences
of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

q
-tensors (
q=1,2
) and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}

q
-tensors. However, since
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors are smooth sections of distinct tensor bundles, such differences cannot be taken directly.

To circumvent this technical issue, we exploit the formalism discussed in Section
3.4
. We first apply the bundle morphism of Section
3.4
to each of the equations for
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

q
-tensors in the system, transforming each of the terms in the equations into a
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}

q
-tensor. The new nonlinear system of null structure and Bianchi equations for
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors obtained is dubbed
reduced
system of vacuum Einstein equations and is presented
in geometric form
in Section
8.1
.

With the reduced system of equations at hand, one can now derive the nonlinear system of equations in renormalised form. One directly takes
geometric differences
between the reduced equations and the corresponding equations for the Kerr background quantities,
all the equations being equations for
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors
. As for the reduced system of equations, the renormalised system of vacuum Einstein equations is a system for
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors, dubbed
renormalised quantities
. The complete derivation of the renormalised system of equations is left to the reader.

3.6

The linearisation procedure

In Section
9
, we perform the linearisation of general
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors (and their derivatives) around
\epsilon=0
. The linearisation of a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

q
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
is the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}

q
-tensor

\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\varsigma}

obtained by linearising the geometric difference

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}(\epsilon)-\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(0)=\epsilon\cdot\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\varsigma}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})

around
\epsilon=0
, with
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}(\epsilon)
the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensor image of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
via the bundle morphism of Section
3.4
. All the linearised quantities are global,
regular

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors on the whole
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}
, and all the linearisation formulae are presented for general geometric quantities and
in geometric form
. Some residual technical difficulties in the linearisation procedure are to be addressed. These are outlined in Section
9.1
, which serves as an overview to the section.

The general linearisation formulae are then applied to linearise the connection coefficients and curvature components of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
. See Section
9.5
.

3.7

The system of linearised gravity

In Section
10
, we present the system of linearised gravity obtained by applying the linearisation formulae of Section
3.6
to linearise, in geometric form, the renormalised vacuum Einstein equations. The final system of linearised equations can be handled without knowledge about its derivation or reference to the nonlinear theory.

The system of linearised gravity is a system of equations for global,
regular

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors on the whole
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}
, and is presented
in geometric form
in Sections
10.1
-
10.3
.

Appendix
B
presents the key structural properties of the system of linearised gravity. In addition to the features described therein, we mention that the system exhibits terms reflecting the variability of the distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
employed in its derivation. This is, for instance, the case of the last two terms in the linearised Codazzi equation

\not{\text{div}}\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\widehat{\chi}}+\ldots=\frac{1}{2}\,\nablasl(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\text{tr}\chi})-\frac{1}{2}\,{}^{\star}\nablasl(\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})-\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\nablasl_{3}(\text{tr}\chi))\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}_{4}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\nablasl_{4}(\text{tr}\chi))\,\overset{\text{\scalebox{.6}{$(1)$}}}{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}_{3}+\ldots\,.

Consistently with the fact that the variability of the distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
persists for
|a|=0
, these terms survive for
|a|=0
.

4

The vacuum Einstein equations

Let
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
be a
3+1
-dimensional, smooth, time-oriented Lorentzian manifold.

In this section and throughout the paper, we adopt the bolded notation of
[
17
]
in the nonlinear setting.

4.1

Preliminary definitions

We give some preliminary definitions.

Definition 4.1

(Null frame)
.

A
null frame

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}=(\eo,\etw,\et,\ef)

on
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
is a frame on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
such that the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\ea,\eb)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\et,\ef)

\displaystyle=-2\,,

(52)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\et,\et)=\boldsymbol{g}(\ef,\ef)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\ea,\et)=\boldsymbol{g}(\ea,\ef)

\displaystyle=0

(53)

hold, with
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}=\left\{\boldsymbol{1},\boldsymbol{2}\right\}
.

Remark 4.2
.

In Definition
4.1
, the frame vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
are assumed to be
orthonormal
.

Remark 4.3
.

A
local
null frame on
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
is a
local
frame on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
such that the identities (
52
)-(
53
) hold.

Definition 4.4

(Horizontal distribution)
.

The (local)
horizontal distribution
associated to a (local) null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is the (local) distribution

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}:=\left\langle\et,\ef\right\rangle^{\boldsymbol{\perp}}

on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
.

Remark 4.5
.

Strictly speaking, the horizontal distribution is associated to the pair of null vector fields
(\et,\ef)
(as opposed to the full frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
, as phrased for future convenience in Definition
4.4
). In fact, null frames that only differ by the choice of orthonormal vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
induce the same horizontal distribution. We also emphasise that the domain of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
on which the horizontal distribution is well-defined coincides with that of the null pair
(\et,\ef)
, and may in principle be larger than the domain on which the null pair
(\et,\ef)
can be completed to a null frame. Indeed, in Section
5
we will deal with a null pair which is
globally
well-defined on the manifold considered, but can only
locally
be completed to a null frame. In such a scenario, the associated horizontal distribution will be
global
.

Remark 4.6
.

The horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
can be regarded as an abstract tensor bundle on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
or, alternatively, as a sub-bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\subset T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
via the inclusion map

\iota:\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\hookrightarrow T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}\,.

Such a distinction will be exploited in Definitions
4.12
and
4.17
.

Crucially, the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is allowed to be a
non-integrable
distribution.
39
39

39

We recall that an
n
-dimensional
integrable
distribution is a distribution which admits a (local) basis
\left\{\boldsymbol{X_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{X_{n}}\right\}
spanning all the Lie brackets
[\boldsymbol{X_{i}},\boldsymbol{X_{j}}]
, with
1\leq\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}\leq n
. A distribution is
non-integrable
if it fails to be integrable.

Definition 4.7

(Non-integrable null frame)
.

A
non-integrable
null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is a null frame whose associated horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is a non-integrable distribution.

Remark 4.8
.

The vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
form a local orthonormal basis of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\subset T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. By Definition
4.7
, the null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is a non-integrable frame if and only if, for any choice of
(\eo,\etw)
, at least one of the relations

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}([\eo,\etw],\ef)

\displaystyle\neq 0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}([\eo,\etw],\et)

\displaystyle\neq 0

holds.

We now define the main geometric objects of the paper, namely
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors
. In the following definition, the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is regarded as an abstract tensor bundle on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
.

Definition 4.9

(
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors)
.

A
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(k,q)
-tensor field

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
, with
k,q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, is a smooth section

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\in\Gamma(\otimes_{k}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\otimes_{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})^{\boldsymbol{\star}})\,.

In particular, a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

vector field

\boldsymbol{X}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
is a smooth section

\boldsymbol{X}\in\Gamma(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})\,.

Remark 4.10
.

The domain of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
on which a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensor is well-defined coincides with that of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
, and may in principle be larger than the domain on which the null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
can be defined. See the related Remark
4.5
.

Remark 4.11
.

We often refer to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(0,q)
-tensor fields as
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

q
-tensors
.

Let
\iota_{k}
be the
inclusion map

\iota_{k}:\otimes_{k}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\hookrightarrow\otimes_{k}T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}\,,

with
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote
\iota_{k=1}
by
\iota
.

For later convenience, we now define how
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors can be canonically extended to spacetime tensors. We start with
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields.

Definition 4.12

(Canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}
be a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. The
canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{X}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
is the smooth section

\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ}\in\Gamma(T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})

such that

(p,\boldsymbol{X}_{p}^{\circ})=\iota(p,\boldsymbol{X}_{p})

for all
(p,\boldsymbol{X}_{p}^{\circ})\in T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
.

Remark 4.13
.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ}
be the canonical extension of a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field
\boldsymbol{X}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. Then, by Definitions
4.4
and
4.12
, the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\ef)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\et)

\displaystyle=0

hold.

Remark 4.14
.

With a slight abuse of notation, the frame vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
will be treated both as
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
and as their canonical extensions.

We now define the canonical extension of higher-rank
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(k,0)
-tensors. For
k=1
, the following definition and remark reduce to Definition
4.12
and Remark
4.13
respectively.

Definition 4.15

(Canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(k,0)
-tensors)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
be a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(k,0)
-tensor on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. The
canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
is the smooth section

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}\in\Gamma(\otimes_{k}T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})

such that

(p,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}_{p})=\iota_{k}(p,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{p})

for all
(p,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}_{p})\in\otimes_{k}T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
.

Remark 4.16
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}
be the canonical extension of a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(k,0)
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. Then, by Definitions
4.4
and
4.15
, the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\ldots,\boldsymbol{g}(\ef,\cdot),\ldots)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\ldots,\boldsymbol{g}(\et,\cdot),\ldots)

\displaystyle=0

hold.

We now define the canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(0,q)
-tensors.

Definition 4.17

(Canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(0,q)
-tensors)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
be a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(0,q)
-tensor on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. The
canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
is the smooth section

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}\in\Gamma(\otimes_{q}(T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})^{\star})

such that, for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{X_{q}}
, the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{1}}^{\circ},\ldots,\boldsymbol{X}_{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\circ})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\boldsymbol{X_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{X_{q}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\ldots,\ef,\ldots)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\ldots,\et,\ldots)=0

hold.

We conclude the section with a series of remarks.

Remark 4.18
.

The canonical extension of a full-rank
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensor is
not
a full-rank tensor.

Remark 4.19
.

The canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(0,q)
-tensors, as defined in Definition
4.17
, differs from the extension induced by the inclusion
\otimes_{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})^{\star}\hookrightarrow\otimes_{q}(T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})^{\star}
.
40
40

40

The convenience of our Definition
4.17
of canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(0,q)
-tensors will become apparent. See already Remark
4.25
for an example.
We give a concrete example to elucidate this fact. We consider the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
in local double-null form
\boldsymbol{g}=-4\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}d\boldsymbol{u}\,d\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{\not{\gamma}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta^{A}}\boldsymbol{\theta^{B}}}(d\boldsymbol{\theta^{A}}-\boldsymbol{b^{\theta^{A}}}d\boldsymbol{v})(d\boldsymbol{\theta^{B}}-\boldsymbol{b^{\theta^{B}}}d\boldsymbol{v})
(see
[
18
]
) and the local null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}=(\ef,\et,\ea)=(\partial_{\boldsymbol{v}}+\boldsymbol{b^{\theta^{A}}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\theta^{A}}},\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{-2}\partial_{\boldsymbol{u}},\ea)
. Locally, one has
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\equiv T\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}}
, with
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\right\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. We consider the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}=d\boldsymbol{\theta^{A}}\,.

It is easy to check that the canonical extension of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, as defined in Definition
4.17
, reads

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}=d\boldsymbol{\theta^{A}}-\boldsymbol{b^{\theta^{A}}}d\boldsymbol{v}\,.

The extension of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
induced by the inclusion
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})^{\star}\hookrightarrow(T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})^{\star}
is
d\boldsymbol{\theta^{A}}\in\Gamma((T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})^{\star})
, which manifestly differs from
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}
. In particular, we have
d\boldsymbol{\theta^{A}}(\ef)=\boldsymbol{b^{\theta^{A}}}\neq 0
.

Remark 4.20
.

In Section
7
, any
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\in\Gamma(\otimes_{q}(T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})^{\star})
such that
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\ldots,\ef,\ldots)=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\ldots,\et,\ldots)=0
will be referred to as a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
-horizontal
(0,q)
-tensor
. Similarly, any
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\in\Gamma(\otimes_{k}T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})
such that
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\ldots,\boldsymbol{g}(\ef,\cdot),\ldots)=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\ldots,\boldsymbol{g}(\et,\cdot),\ldots)=0
will be referred to as a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
-horizontal
(k,0)
-tensor
. Using this terminology, the canonical extension of a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(0,q)
-tensor is a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
-horizontal
(0,q)
-tensor. Similarly, the canonical extension of a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(k,0)
-tensor is a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
-horizontal
(k,0)
-tensor. Such a terminology is not adopted anywhere else in the paper.

Remark 4.21
.

All the definitions in this section are manifestly independent of the choice of frame vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
.

4.2

Induced metric, connection coefficients and curvature components

Let
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
be the Levi-Civita connection associated to
\boldsymbol{g}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
,
\boldsymbol{R}
the Riemann curvature tensor on
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
the standard volume form associated to
\boldsymbol{g}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
be a local null frame on
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
, according to Definition
4.1
. All the definitions in this section are independent of the choice of frame vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
. The time-orientation of
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
is such that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\eo,\etw,\et,\ef)=2
.

We introduce the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
covariant tensor fields

\boldsymbol{\not{g}}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}

such that
41
41

41

Recall the abuse of notation noted in Remark
4.14
, which appears here and throughout the section. To be consistent with the notation of Definition
4.12
, one would consider
(\eo,\etw)
as
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields and, for instance, write
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\ea,\eb)=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\circ})
.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\ea,\eb)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\ea,\eb)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}(\ea,\eb)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\ea,\eb,\et,\ef)\,.

Informally, we refer to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
as the
induced metric
on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
and to
\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}
as the
induced volume form
on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
.

Remark 4.22
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field
\boldsymbol{X}
, we have the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor

\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{X},\cdot)

and the identity

(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{X},\cdot))^{\circ}=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\cdot)\,.

Remark 4.23
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}
, we have the identity

\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ})\,.

We define the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
contravariant tensor

\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}

as the inverses of the metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
. One may view
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}
as an induced metric on
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})^{\star}
. We will adopt the notation

(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\boldsymbol{AB}}=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\ea,\cdot),\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\eb,\cdot))\,,

where the left hand side will be often written in the more synthetic form
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}
. We note that the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensors
(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\eo,\cdot),\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\etw,\cdot))
are the dual co-frame to
(\eo,\etw)
and form a local
orthonormal
co-frame of
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})^{\star}
.

Remark 4.24
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, we have the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field

\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\cdot)\,.

We will write the expression

\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\cdot)=(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\ea)\,\eb\,.

(54)

We have the identity

(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\cdot))^{\circ}=\boldsymbol{g}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ},\cdot)\,.

Remark 4.25
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensors
\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\theta}
, we have the identity

\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\boldsymbol{g}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\,.

We define the
connection coefficients
relative to the null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
as the smooth scalar functions

\boldsymbol{\omegah}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\omegabh}

and the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
covariant tensor fields

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\etab}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\yb}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\chib}

such that, with the
frame-index notation

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{k}}})=\boldsymbol{\varsigma_{A_{1}\cdots A_{k}}}
, one has

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{A}e_{4}},\boldsymbol{e_{B}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chib}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{A}e_{3}},\boldsymbol{e_{B}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{3}e_{4}},\boldsymbol{e_{A}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\etab}_{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{4}e_{3}},\boldsymbol{e_{A}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}_{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{4}e_{4}},\boldsymbol{e_{A}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\yb}_{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{3}e_{3}},\boldsymbol{e_{A}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omegah}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{4}e_{3}},\boldsymbol{e_{4}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omegabh}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{3}e_{4}},\boldsymbol{e_{3}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{A}e_{4}},\boldsymbol{e_{3}})\,.

We define the
curvature components
relative to the null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
as the smooth scalar functions

\boldsymbol{\rho}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\,,

and the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
covariant tensor fields

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\betab}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\alphab}\,,

such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{R}(\ea,\ef,\eb,\ef)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alphab_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{R}(\ea,\et,\eb,\et)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta_{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{R}(\ea,\ef,\et,\ef)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\betab_{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{R}(\ea,\et,\et,\ef)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\,\boldsymbol{R}(\ef,\et,\ef,\et)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\,\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}R}(\ef,\et,\ef,\et)\,,

where
\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}R}
is the Hodge dual of
\boldsymbol{R}
on
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
. In view of the symmetries of
\boldsymbol{R}
, both
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\alphab}
are
symmetric

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors.

4.2.1

Decomposition of the second fundamental forms

The following proposition relates the symmetries of the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors
\boldsymbol{\chi}
and
\boldsymbol{\chib}
and the integrability of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
. The proof is an easy check left to the reader.

Proposition 4.26
.

The distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable if and only if there exists a choice of
(\eo,\etw)
such that the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\chi_{BA}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chib_{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\chib_{BA}}

hold. In particular,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}([\ea,\eb],\ef)=0\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{\chi_{AB}}=\boldsymbol{\chi_{BA}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}([\ea,\eb],\et)=0\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{\chib_{AB}}=\boldsymbol{\chib_{BA}}\,.

Since the distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is allowed to be non-integrable, the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors
\boldsymbol{\chi}
and
\boldsymbol{\chib}
do
not
possess, in general, any symmetry.

We define the
symmetric

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors

\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\tiny{\Circle}}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\chib_{\tiny{\Circle}}}\,,

the
antisymmetric

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors

\boldsymbol{\chi_{\tiny{\Square}}}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\chib_{\tiny{\Square}}}

and the
symmetric traceless

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors

\boldsymbol{\chih}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\chibh}

such that

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\tiny{\Circle}}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,({\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}+{\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{\boldsymbol{BA}})\,,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\chib_{\tiny{\Circle}}}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,({\boldsymbol{\chib}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}+{\boldsymbol{\chib}}_{\boldsymbol{BA}})\,,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\tiny{\Square}}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,({\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}-{\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{\boldsymbol{BA}})\,,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\chib_{\tiny{\Square}}}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,({\boldsymbol{\chib}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}-{\boldsymbol{\chib}}_{\boldsymbol{BA}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chih}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\tiny{\Circle}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chibh}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\chib_{\tiny{\Circle}}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}\,,

where the traces are taken relative to the inverse metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}
, i.e.

\displaystyle(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chi})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\boldsymbol{AB}}{\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,,

\displaystyle(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{\chib}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,.

We define the
antitraces
of
\boldsymbol{\chi}
and
\boldsymbol{\chib}
as the smooth scalar functions
42
42

42

As we shall clarify in the next section, all the frame indices of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors are raised relative to the inverse metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}
, e.g.
\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}=(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\boldsymbol{AC}}(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\boldsymbol{BD}}\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{CD}}
.

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}^{AB}\chi{}_{AB}}\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}^{AB}\chib{}_{AB}}

respectively.

Remark 4.27
.

The antitraces
(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})
and
(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})
encode the (non-)integrability of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
, meaning that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable if and only if both the antitraces identically vanish (see later formula (
72
)).

We decompose the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors
\boldsymbol{\chi}
and
\boldsymbol{\chib}
as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\chih}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chib}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\chibh}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}\,.

4.3

Tensor contractions and products

In this section we define contractions and products of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors. As a general caveat, we note that the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors involved in the expressions are not assumed to possess any symmetry, and thus the order of the frame indices in the formulae is relevant.

Given a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
and a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
two-tensor
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, we introduce the notation

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{1}}}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}

such that

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}}{}^{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}^{AB}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma_{B}}\,,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{1}}}}{}^{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{BC}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{CA}}\,,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}}{}^{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{BC}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{AC}}\,.

Remark 4.28
.

The
\boldsymbol{\sharp}_{\boldsymbol{I}}
-notation allows to keep track of the position of the frame indices when we write tensorial expressions involving tensors which do not possess any symmetry. If
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is symmetric, then
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{1}}}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}
and we simply write
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}
.

We define the full contraction

\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{\theta_{AB}}

and the duality relations

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\varsigma}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{{\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}}{}^{B}_{A}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma_{B}}\,,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\theta}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{{\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}}{}^{C}_{A}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta_{BC}}\,.

By definition, we have
{\boldsymbol{{{}^{\star}}{{}^{\star}}\varsigma}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}=-\boldsymbol{\varsigma_{A}}
.

Given the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensors
\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\varsigma}}
and the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
two-tensors
\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}
, we define

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\varsigma}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}^{AB}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma_{A}}\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{\varsigma}_{B}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\boldsymbol{\wedge}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\varsigma}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}^{AB}\varsigma_{A}\,\tilde{\varsigma}_{B}}

and

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}^{AD}}\boldsymbol{\not{g}^{CB}\theta_{AC}\,\tilde{\theta}_{BD}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\wedge}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}^{AD}\not{g}^{CB}\theta_{AC}\,\tilde{\theta}_{BD}}\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\times}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\theta}})_{\boldsymbol{AB}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{{\theta^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{C}\,\tilde{\theta}_{CB}}\,.

We also have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{\varsigma}}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\otimes\boldsymbol{\tilde{\varsigma}})+(\boldsymbol{\tilde{\varsigma}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\varsigma})-(\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\varsigma}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}\,.

The
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
two-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{\varsigma}}
is symmetric and traceless relative to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}^{-1}}
.

4.4

Differential operators

This section deals with differential operators acting on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors. As a general caveat, we note that the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors involved in the expressions are not assumed to possess any symmetry, and thus the order of the frame indices in the formulae is relevant.

4.4.1

Covariant derivative

The Levi-Civita connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
of
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
induces a natural connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
. In this section, we shall define the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
and discuss its two main properties, namely its compatibility with the (already defined) induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
and its torsion. The latter property is novel and is tied to the non-integrability of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
.

We start with two definitions.

Definition 4.29
.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}\in\Gamma(T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})
. We define the map

\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}

between
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors such that

•

For any smooth scalar function
\boldsymbol{f}
, we have the smooth scalar function
\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}f}
such that

\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}f}=\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{f})\,;

•

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field
\boldsymbol{Y}
, we have the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field
\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}Y}
such that
43
43

43

Recall Definition
4.12
and the abuse of notation noted in Remark
4.14
. To be fully consistent with the notation, one would consider
(\eo,\etw)
as
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields and write
\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}Y}=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X}Y^{\circ}},\boldsymbol{e}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{1}})\,\boldsymbol{e_{1}}+\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X}Y^{\circ}},\boldsymbol{e}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{2}})\,\boldsymbol{e_{2}}
. Note also that one can write
\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}Y}=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\cdot)
, with
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor such that
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\ea)=\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X}Y^{\circ}},\boldsymbol{e}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{A}})
. This coincides with the definition (
55
) via the formula (
54
).

\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}Y}=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X}Y^{\circ}},\boldsymbol{e_{A}})\,\boldsymbol{e_{B}}\,;

(55)

•

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y_{k}}
, we have the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}\varsigma}
such that

(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}\varsigma})(\boldsymbol{Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y_{k}})=\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\boldsymbol{Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y_{k}}))-\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y_{k}})-\cdots-\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\boldsymbol{Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}Y_{k}})\,.

Definition 4.30
.

We define the map

\boldsymbol{\nablasl}

between
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors such that

•

For any smooth scalar function
\boldsymbol{f}
, we have the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\nablasl f}
such that

(\boldsymbol{\nablasl f})(\boldsymbol{X})=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}f}

for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field
\boldsymbol{X}
;

•

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field
\boldsymbol{Y}
, we have the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\nablasl Y}
such that

(\boldsymbol{\nablasl Y})(\boldsymbol{X})=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}Y}

for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field
\boldsymbol{X}
;

•

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, we have the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

(k+1)
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\nablasl\varsigma}
such that

(\boldsymbol{\nablasl\varsigma})(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y_{k}})=(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}\varsigma})(\boldsymbol{Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y_{k}})

for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y_{k}}
.

Remark 4.31
.

It is easy to check that Definition
4.29
defines a
covariant derivative
acting on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors (indeed, the map
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
is linear and satisfies the Leibniz rule). The map
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
is a linear
connection
over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors. We refer to
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
as the
induced covariant derivative
over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors and to
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
as the
induced connection
over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors.

Let
[\cdot,\cdot]
denote the standard Lie brackets of
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
, i.e.

[\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}](\boldsymbol{f})=\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{Y}(\boldsymbol{f}))-\boldsymbol{Y}(\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{f}))

for any
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\in\Gamma(T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in C^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})
. The two following propositions address two key properties of the covariant derivative
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
, namely its
torsion
and
compatibility
with the metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
. In particular, Proposition
4.32
captures a new feature of
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
arising when non-integrable structures are allowed. See also Remark
4.34
.

Proposition 4.32
.

The horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable if and only if, for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}
, we have

(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{Y})^{\circ}-(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{X})^{\circ}=[\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}]\,.

(56)

Proof.

We start by noting the identity

[\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}]=\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X^{\circ}}Y^{\circ}}-\boldsymbol{\nabla_{Y^{\circ}}X^{\circ}}

(57)

for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}
, with
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
the Levi-Civita (and thus torsion-free) connection of
\boldsymbol{g}
.

If
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable, then, for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}([\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}],\ef)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}([\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}],\et)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(58)

where the equalities hold by the definition of integrable distribution. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X^{\circ}}Y^{\circ}}-\boldsymbol{\nabla_{Y^{\circ}}X^{\circ}}

\displaystyle=[\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}]

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{g}([\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}],\ea)\eb

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X^{\circ}}Y^{\circ}}-\boldsymbol{\nabla_{Y^{\circ}}X^{\circ}},\ea)\eb

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{Y})^{\circ}-(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{X})^{\circ}\,,

where the first and third equalities use (
57
), the second uses (
58
) and the last equality holds by definition of
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{Y}
. This proves the identity (
56
).

To now prove the other direction of Proposition
4.32
, we assume that, for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}
, the identity (
56
) holds. By Definition
4.29
, the quantity
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{X}
is a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field. Thus, the left hand side of (
56
) is the canonical extension of a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field, implying

[\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}]\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\,.

By definition of integrable distribution, one concludes that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.

∎

Proposition 4.33
.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}\in\Gamma(T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})
. We have the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}}\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}}\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=0\,.

Proof.

We prove the first identity, the proof of the second identity is analogous. We compute

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}}\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}})(\ea,\eb)

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}}\ea,\eb)-\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\ea,\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}}\eb)

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{CD}}\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X}}\ea,\boldsymbol{e_{C}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{e_{D}},\eb)-\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{CD}}\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X}}\eb,\boldsymbol{e_{C}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{e_{D}},\ea)

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X}}\ea,\boldsymbol{e_{B}})-\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{X}}\eb,\boldsymbol{e_{A}})

\displaystyle=0\,,

where the first equality holds by Definition
4.29
and the equality
\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\ea,\eb))=\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{g}(\ea,\eb))=0
, the second equality by Definition
4.29
and the last equality by the
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
being the Levi-Civita (and thus metric compatible) connection of
\boldsymbol{g}
.

∎

Remark 4.34
.

In view of Proposition
4.33
, the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors is
compatible
with the induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
. However, Proposition
4.32
implies that the induced connection is
torsion-free
(and thus
Levi-Civita
relative to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
) if and only if the distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is
integrable
. Thus, in general,
the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
is
not
the Levi-Civita connection of the induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
.

The following proposition states that, in fact, the failure of
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
to be Levi-Civita relative to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
noted in Remark
4.34
is a manifestation of a more general property: If the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is
non-integrable
, then there exists
no
Levi-Civita connection associated to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors.

Proposition 4.35
.

The horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable if and only if there exists a Levi-Civita connection associated to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors.

Proof.

Assume that the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable. Then, we consider the connection over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors from Definition
4.30
. By Propositions
4.32
and
4.33
, such a connection is Levi-Civita relative to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
.

To prove the other direction of the proposition, suppose
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\text{\tiny{LC}}}
is a Levi-Civita connection associated to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}
over the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors. Then, since
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\text{\tiny{LC}}}
is torsion-free, we have the identity

(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\text{\tiny{LC}}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{Y})^{\circ}-(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\text{\tiny{LC}}}_{\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{X})^{\circ}=[\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}]

(59)

for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}
. Since
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\text{\tiny{LC}}}
is a connection over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
tensors, the quantity
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\text{\tiny{LC}}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\text{\tiny{LC}}}_{\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{X}
is a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field. Thus, the left hand side of (
59
) is the canonical extension of a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field, implying

[\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}]\in\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\,.

By definition of integrable distribution, one concludes that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.

∎

In view of Proposition
4.33
, we can define the
antisymmetric

2\times 2
matrices

\boldsymbol{\not{M}{}_{A}^{B}}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\not{\underline{M}}{}_{A}^{B}}\,,\,{}_{\boldsymbol{1}}\boldsymbol{\not{H}{}_{A}^{B}}\,,\,{}_{\boldsymbol{2}}\boldsymbol{\not{H}{}_{A}^{B}}

such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}\ea

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{M}{}_{A}^{B}}\eb\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}}\ea

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{\underline{M}}{}_{A}^{B}}\eb\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{1}}\ea

\displaystyle={}_{\boldsymbol{1}}\boldsymbol{\not{H}{}_{A}^{B}}\boldsymbol{e_{B}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{2}}\ea

\displaystyle={}_{\boldsymbol{2}}\boldsymbol{\not{H}{}_{A}^{B}}\boldsymbol{e_{B}}\,.

We shall now define a set of differential operators employing the covariant derivative
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
. For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
two-tensor
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, we define the divergence operator
44
44

44

The reader familiar with
[
17
]
should note that our definition of
\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta}
contracts the frame indices in a different order.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}^{AB}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma})_{\boldsymbol{B}}\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\boldsymbol{A}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}^{CB}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{C}}\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,,

the curl operator

\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{curl}}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}=\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}^{AB}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{A}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma})_{\boldsymbol{B}}

and the differential operator

\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\,\varsigma}=-\frac{1}{2}((\boldsymbol{\nablasl\varsigma})+(\boldsymbol{\nablasl\varsigma})^{\mathsf{T}}-(\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}})\,.

The
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
two-tensor
\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\,\varsigma}
is symmetric and traceless relative to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}^{-1}}
.

For any smooth scalar functions
\boldsymbol{f}
and
\boldsymbol{h}
, we define the differential operator

\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{\star}}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{h})=-\boldsymbol{\nablasl}\boldsymbol{f}+{}^{\star}\boldsymbol{\nablasl}\boldsymbol{h}\,.

4.4.2

Lie derivative

Let
\boldsymbol{X}\in\Gamma(T\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}})
. We define the differential operator

\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}}

such that, for any smooth scalar function
\boldsymbol{f}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, we have the smooth scalar function
\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{X}}\boldsymbol{f}
and the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{X}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{X}}\boldsymbol{f}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}f}\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{X}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma})(\boldsymbol{Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y_{k}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})(\boldsymbol{Y^{\circ}_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y^{\circ}_{k}})

(60)

for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{Y_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Y_{k}}
, with
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}
the spacetime Lie derivative of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}
with respect to
\boldsymbol{X}
.

The following proposition is useful to relate projected Lie derivatives to projected covariant derivatives.

Proposition 4.36
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
two-tensor
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, we have the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{4}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\varsigma}+\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\varsigma}\,,

(61)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{3}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\varsigma}+\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\varsigma}\,,

(62)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{4}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\theta}+\boldsymbol{\chi_{\tiny{\Circle}}\times\theta}+\boldsymbol{\theta\times\chi_{\tiny{\Circle}}}+\boldsymbol{\chi_{\tiny{\Square}}\times\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta\times\chi_{\tiny{\Square}}}\,,

(63)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{3}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\theta}+\boldsymbol{\chib_{\tiny{\Circle}}\times\,\theta}+\boldsymbol{\theta\times\chib_{\tiny{\Circle}}}+\boldsymbol{\chib_{\tiny{\Square}}\times\,\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta\times\chib_{\tiny{\Square}}}\,.

(64)

Proof.

We prove the formula (
61
), the proof of the formulae (
62
), (
63
) and (
64
) is analogous. We compute

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{4}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma})(\ea)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}_{4}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})(\ea)

\displaystyle=\ef(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\ea))-\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}([\ef,\ea])

\displaystyle=\ef(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\ea))-\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}((\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}\ea)^{\circ}-\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}}{}^{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\eb)

\displaystyle=\ef(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\ea))-\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}\ea)+\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}}{}^{\boldsymbol{B}}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{\boldsymbol{B}}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\varsigma})(\ea)+(\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\varsigma})(\ea)\,,

where the first equality holds by the definition of
\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{4}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
.

∎

4.5

Curvature of horizontal distributions

In this section, we introduce the notion of
curvature
of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
. As we shall see, when
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is allowed to be non-integrable, some of the usual curvature quantities are no longer tensorial and lose their symmetries. These new features may be seen as yet another manifestation of the torsion of the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
.

Definition 4.37
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}
, we define the differential operator

\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}}

such that, for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field
\boldsymbol{Z}
, we have the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
and the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector field
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{Z}
such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{Y}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma})-\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}}\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{Z}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{Y}}\boldsymbol{Z})-\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}}\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{Z}\,.

For any point
p\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
, we consider the map

\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}:(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})_{p}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})_{p}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})_{p}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}})_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

such that

\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{Z}-\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{W})

(65)

for any local
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{W}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}
.

Definition 4.38
.

We define the
Riemann curvature
of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
as the (local) smooth map
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
such that

\boldsymbol{\not{R}}:p\mapsto\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}

(66)

for any (local)
p\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}
.

We have the following proposition, which captures the failure of the Riemann curvature of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
to be tensorial when
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is non-integrable.

Proposition 4.39
.

The Riemann curvature
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is a (local)
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
four-tensor if and only if
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.

Proof.

For any smooth scalar function
\boldsymbol{f}
, we compute

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{f}\,\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})=

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}}(\boldsymbol{f}\,\boldsymbol{Z})-\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{X}}(\boldsymbol{f}\,\boldsymbol{Z}),\boldsymbol{W})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{f}\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}}\boldsymbol{Z}-\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{W})

\displaystyle+(\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{Y}(\boldsymbol{f}))-\boldsymbol{Y}(\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{f}))-(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}}\boldsymbol{Y})(\boldsymbol{f})+(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{Y}}\boldsymbol{X})(\boldsymbol{f}))\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{W})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{f}\,\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})+([\boldsymbol{X}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{Y}^{\circ}](\boldsymbol{f})-(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{X}}\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{Y}}\boldsymbol{X})(\boldsymbol{f}))\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{W})\,,

(67)

where in the second identity we used Definition
4.37
. By applying Proposition
4.32
, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}
is a linear map (i.e. the second term in (
67
) identically vanishes) if and only if
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.

∎

Remark 4.40
.

In view of Proposition
4.39
, the Riemann curvature
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
as defined by the map (
66
) is
not
, in general, a tensorial quantity, the failure deriving from the torsion associated to the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
(see Proposition
4.32
and its application in the proof of Proposition
4.39
). When
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable, the map (
66
) coincides with the Riemann curvature tensor of the (local) integral manifold of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
.

The two following propositions collect the symmetries of the Riemann curvature
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
. The proof is easy and is left to the reader. We note that the symmetries of
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
collected in Proposition
4.42
are conditional to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
being integrable, in that they do not hold when the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}
has torsion.

Proposition 4.41
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{W}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}
, we have the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{X})\,.

Proposition 4.42
.

We have both the identities

\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})=\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{Z})

and

\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})+\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{X})+\boldsymbol{\not{R}}_{p}(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z})=0

for any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
vector fields
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{W}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.

Remark 4.43
.

It follows immediately from Propositions
4.41
and
4.42
that

\boldsymbol{\not{R}}\propto\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}

if and only if
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.

We now introduce the
Gauss curvature
of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
. The definition is adapted to the fact that the curvature of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is, in general, not tensorial.

Definition 4.44
.

We define the
Gauss curvature
of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
as the smooth scalar function

\boldsymbol{\not{K}}=\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{\not{R}}(\ea,\eb,\ea,\eb)\,.

(68)

Remark 4.45
.

When
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable, the scalar function
\boldsymbol{\not{K}}
coincides with the Gauss curvature of the (local) integral manifold of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
.

We have the following proposition. The proof is easy and left to the reader. See related Remark
4.43
.

Proposition 4.46
.

We have the identity

\boldsymbol{\not{R}}=\boldsymbol{\not{K}}\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}

if and only if
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.

We define the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
four-
tensor
45
45

45

The fact that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{R}}}
is a tensor can be easily checked. Crucially, this holds unconditionally, i.e. without the need to assume that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{R}}}
such that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{R}}}(\boldsymbol{e_{A}},\boldsymbol{e_{D}},\boldsymbol{e_{C}},\boldsymbol{e_{B}})=

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{\not{R}}(\boldsymbol{e_{A}},\boldsymbol{e_{D}},\boldsymbol{e_{C}},\boldsymbol{e_{B}})

(69)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}e_{D}},\boldsymbol{e_{A}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}(\boldsymbol{e_{B}},\boldsymbol{e_{C}})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}e_{D}},\boldsymbol{e_{A}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}(\boldsymbol{e_{B}},\boldsymbol{e_{C}})\,.

One can check that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{R}}}
satisfies the symmetries of Proposition
4.42
unconditionally, i.e. without the need to assume that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable.

Remark 4.47
.

The quantity
\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
is intrinsic to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
, whereas he quantity
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{R}}}
contains two terms that are extrinsic with respect to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
. For this reason, it is more natural to consider the former quantity as the Riemann curvature of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
.

The tensor
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{R}}}
appears in the formal derivation of the Gauss equation (
103
) (see equation (
122
) in Section
4.8
). In that regard, the following definition will be convenient.

Definition 4.48
.

We define the
corrected Gauss curvature
of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
as the smooth scalar function

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{K}}}=\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{AC}}\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{BD}}\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{R}}}(\ea,\eb,\boldsymbol{e_{C}},\boldsymbol{e_{D}})\,.

(70)

We have the identity

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{K}}}=\boldsymbol{\not{K}}+\frac{1}{4}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{BD}}\,\boldsymbol{\not{\underline{M}}}{}_{\boldsymbol{D}}^{\boldsymbol{C}}\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{BC}}+\frac{1}{4}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{BD}}\,\boldsymbol{\not{M}}{}_{\boldsymbol{D}}^{\boldsymbol{C}}\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{BC}}\,.

(71)

Remark 4.49
.

When
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
is integrable, we have
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{R}}}\equiv\boldsymbol{\not{R}}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{K}}}\equiv\boldsymbol{\not{K}}
.

4.6

Some useful formulae

In this section, we collect some useful formulae.

4.6.1

Covariant derivative

For the covariant derivative of the frame vector fields, we have the following decomposition formulae

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{A}e_{3}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{{\chib^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}}{}_{A}^{B}\,e_{B}}+\boldsymbol{\zeta_{A}\,e_{3}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{A}e_{4}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{{\chi^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}}{}_{A}^{B}\,e_{B}}-\boldsymbol{\zeta_{A}\,e_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{3}e_{3}}

\displaystyle=2\,\boldsymbol{\yb^{A}e_{A}}+\boldsymbol{\omegabh e_{3}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{3}e_{4}}

\displaystyle=2\,\boldsymbol{\eta^{A}e_{A}}-\boldsymbol{\omegabh e_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{4}e_{3}}

\displaystyle=2\,\boldsymbol{\etab^{A}e_{A}}-\boldsymbol{\omegah e_{3}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{4}e_{4}}

\displaystyle=2\,\boldsymbol{\xi^{A}e_{A}}+\boldsymbol{\omegah e_{4}}\,,

and
46
46

46

We note the slight abuse of notation in the formulae for the covariant derivatives
\boldsymbol{\nabla_{I}\ea}
. To be fully consistent with our notation, one would, for instance, write
\boldsymbol{\nabla_{4}e_{A}}=(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}e_{A}})^{\circ}+\boldsymbol{\xi_{A}\,e_{3}}+\boldsymbol{\etab_{A}e_{4}}
.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{A}e_{B}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{A}e_{B}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\chi}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{e_{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\chib}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{e_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{3}e_{A}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}e_{A}}+\boldsymbol{\eta_{A}\,e_{3}}+\boldsymbol{\yb_{A}e_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla_{4}e_{A}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}e_{A}}+\boldsymbol{\xi_{A}\,e_{3}}+\boldsymbol{\etab_{A}e_{4}}\,.

4.6.2

Frame commutators

The commutators of the frame vector fields read

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{e_{A}},\boldsymbol{e_{B}}]

\displaystyle=\left({}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\boldsymbol{\not{H}{}_{B}^{C}}-{}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{\not{H}{}_{A}^{C}}\right)\boldsymbol{e_{C}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,\boldsymbol{e_{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,\boldsymbol{e_{4}}\,,

(72)

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{e_{3}},\boldsymbol{e_{A}}]

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{\not{\underline{M}}{}_{A}^{B}}-\boldsymbol{{\chib^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}}{}_{A}^{B}}\right)\boldsymbol{e_{B}}+\left(\boldsymbol{\eta_{A}}-\boldsymbol{\zeta_{A}}\right)\boldsymbol{e_{3}}+\boldsymbol{\yb_{A}e_{4}}\,,

(73)

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{e_{4}},\boldsymbol{e_{A}}]

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{\not{M}{}_{A}^{B}}-\boldsymbol{{\chi^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}}{}_{A}^{B}}\right)\boldsymbol{e_{B}}+\boldsymbol{\xi_{A}\,e_{3}}+\left(\boldsymbol{\etab_{A}}+\boldsymbol{\zeta_{A}}\right)\boldsymbol{e_{4}}\,,

(74)

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{e_{3}},\boldsymbol{e_{4}}]

\displaystyle=2\left(\boldsymbol{\eta^{A}}-\boldsymbol{\etab^{A}}\right)\boldsymbol{e_{A}}+\boldsymbol{\omegah e_{3}}-\boldsymbol{\omegabh e_{4}}\,.

(75)

The last two terms on the right hand side of (
72
) encode the (non-)integrability of the horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
.

4.6.3

Commutation formulae

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
and

two-tensor
\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}},\boldsymbol{\nablasl}]\boldsymbol{\varsigma}=

\displaystyle\,-\boldsymbol{\chi}\boldsymbol{\times}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl\varsigma})+\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{3}}\varsigma})+(\boldsymbol{\etab}+\boldsymbol{\zeta})\otimes(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{4}}\varsigma})

(76)

\displaystyle+(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\varsigma})\,\boldsymbol{\chib}-(\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\varsigma})\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}+(\boldsymbol{\etab},\boldsymbol{\varsigma})\,\boldsymbol{\chi}-(\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\varsigma})\otimes\boldsymbol{\etab}+(\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\beta})\otimes(\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\varsigma})\,,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}},\boldsymbol{\nablasl}]\boldsymbol{\varsigma}=

\displaystyle\,-\boldsymbol{\chib}\boldsymbol{\times}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl\varsigma})+\boldsymbol{\yb}\otimes(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{4}}\varsigma})+(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\zeta})\otimes(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{3}}\varsigma})

(77)

\displaystyle+(\boldsymbol{\yb},\boldsymbol{\varsigma})\,\boldsymbol{\chi}-(\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\varsigma})\otimes\boldsymbol{\yb}+(\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\varsigma})\,\boldsymbol{\chib}-(\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\varsigma})\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}-(\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\betab})\otimes(\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\varsigma})\,,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}},\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}]\boldsymbol{\varsigma}=

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{\hat{\omega}}\,(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma})-\boldsymbol{\hat{\omegab}}\,(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma})+2\,(\boldsymbol{\nablasl\varsigma})^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{1}}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\etab})

(78)

\displaystyle+2\,(\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\varsigma})\,\boldsymbol{\etab}-2\,(\boldsymbol{\etab},\boldsymbol{\varsigma})\,\boldsymbol{\eta}+2\,(\boldsymbol{\yb},\boldsymbol{\varsigma})\,\boldsymbol{\xi}-2\,(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\varsigma})\,\boldsymbol{\yb}+2\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\varsigma})

and

\displaystyle([\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}},\boldsymbol{\nablasl}]\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\boldsymbol{ABC}}=

\displaystyle\,-\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{D}\theta})_{\boldsymbol{BC}}+\boldsymbol{\xi_{A}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{3}}\theta})_{\boldsymbol{BC}}+(\boldsymbol{\etab_{A}}+\boldsymbol{\zeta_{A}})(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{4}}\theta})_{\boldsymbol{BC}}

(79)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\chib_{AB}\xi^{D}\theta_{DC}}-\boldsymbol{\xi_{B}}\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}}\boldsymbol{\theta_{DC}}+\boldsymbol{\chi_{AB}\etab^{D}\theta_{DC}}-\boldsymbol{\etab_{B}}\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}}\boldsymbol{\theta_{DC}}+\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}\boldsymbol{{}_{B}^{D}}(\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\beta})_{\boldsymbol{A}}\boldsymbol{\theta_{DC}}

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\chib_{AC}\xi^{D}\theta_{BD}}-\boldsymbol{\xi_{C}}\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}}\boldsymbol{\theta_{BD}}+\boldsymbol{\chi_{AC}\etab^{D}\theta_{BD}}-\boldsymbol{\etab_{C}}\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}}\boldsymbol{\theta_{BD}}+\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}\boldsymbol{{}_{C}^{D}}(\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\beta})_{\boldsymbol{A}}\boldsymbol{\theta_{BD}}\,,

\displaystyle([\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}},\boldsymbol{\nablasl}]\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\boldsymbol{ABC}}=

\displaystyle\,-\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{D}\theta})_{\boldsymbol{BC}}+\boldsymbol{\yb_{A}}(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{4}}\theta})_{\boldsymbol{BC}}+(\boldsymbol{\eta_{A}}-\boldsymbol{\zeta_{A}})(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{3}}\theta})_{\boldsymbol{BC}}

(80)

(81)

]
. Note that, in our case, one has to keep track of the order of the indices of
