4.7

The nonlinear system of equations

We assume that
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
solves the vacuum Einstein equations

\boldsymbol{\ric}(\boldsymbol{g})=0\,.

The nonlinear system of equations for the connections coefficients and curvature components relative to a local null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is presented below. The formal derivation of the system appears in Section
4.8
.

Remark 4.50
.

It is crucial, at this stage, that the frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is allowed to be non-integrable. The system of equations of this section differs from the one appearing in
[
14
]
, where the frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is assumed to be integrable. The antitraces
(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})
and
(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})
generate new terms in our equations. Some of the elliptic equations of
[
14
]
may now be viewed as transport equations for
(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})
and
(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})
. We also note that, in the equations for symmetric quantities, the second fundamental forms appearing in the equations of
[
14
]
are replaced by the symmetric part of the second fundamental forms (e.g. in the first variational formulae). The Gauss equation is written in terms of the corrected Gauss curvature (
70
).

Remark 4.51
.

If the frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is assumed to be integrable, then the identities

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})

\displaystyle=0

hold and our system of equations reduces to the system of equations appearing in
[
14
]
.

4.7.1

Null structure equations

We have the first variational formulae

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{4}}\,\not{g}}

\displaystyle=2\,\boldsymbol{\chih}+(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chi}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{3}}\,\not{g}}

\displaystyle=2\,\boldsymbol{\chibh}+(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}\,,

(82)

the second variational formulae

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\chih}+(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\chih}-\boldsymbol{\omegah\chih}=-2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\,\xi}+(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{\etab}+2\,\boldsymbol{\zeta})\,\boldsymbol{\widehat{\otimes}\,\xi}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,,

(83)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\chibh}+(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\chibh}-\boldsymbol{\omegabh\,\chibh}=-2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\yb}+(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{\etab}-2\,\boldsymbol{\zeta})\,\boldsymbol{\widehat{\otimes}\,\yb}-\boldsymbol{\alphab}\,,

(84)

the Raychaudhuri equations

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})^{2}-\boldsymbol{\omegah}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})=-(\boldsymbol{\chih},\boldsymbol{\chih})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})^{2}+2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\,\xi}+2\,(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{\etab}+2\,\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\xi})\,,

(85)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}}(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})^{2}-\boldsymbol{\omegabh}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})=-(\boldsymbol{\chibh},\boldsymbol{\chibh})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})^{2}+2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\yb}+2\,(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{\etab}-2\,\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\yb})\,,

(86)

the mixed transport equations

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\chibh}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\chibh}+\boldsymbol{\omegah\chibh}=

\displaystyle\,-2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\etab}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\chih}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})(^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\chih})-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})(^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\chibh})

(87)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\etab\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\etab}+\boldsymbol{\xi\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\yb}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\chih}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\chih}+\boldsymbol{\omegabh\chih}=

\displaystyle\,-2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\,\eta}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\chibh}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})(^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\chibh})-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})(^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\chih})

(88)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\eta\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\eta}+\boldsymbol{\yb\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\xi}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})+\boldsymbol{\omegah}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})=

\displaystyle\,-(\boldsymbol{\chih},\boldsymbol{\chibh})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})+2\,(\boldsymbol{\etab},\boldsymbol{\etab})+2\,\boldsymbol{\rho}

(89)

\displaystyle+2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\etab}+2\,(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\yb})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}}(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})+\boldsymbol{\omegabh}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})=

\displaystyle\,-(\boldsymbol{\chih},\boldsymbol{\chibh})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})+2\,(\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\eta})+2\,\boldsymbol{\rho}

(90)

\displaystyle+2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\,\eta}+2\,(\boldsymbol{\yb},\boldsymbol{\xi})\,,

the transport equations for the antitraces of the second fundamental forms

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})+(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})-\boldsymbol{\omegah}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})=2\,(\boldsymbol{\etab}+2\,\boldsymbol{\zeta})\boldsymbol{\wedge\,\xi}+2\,\boldsymbol{\xi\wedge\eta}+2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{curl}}\,\xi}\,,

(91)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}}(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})+(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})-\boldsymbol{\omegabh}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})=2\,(\boldsymbol{\eta}-2\,\boldsymbol{\zeta})\boldsymbol{\wedge\yb}+2\,\boldsymbol{\yb\wedge\etab}+2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{curl}}\,\yb}\,,

(92)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}}(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})+\boldsymbol{\omegah}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})=

\displaystyle\,-\boldsymbol{\chih\wedge\chibh}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})+2\,\boldsymbol{\xi\wedge\yb}

(93)

\displaystyle+2\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}+2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{curl}}\,\etab}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}}(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})+\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})+\boldsymbol{\omegabh}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})=

\displaystyle\,-\boldsymbol{\chibh\wedge\chih}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})+2\,\boldsymbol{\yb\wedge\,\xi}

(94)

\displaystyle-2\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}+2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{curl}}\,\eta}

and the transport equations

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\eta}=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\xi}-\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{1}}\cdot}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\etab})+2\,\boldsymbol{\omegabh\xi}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\,,

(95)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\etab}=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\yb}+\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{1}}\cdot}(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\etab})+2\,\boldsymbol{\omegah\yb}+\boldsymbol{\betab}\,,

(96)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\omegabh}+\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\omegah}=2\,(\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\etab})-2\,(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\yb})-2\,\boldsymbol{\omegah\omegabh}-2\,(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\etab},\boldsymbol{\zeta})-2\,\boldsymbol{\rho}

(97)

and the transport equations for the torsion

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\zeta}=-\boldsymbol{\nablasl\omegah}+\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot}(\boldsymbol{\etab}-\boldsymbol{\zeta})-\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\xi}-\boldsymbol{\omegah}\,(\boldsymbol{\etab}+\boldsymbol{\zeta})+\boldsymbol{\omegabh\xi}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\,,

(98)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\zeta}=\boldsymbol{\nablasl\omegabh}-\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot}(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{\zeta})+\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\yb}+\boldsymbol{\omegabh}\,(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\zeta})-\boldsymbol{\omegah\yb}-\boldsymbol{\betab}

(99)

and the elliptic equation for the torsion

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{curl}}\,\zeta}=-\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\chih\wedge\chibh}+\frac{1}{4}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})-\frac{1}{4}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\omegah}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\omegabh}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\,.

(100)

We have the elliptic Codazzi equations

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\chih}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})-\frac{1}{2}\,{}^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})-\boldsymbol{\chih^{\sharp}\cdot\,\zeta}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\zeta}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}-(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\xi}

(101)

\displaystyle-(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\eta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\chibh}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})-\frac{1}{2}\,{}^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})+\boldsymbol{\chibh^{\sharp}\cdot\,\zeta}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\zeta}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}-(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\yb}

(102)

\displaystyle-(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\etab}+\boldsymbol{\betab}

and the elliptic Gauss equation

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{K}}}=\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\chih},\boldsymbol{\chibh})-\frac{1}{4}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})-\frac{1}{4}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})-\boldsymbol{\rho}\,.

(103)

Remark 4.52
.

For future convenience, we note that the null structure equations (
95
), (
96
), (
98
) and (
99
) can be written in the alternative form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}}_{\boldsymbol{4}}\,\boldsymbol{\eta}-(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi}){}^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\xi}+\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{1}}\cdot}\,\boldsymbol{\etab}+2\,\boldsymbol{\omegabh\xi}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}\,\boldsymbol{\etab}-(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib}){}^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\etab}=\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\yb}+\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{1}}\cdot}\,\boldsymbol{\eta}+2\,\boldsymbol{\omegah\yb}+\boldsymbol{\betab}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}}_{\boldsymbol{4}}\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}=-\boldsymbol{\nablasl\omegah}+\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot}\,\boldsymbol{\etab}-\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\,\xi}-\boldsymbol{\omegah}\,(\boldsymbol{\etab}+\boldsymbol{\zeta})+\boldsymbol{\omegabh\xi}-\boldsymbol{\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}}_{\boldsymbol{3}}\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\boldsymbol{\nablasl\omegabh}-\boldsymbol{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot}\,\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}\cdot\yb}+\boldsymbol{\omegabh}\,(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\zeta})-\boldsymbol{\omegah\yb}-\boldsymbol{\betab}\,.

4.7.2

Bianchi equations

The Bianchi equations read

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}+2\,\boldsymbol{\omegabh\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib}){\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\alpha}}=-2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\,\beta}-3\,\boldsymbol{\rho\chih}-3\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}{\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\chih}}+(4\,\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{\zeta})\,\boldsymbol{\widehat{\otimes}\,\beta}\,,

(104)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\beta}+2\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\omegah\beta}-2\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\beta}=\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\,\alpha}+(\boldsymbol{\etab^{\sharp}}+2\,\boldsymbol{\zeta^{\sharp}})\boldsymbol{\cdot\,\alpha}+3\,\boldsymbol{\rho\,\xi}+3\,\boldsymbol{\sigma{}^{\star}\xi}\,,

(105)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\beta}+(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\beta}+\boldsymbol{\omegabh\beta}+(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\beta}=\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{\star}}(-\boldsymbol{\rho},\boldsymbol{\sigma})+3\,\boldsymbol{\rho\,\eta}+3\,\boldsymbol{\sigma{}^{\star}\eta}+2\,\boldsymbol{\chih^{\sharp}\cdot\betab}+\boldsymbol{\yb^{\sharp}\cdot\,\alpha}\,,

(106)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\rho}=\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\,\beta}+(2\,\boldsymbol{\etab}+\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\beta})-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\chibh},\boldsymbol{\alpha})-2\,(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\betab})-\frac{3}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\,,

(107)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\sigma}+\frac{3}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}=-\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{curl}}\,\beta}-(2\,\boldsymbol{\etab}+\boldsymbol{\zeta})\boldsymbol{\wedge\,\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\chibh\wedge\,\alpha}-2\,\boldsymbol{\xi\wedge\betab}+\frac{3}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,,

(108)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\rho}=-\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\betab}-(2\,\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\betab})-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\chih},\boldsymbol{\alphab})+2\,(\boldsymbol{\yb},\boldsymbol{\beta})+\frac{3}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\,,

(109)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\sigma}+\frac{3}{2}\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}=-\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{curl}}\betab}-(2\,\boldsymbol{\eta}-\boldsymbol{\zeta})\boldsymbol{\wedge\betab}-\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\chih\wedge\alphab}-2\,\boldsymbol{\yb\wedge\,\beta}-\frac{3}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\rho}\,,

(110)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\betab}+(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\betab}+\boldsymbol{\omegah\betab}+(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\betab}=\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{1}^{\star}}(\boldsymbol{\rho},\boldsymbol{\sigma})-3\,\boldsymbol{\rho\,\etab}+3\,\boldsymbol{\sigma{}^{\star}\etab}+2\,\boldsymbol{\chibh^{\sharp}\cdot\,\beta}-\boldsymbol{\xi^{\sharp}\cdot\alphab}\,,

(111)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{3}\betab}+2\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})\,\boldsymbol{\betab}-\boldsymbol{\omegabh\betab}-2\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\betab}=-\boldsymbol{\not{\textbf{div}}\alphab}-(\boldsymbol{\eta^{\sharp}}-2\,\boldsymbol{\zeta^{\sharp}})\boldsymbol{\cdot\alphab}-3\,\boldsymbol{\rho\yb}+3\,\boldsymbol{\sigma{}^{\star}\yb}\,,

(112)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{4}\alphab}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})\,\boldsymbol{\alphab}+2\,\boldsymbol{\omegah\alphab}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi}){\boldsymbol{{}^{\star}\alphab}}=2\,\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\betab}-3\,\boldsymbol{\rho\chibh}+3\,\boldsymbol{\sigma{{}^{\star}\chibh}}-(4\,\boldsymbol{\etab}-\boldsymbol{\zeta})\,\boldsymbol{\widehat{\otimes}\,\betab}\,.

(113)

4.8

The formal derivation of the equations

We derive the system of vacuum Einstein equations of Section
4.7
. In this section only, we abandon the bolded notation. In our derivation of the equations, we will box the terms that do not appear in the derivation of the system of equations of
[
14
]
.

4.8.1

Null structure equations

To derive the null structure equations, one applies the formula

g(\nabla_{e_{I}}\nabla_{e_{J}}e_{K},e_{L})-g(\nabla_{e_{J}}\nabla_{e_{I}}e_{K},e_{L})-g(\nabla_{[e_{I},e_{J}]}e_{K},e_{L})=R(e_{L},e_{K},e_{I},e_{J})\,,

(114)

with
I,J,K,L=\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
and
R
the Riemann curvature tensor of
(\mathcal{M},g)
. We recall that, in our notation,
\nabla_{e_{I}}\nabla_{e_{J}}e_{K}=\nabla_{e_{I}}(\nabla_{e_{J}}e_{K})
. The reader should already note that, when
I,J=\left\{1,2\right\}
,
the commutator in the last term on the left hand side of (
114
) captures the non-integrability of the null frame
\mathcal{N}
.

We only derive a subset of the null structure equations. The remaining equations can be derived by conjugation (see Remark
4.53
). We also omit the straightforward derivation of the first variational formulae.

Before starting, let us note that, crucially,
in our computations one has to keep track of the order of the frame indices of
\chi
and
\chib
.

All the equalities in this first part of the computation are equalities between scalar functions and all the connection coefficients and curvature components are intended as
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
tensors. We exploit the spacetime scalar formula (
114
) to derive identities for frame components of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
tensors. The vacuum Einstein equations are not, at this stage, assumed to hold. We present the computation of the first term in full detail, to clarify the steps. The logic in computing the following terms is analogous and presented in a more compact fashion.

We compute

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{3}\nabla_{A}e_{3},e_{B})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{3}(\chib_{AB})-g(\nabla_{A}e_{3},\nabla_{3}e_{B})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{3}(\chib_{AB})-g(\chib^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}e_{C}+\zeta_{A}e_{3},\not{\underline{M}}_{B}^{D}e_{D}+\eta_{B}e_{3}+\yb_{B}e_{4})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{3}(\chib_{AB})-\not{\underline{M}}_{B}^{C}\chib_{AC}+2\zeta_{A}\yb_{B}

\displaystyle=\nablasl_{3}(\chib_{AB})-\not{\underline{M}}_{B}^{C}\chib_{AC}+2\zeta_{A}\yb_{B}

\displaystyle=(\nablasl_{3}\chib)_{AB}+\not{\underline{M}}_{A}^{C}\chib_{CB}+2\zeta_{A}\yb_{B}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{A}\nabla_{3}e_{3},e_{B})

\displaystyle=2(\nablasl\yb)_{AB}+\omegabh\chib_{AB}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{[e_{3},e_{A}]}e_{3},e_{B})

\displaystyle=(\not{\underline{M}}_{A}^{C}-{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{C})\chib_{CB}+2(\eta_{A}-\zeta_{A})\yb_{B}+2\yb_{A}\etab_{B}

and obtain the equation

(\nablasl_{3}\chib)_{AB}=2(\nablasl\yb)_{AB}+\omegabh\chib_{AB}-{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{C}\chib_{CB}+2(\eta_{A}-2\zeta_{A})\yb_{B}+2\yb_{A}\etab_{B}-\alphab_{AB}\,.

(115)

Note that in the detailed computation of the first term we used the identities
\nabla_{3}(\chib_{AB})=\nablasl_{3}(\chib_{AB})
and
(\nablasl_{3}\chib)_{AB}=\nablasl_{3}(\chib_{AB})-\not{\underline{M}}_{A}^{C}\chib_{CB}-\not{\underline{M}}_{B}^{C}\chib_{AC}
, the former holding because we apply the covariant derivative to a scalar function (and thus one can exchange the connection) and the latter by definition of the induced covariant derivative
\nablasl
applied to a
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
covariant tensor.

Similarly, we compute

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{4}\nabla_{A}e_{3},e_{B})

\displaystyle=(\nablasl_{4}\chib)_{AB}+\not{M}_{A}^{C}\chib_{CB}+2\zeta_{A}\etab_{B}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{A}\nabla_{4}e_{3},e_{B})

\displaystyle=2(\nablasl\etab)_{AB}-\omegah\chib_{AB}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{[e_{4},e_{A}]}e_{3},e_{B})

\displaystyle=(\not{M}_{A}^{C}-{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{C})\chib_{CB}+2\xi_{A}\yb_{B}+2(\etab_{A}+\zeta_{A})\etab_{B}

and obtain the equation

(\nablasl_{4}\chib)_{AB}=-2\zeta_{A}\etab_{B}+2(\nablasl\etab)_{AB}-\omegah\chib_{AB}-{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{C}\chib_{CB}+2\xi_{A}\yb_{B}+2(\etab_{A}+\zeta_{A})\etab_{B}+R(e_{B},e_{3},e_{4},e_{A})\,.

(116)

We compute

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{C}\nabla_{B}e_{3},e_{A})

\displaystyle=(\nablasl\chib)_{CBA}+{}_{C}\not{H}_{B}^{D}\chib_{DA}+\zeta_{B}\chib_{CA}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{B}\nabla_{C}e_{3},e_{A})

\displaystyle=(\nablasl\chib)_{BCA}+{}_{B}\not{\underline{M}}_{C}^{D}\chib_{DA}+\zeta_{C}\chib_{BA}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{[e_{C},e_{B}]}e_{3},e_{A})

\displaystyle=({}_{C}\not{H}_{B}^{D}-{}_{B}\not{\underline{M}}_{C}^{D})\chib_{DA}\boxed{+(\chi_{CB}-\chi_{BC})\yb_{A}}\,\boxed{+(\chib_{CB}-\chib_{BC})\etab_{A}}

and obtain the equation

\displaystyle(\nablasl\chib)_{CBA}+\zeta_{B}\chib_{CA}=

\displaystyle\,(\nablasl\chib)_{BCA}+\zeta_{C}\chib_{BA}\boxed{+(\chi_{CB}-\chi_{BC})\yb_{A}}\,\boxed{+(\chib_{CB}-\chib_{BC})\etab_{A}}

(117)

\displaystyle+R(e_{A},e_{3},e_{C},e_{B})\,.

We compute

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{3}\nabla_{A}e_{3},e_{4})

\displaystyle=-2(\nablasl_{3}\zeta)_{A}-2\not{\underline{M}}_{A}^{B}\zeta_{B}-2{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{B}\eta_{B}-2\omegabh\zeta_{A}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{A}\nabla_{3}e_{3},e_{4})

\displaystyle=-2(\nablasl\omegabh)_{A}-2{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{B}\yb_{B}-2\omegabh\zeta_{A}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{[e_{3},e_{A}]}e_{3},e_{4})

\displaystyle=-2(\not{\underline{M}}_{A}^{B}-{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{B})\zeta_{B}-2(\eta_{A}-\zeta_{A})\omegabh+2\omegah\yb_{A}

and obtain the equation

(\nablasl_{3}\zeta)_{A}=(\nablasl\omegabh)_{A}-{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{B}\eta_{B}+{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{B}\yb_{B}-{\chib^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{B}\zeta_{B}+\omegabh(\eta_{A}-\zeta_{A})-\omegah\yb_{A}-\betab_{A}\,.

(118)

We compute

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{3}\nabla_{4}e_{3},e_{A})

\displaystyle=2(\nablasl_{3}\etab)_{A}-2\omegah\yb_{A}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{4}\nabla_{3}e_{3},e_{A})

\displaystyle=2(\nablasl_{4}\yb)_{A}+2\omegabh\etab_{A}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{[e_{3},e_{4}]}e_{3},e_{A})

\displaystyle=2{\chib^{\sharp_{1}}}{}^{B}_{A}(\eta_{B}-\etab_{B})+2\omegah\yb_{A}-2\omegabh\etab_{A}

and obtain the equation

(\nablasl_{3}\etab)_{A}-(\nablasl_{4}\yb)_{A}={\chib^{\sharp_{1}}}{}^{B}_{A}(\eta_{B}-\etab_{B})+2\omegah\yb_{A}+\betab_{A}\,.

(119)

We compute

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{4}\nabla_{3}e_{3},e_{4})

\displaystyle=-2\nablasl_{4}\omegabh-4\xi^{A}\yb_{A}+2\omegah\omegabh\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{3}\nabla_{4}e_{3},e_{4})

\displaystyle=2\nablasl_{3}\omegah-4\eta^{A}\etab_{A}+2\omegah\omegabh\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{[e_{4},e_{3}]}e_{3},e_{4})

\displaystyle=4(\eta^{A}-\etab^{A})\zeta_{A}+4\omegah\omegabh

and obtain the equation

\nablasl_{4}\omegabh+\nablasl_{3}\omegah=2\eta^{A}\etab_{A}-2\xi^{A}\yb_{A}-2\omegah\omegabh-2(\eta^{A}-\etab^{A})\zeta_{A}-2\rho\,.

(120)

We compute

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}e_{4},e_{3})

\displaystyle=2(\nablasl\zeta)_{AB}-\chi_{BD}\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}-2\zeta_{A}\zeta_{B}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{B}\nabla_{A}e_{4},e_{3})

\displaystyle=2(\nablasl\zeta)_{BA}-\chi_{AD}\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{B}^{D}-2\zeta_{A}\zeta_{B}\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{[e_{A},e_{B}]}e_{4},e_{3})

\displaystyle=2({}_{A}\not{H}_{B}^{C}-{}_{B}\not{H}_{A}^{C})\zeta_{C}\boxed{-\omegah(\chib_{AB}-\chib_{BA})}\,\boxed{+\omegabh(\chi_{AB}-\chi_{BA})}

and obtain the equation

\displaystyle 2(\nablasl\zeta)_{AB}-2(\nablasl\zeta)_{BA}-\chi_{BD}\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}{}_{A}^{D}

\displaystyle+\chi_{AD}\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{B}^{D}-2({}_{A}\not{H}_{B}^{C}-{}_{B}\not{H}_{A}^{C})\zeta_{C}

(121)

\displaystyle\boxed{+\omegah(\chib_{AB}-\chib_{BA})}\,\boxed{-\omegabh(\chi_{AB}-\chi_{BA})}=R(e_{3},e_{4},e_{A},e_{B})\,.

Following a slightly different logic, we compute

\displaystyle g(\nabla^{2}_{e_{C},e_{B}}e_{D},e_{A})=

\displaystyle\,g(\nabla_{C}(\nabla_{e_{B}}e_{D})-\nabla_{\nabla_{C}e_{B}}e_{D},e_{A})\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,g(\nabla_{C}(\nablasl_{B}e_{D}+\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{BD}e_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\,\chib_{BD}e_{4}),e_{A})

\displaystyle-g(\nabla_{\nablasl_{C}e_{B}+\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{CB}e_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\,\chib_{CB}e_{4}}e_{D},e_{A})\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,g(\nabla_{C}(\nablasl_{B}e_{D}),e_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{BD}\,g(\nabla_{C}e_{3},e_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\chib_{BD}g(\nabla_{C}e_{4},e_{A})

\displaystyle-g(\nabla_{\nablasl_{C}e_{B}}e_{D},e_{A})-\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{CB}\,g(\nabla_{3}e_{D},e_{A})-\frac{1}{2}\,\chib_{CB}g(\nabla_{4}e_{D},e_{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,g(\nablasl_{C}(\nablasl_{B}e_{D}),e_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{BD}\,\chib_{CA}+\frac{1}{2}\,\chib_{BD}\,\chi_{CA}

\displaystyle-g(\nablasl_{\nablasl_{C}e_{B}}e_{D},e_{A})-\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{CB}\,g(\nablasl_{3}e_{D},e_{A})-\frac{1}{2}\,\chib_{CB}g(\nablasl_{4}e_{D},e_{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\not{g}(\nablasl^{2}_{e_{C},e_{B}}e_{D},e_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{BD}\chib_{CA}+\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{CA}\chib_{BD}-\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{CB}\,\not{g}(\nablasl_{3}e_{D},e_{A})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\chib_{CB}\not{g}(\nablasl_{4}e_{D},e_{A})\,,

\displaystyle g(\nabla^{2}_{e_{B},e_{C}}e_{D},e_{A})=

\displaystyle\,\not{g}(\nablasl^{2}_{e_{B},e_{C}}e_{D},e_{A})+\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{CD}\chib_{BA}+\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{BA}\chib_{CD}-\frac{1}{2}\,\chi_{BC}\,\not{g}(\nablasl_{3}e_{D},e_{A})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\chib_{BC}\not{g}(\nablasl_{4}e_{D},e_{A})

and obtain the equation

\displaystyle\boxed{\not{R}(e_{A},e_{D},e_{C},e_{B})}\,

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,(\chi_{BD}\chib_{CA}+\chi_{CA}\chib_{BD})-\frac{1}{2}\,(\chi_{CD}\chib_{BA}+\chi_{BA}\chib_{CD})

(122)

\displaystyle\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}\,(\chi_{CB}-\chi_{BC})\,\not{g}(\nablasl_{3}e_{D},e_{A})}\,\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}\,(\chib_{CB}-\chib_{BC})\,\not{g}(\nablasl_{4}e_{D},e_{A})}

\displaystyle=R(e_{A},e_{D},e_{C},e_{B})\,.

We note that we used the identities
g(\nabla_{C}(\nablasl_{B}e_{D}),e_{A})=g(\nablasl_{C}(\nablasl_{B}e_{D}),e_{A})
and
g(\nabla_{\nablasl_{C}e_{B}}e_{D},e_{A})=g(\nablasl_{\nablasl_{C}e_{B}}e_{D},e_{A})
, holding by definition of the induced covariant derivative
\nablasl
applied to a
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
vector field, and the identity
g_{AB}=\not{g}_{AB}
. We also recall the definition of the map
\not{R}
from (
66
) and the fact that the
sum
of the boxed terms in the equation (
122
) is tensorial (although the single terms separately are not).

We now assume that
(\mathcal{M},g)
is a solution to the vacuum Einstein equations

\ric(g)=0\,.

Then, one has the identities

\displaystyle\tr\alpha

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\tr\alphab

\displaystyle=0\,,

(123)

where the trace is taken with respect to
\not{g}^{-1}
, and the identities

\displaystyle R_{A34B}

\displaystyle=\rho\,\not{g}_{AB}-\sigma\,\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}\,,

(124)

\displaystyle R_{A3CB}

\displaystyle=(^{\star}\betab)_{A}\not{\varepsilon}_{CB}\,,

(125)

\displaystyle R_{34AB}

\displaystyle=2\,\sigma\,\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}

(126)

and

\not{g}^{AC}\not{g}^{BD}R_{ABCD}=-2\,\rho\,.

(127)

The identities (
124
)-(
127
) rely on the symmetries of the Riemann curvature tensor
R
and the Bianchi identities for
R
. The non-integrability of the null frame
\mathcal{N}
does not enter into the computation. The proof of the identities (
124
)-(
127
) is thus analogous to that of
[
14
]
.

The identities (
124
), (
125
) and (
126
) can be used to replace the curvature terms in the equations (
116
), (
117
) and (
121
) respectively. For instance, one can use the identity (
124
) to write the equation (
116
) as

(\nablasl_{4}\chib)_{AB}=-2\zeta_{A}\etab_{B}+2(\nablasl\etab)_{AB}-\omegah\chib_{AB}-{\chi^{\sharp_{2}}}{}_{A}^{C}\chib_{CB}+2\xi_{A}\yb_{B}+2(\etab_{A}+\zeta_{A})\etab_{B}+\rho\,\not{g}_{AB}+\sigma\,\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}\,.

With these last replacements of the curvature terms, all the equations (
115
)-(
121
) can now be viewed as equations for frame components of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
tensors, and can thus be written in
geometric form
as equations for
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
tensors. For example, one can write the equation (
116
) as

\nablasl_{4}\chib=-2\zeta\otimes\etab+2\nablasl\etab-\omegah\chib-\chi\times\chib+2\xi\otimes\yb+2(\etab+\zeta)\otimes\etab+\rho\,\not{g}-\sigma\,\not{\varepsilon}\,.

To obtain the system of equations in its final form, some additional manipulations are needed.

By taking the
trace
(with respect to
\not{g}^{-1}
) part, the
antitrace
(with respect to
\not{\varepsilon}^{-1}
) part and the
symmetric traceless
part of the equation (
115
), one splits the equation (
115
) into the three equations

\displaystyle\nablasl_{3}(\text{tr}\chib)+\frac{1}{2}(\text{tr}\chib)^{2}-\omegabh(\text{tr}\chib)=-(\chibh,\chibh)\boxed{+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)^{2}}+2\,\not{\text{div}}\yb+2(\eta+\etab-2\zeta,\yb)\,,

\displaystyle\boxed{\nablasl_{3}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)+(\text{tr}\chib)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)-\omegabh(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}=2(\eta-2\zeta)\wedge\yb+2\yb\wedge\etab+2\,\not{\text{curl}}\,\yb\,,

\displaystyle\nablasl_{3}\chibh+(\text{tr}\chib)\chibh-\omegabh\chibh=-2\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\yb+(\eta+\etab-2\zeta)\htimes\yb-\alphab

respectively. Note that we used the identities

\displaystyle(\chib,\chib)

\displaystyle=(\chibh,\chibh)+\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\chib)^{2}\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)^{2}}

\displaystyle\chib\wedge\chib

\displaystyle=\boxed{(\text{tr}\chib)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}

and

\displaystyle(\chib\times\chib)-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{g}^{AB}(\chib\times\chib)_{AB})\,\not{g}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}^{AB}(\chib\times\chib)_{AB})\,\not{\varepsilon}=(\text{tr}\chib)\chibh

and the second of the identities (
123
).

By taking the
trace
part, the
antitrace
part and the
symmetric traceless
part of equation (
116
), one obtains

\displaystyle\nablasl_{4}(\text{tr}\chib)+\frac{1}{2}(\text{tr}\chi)(\text{tr}\chib)+\omegah(\text{tr}\chib)=-(\chih,\chibh)\boxed{+\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}+2(\etab,\etab)+2\rho+2\,\not{\text{div}}\etab+2(\xi,\yb)\,,

\displaystyle\boxed{\nablasl_{4}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)+\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\chi)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)+\omegah(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}=-\chih\wedge\chibh\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\chib)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)}+2\xi\wedge\yb+2\sigma+2\,\not{\text{curl}}\,\etab\,,

\displaystyle\nablasl_{4}\chibh+\frac{1}{2}(\text{tr}\chi)\chibh+\omegah\chibh=-2\not{\mathcal{D}}_{2}^{\star}\etab-\frac{1}{2}(\text{tr}\chib)\chih\boxed{+\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)({}^{\star}\chih)}\,\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)({}^{\star}\chibh)}+\etab\htimes\etab+\xi\htimes\yb

respectively. Note that we used the identities

\displaystyle(\chi,\chib)

\displaystyle=(\chih,\chibh)+\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\chi)(\text{tr}\chib)\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}\,,

\displaystyle\chi\wedge\chib

\displaystyle=\chih\wedge\chibh\boxed{+\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\chi)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}\,\boxed{+\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\chib)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)}

and

\displaystyle(\chi\times\chib)-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{g}^{AB}(\chi\times\chib)_{AB})\,\not{g}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}^{AB}(\chi\times\chib)_{AB})\,\not{\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\chi)\chibh+\frac{1}{2}\,(\text{tr}\chib)\chih

\displaystyle\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)({}^{\star}\chih)}\,\boxed{+\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)({}^{\star}\chibh)}\,.

By taking the trace of the equation (
117
), one obtains the Codazzi equation

\displaystyle\not{\text{div}}\chibh=

\displaystyle\,\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}\,{}^{\star}\nablasl(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}+\chibh^{\sharp}\cdot\zeta\boxed{+\frac{1}{2}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib){}^{\star}\zeta}-\frac{1}{2}(\text{tr}\chib)\zeta+\frac{1}{2}\nablasl(\text{tr}\chib)\boxed{-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi){}^{\star}\yb}

\displaystyle\boxed{-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib){}^{\star}\etab}+\betab\,.

Note that one can equivalently take the trace of the equation (
117
) relative to either
\not{g}^{CA}
or
\not{g}^{BA}
. The trace relative to
\not{g}^{CB}
vanishes because the equation is antisymmetric in the horizontal indices
B,C
. Since the equation (
117
) is antisymmetric in two of the three horizontal indices, the equation is equivalent to the equation for
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
one-tensors obtained by taking any of its non-trivial traces or antitraces.

By taking the trace of the equation (
122
) relative to
\not{g}^{AC}\not{g}^{BD}
, one obtains the Gauss equation

\boxed{\widetilde{\not{K}}}=\frac{1}{2}(\chih,\chibh)-\frac{1}{4}(\text{tr}\chi)(\text{tr}\chib)\boxed{-\frac{1}{4}(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}-\rho\,,

where we used the identity (
127
) and the definition of the corrected Gauss curvature
\widetilde{\not{K}}
from (
70
).

By taking the antitrace of equation (
121
), one obtains

\not{\text{curl}}\,\zeta=-\frac{1}{2}\,\chih\wedge\chibh\boxed{+\frac{1}{4}\,(\text{tr}\chi)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}\,\boxed{-\frac{1}{4}\,(\text{tr}\chib)(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)}\,\boxed{-\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)\,\omegah}\,\boxed{+\frac{1}{2}\,(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)\,\omegabh}+\sigma\,.

Since the equation (
121
) is antisymmetric in the two horizontal indices, it is equivalent to the scalar equation obtained by taking its antitrace.

Remark 4.53
.

As already mentioned, the remaining null structure equations can be immediately deduced from the ones derived above. To do that, one exchanges
\nablasl_{4}
-derivatives with
\nablasl_{3}
-derivatives (and vice-versa) and the barred quantities with the unbarred quantities (and vice-versa). We note that
\rho
remains unchanged under conjugation, while
\zeta
and
\sigma
are to be replaced by
-\zeta
and
-\sigma
respectively. We also note that one substitutes
\beta
with
-\betab
and
\betab
with
-\beta
.

The derivation of the null structure equations of Section
4.7
is now complete.

4.8.2

Bianchi equations

We now assume that
(\mathcal{M},g)
is a solution to the vacuum Einstein equations

\ric(g)=0\,.

To derive the Bianchi equations, one applies the contracted Bianchi identities
47
47

47

These are equivalent to the second Bianchi identities
\nabla_{[I}R_{JK]LM}=0
when
(\mathcal{M},g)
solves the vacuum Einstein equations.

(\nabla^{I}R)_{IJKL}=0\,,

(128)

with
I,J,K,L=\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
and
R
the Riemann curvature tensor of
(\mathcal{M},g)
.

As for the null structure equations, we only derive a subset of the Bianchi equations. The remaining equations can be derived by conjugation or by Hodge duality (see Remark
4.54
).

Before starting, let us note that, crucially,
in our computations one has to keep track of the order of the frame indices of
\chi
and
\chib
.

All the equalities in this first part of the computation are equalities between scalar functions and all the connection coefficients and curvature components are intended as
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
tensors. We exploit the spacetime scalar identity (
128
) to derive identities for frame components of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
tensors. We present the computation of the first term in full detail, to clarify the steps. The logic in computing the following terms is analogous and presented in a more compact fashion.

We compute

\displaystyle(\nabla_{3}R)_{A4B4}=

\displaystyle\,\nabla_{3}(R_{A4B4})-R(\nabla_{3}e_{A},e_{4},e_{B},e_{4})-R(e_{A},\nabla_{3}e_{4},e_{B},e_{4})

\displaystyle-R(e_{A},e_{4},\nabla_{3}e_{B},e_{4})-R(e_{A},e_{4},e_{B},\nabla_{3}e_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\nabla_{3}(\alpha_{AB})-\not{\underline{M}}_{A}^{C}\alpha_{CB}-2\eta_{A}\beta_{B}-2\eta^{C}R_{ACB4}+\omegabh\alpha_{AB}

\displaystyle-\not{\underline{M}}_{B}^{C}\alpha_{AC}-2\eta_{B}\beta_{A}-2\eta^{C}R_{A4BC}+\omegabh\alpha_{AB}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\nablasl_{3}(\alpha_{AB})-\not{\underline{M}}_{A}^{C}\alpha_{CB}-\not{\underline{M}}_{B}^{C}\alpha_{AC}+2\omegabh\alpha_{AB}

\displaystyle-2\eta_{A}\beta_{B}-2\eta_{B}\beta_{A}+2\eta^{C}({}^{\star}\beta)_{A}\not{\varepsilon}_{BC}+2\eta^{C}({}^{\star}\beta)_{B}\not{\varepsilon}_{AC}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,(\nablasl_{3}\alpha)_{AB}+2\omegabh\alpha_{AB}-4(\eta\hat{\otimes}\beta)_{AB}\,,

where we used the identities
\nabla_{3}(\alpha_{AB})=\nablasl_{3}(\alpha_{AB})
and
(\nablasl_{3}\alpha)_{AB}=\nablasl_{3}(\alpha_{AB})-\not{\underline{M}}_{A}^{C}\alpha_{CB}-\not{\underline{M}}_{B}^{C}\alpha_{AC}
, the former holding because we apply the covariant derivative to a scalar function (and thus one can exchange the connection) and the latter by definition of the induced covariant derivative
\nablasl
applied to a
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
covariant tensor. We also used the conjugate of the identity (
125
) and the identity

2\eta^{C}({}^{\star}\beta)_{A}\not{\varepsilon}_{BC}=2\eta_{B}\beta_{A}-2(\beta\hat{\otimes}\eta)_{AB}\,.

Similarly, we compute

\displaystyle(\nabla_{4}R)_{A3B3}=

\displaystyle\,(\nablasl_{4}\alphab)_{AB}+2\omegah\alphab_{AB}+4(\etab\hat{\otimes}\betab)_{AB}\,,

(129)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{4}R)_{A4B3}=

\displaystyle\,-(\nablasl_{4}\rho)\not{g}_{AB}-(\nablasl_{4}\sigma)\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}-4\xi_{A}\betab_{B}+2(\betab\hat{\otimes}\xi)_{AB}

(130)

\displaystyle+4\etab_{B}\beta_{A}-2(\beta\hat{\otimes}\etab)_{AB}\,,

\displaystyle(\nabla_{4}R)_{A434}=

\displaystyle\,2(\nablasl_{4}\beta)_{A}-2\omegah\beta_{A}-2\etab^{B}\alpha_{AB}-6\xi_{A}\rho-6({}^{\star}\xi)_{A}\sigma\,,

(131)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{3}R)_{A434}=

\displaystyle\,2(\nablasl_{3}\beta)_{A}+2\omegabh\beta_{A}-2\yb^{B}\alpha_{AB}-6\eta_{A}\rho-6({}^{\star}\eta)_{A}\sigma\,,

(132)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{A}R)_{B4C4}=

\displaystyle\,(\nablasl_{A}\alpha)_{BC}+2\zeta_{A}\alpha_{BC}-2(\chi_{AB}\beta_{C}+\chi_{AC}\beta_{B}-\chi_{AD}\beta^{D}\not{g}_{BC})\,,

(133)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{A}R)_{B434}=

\displaystyle\,2(\nablasl_{A}\beta)_{B}+2\zeta_{A}\beta_{B}-\chib^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\alpha_{BC}-3\rho\chi_{AB}-3\sigma\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{B}^{C}\chi_{AC}\,,

(134)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{4}R)_{3434}=

\displaystyle\,4\nablasl_{4}\rho-8(\etab,\beta)+8(\xi,\betab)

(135)

and the contracted terms

\displaystyle(\nabla^{B}R)_{A4B4}=

\displaystyle\,(\nablasl^{B}\alpha)_{AB}+2\zeta^{B}\alpha_{AB}-2(\tr\chi)\beta_{A}\boxed{+2(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)({}^{\star}\beta)_{A}}\,,

(136)

\displaystyle(\nabla^{B}R)_{A3B3}=

\displaystyle\,(\nablasl^{B}\alphab)_{AB}-2\zeta^{B}\alphab_{AB}+2(\tr\chib)\betab_{A}\boxed{-2(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)({}^{\star}\betab)_{A}}\,,

(137)

where to compute the identities (
136
) and (
137
) we used the identity

-\chi^{\sharp_{1}}{}^{B}_{A}\beta_{B}-(\text{tr}\chi)\beta_{A}+\chi^{BD}({}^{\star}\beta)_{B}\not{\varepsilon}_{AD}+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)({}^{\star}\beta)_{A}=-2(\text{tr}\chi)\beta_{A}+2(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)({}^{\star}\beta)_{A}

(138)

and its conjugate. Using the identity (
134
) and its conjugate, we compute the sums

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((\nabla_{B}R)_{A434}+(\nabla_{A}R)_{B434})=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left(2(\nablasl_{B}\beta)_{A}+2\zeta_{B}\beta_{A}-\chib^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{B}^{C}\alpha_{AC}-3\rho\chi_{BA}-3\sigma\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\chi_{BC}\right.

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left(2(\nablasl_{B}\betab)_{A}-2\zeta_{B}\betab_{A}+\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{B}^{C}\alphab_{AC}+3\rho\chib_{BA}-3\sigma\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\chib_{BC}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+2(\nablasl_{A}\betab)_{B}-2\zeta_{A}\betab_{B}+\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\alphab_{BC}+3\rho\chib_{AB}-3\sigma\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{B}^{C}\chib_{AC}\right)

respectively. We compute the term

\displaystyle(\nabla_{C}R)_{A3B4}=

\displaystyle\,(\nablasl_{C}\sigma)\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}-(\nablasl_{C}\rho)\not{g}_{AB}+\chib_{CA}\beta_{B}-\chi_{CB}\betab_{A}

(140)

\displaystyle+\chib^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{C}^{D}({}^{\star}\beta)_{B}\not{\varepsilon}_{AD}-\chi^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{C}^{D}({}^{\star}\betab)_{A}\not{\varepsilon}_{BD}\,,

) and (the conjugate of) (

\displaystyle(\nabla^{A}R)_{A434}=

\displaystyle\,2\not{\divv}\beta+2(\zeta,\beta)-(\chib,\alpha)-3\rho(\tr\chi)\boxed{-3\sigma(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)}\,,

(141)

\displaystyle(\nabla^{A}R)_{A334}=

\displaystyle\,2\not{\divv}\betab-2(\zeta,\betab)+(\chi,\alphab)+3\rho(\tr\chib)\boxed{-3\sigma(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)}\,,

\displaystyle(\nabla^{B}R)_{A3B4}=

\displaystyle\,\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\nablasl_{C}\sigma-\nablasl_{A}\rho+2(\chibh\cdot\beta)_{A}-(\tr\chi)\betab_{A}\boxed{-(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)({}^{\star}\betab)_{A}}\,,

(143)

\displaystyle(\nabla^{B}R)_{B3A4}=

\displaystyle\,-\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\nablasl_{C}\sigma-\nablasl_{A}\rho-2(\chih\cdot\betab)_{A}+(\tr\chib)\beta_{A}\boxed{+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)({}^{\star}\beta)_{A}}

of the identity (

), the conjugate of the identity (

We now apply the contracted Bianchi identities (
128
). We compute

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\nabla^{I}R)_{I434}

\displaystyle=(\nabla^{A}R)_{A434}+(\nabla^{3}R)_{3434}

\displaystyle=(\nabla^{A}R)_{A434}-\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{4}R)_{3434}

\displaystyle=2\not{\divv}\beta+2(\zeta,\beta)-(\chib,\alpha)-3\rho(\tr\chi)\boxed{-3\sigma(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)}-2\nablasl_{4}\rho+4(\etab,\beta)-4(\xi,\betab)\,,

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\,(\nablasl^{B}\alpha)_{AB}+2\zeta^{B}\alpha_{AB}-2(\tr\chi)\beta_{A}\boxed{+2(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)({}^{\star}\beta)_{A}}-(\nablasl_{4}\beta)_{A}+\omegah\beta_{A}+\etab^{B}\alpha_{AB}

\displaystyle\,-\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\nablasl_{C}\sigma-\nablasl_{A}\rho-2(\chih\cdot\betab)_{A}+(\tr\chib)\beta_{A}\boxed{+(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)({}^{\star}\beta)_{A}}+(\nablasl_{3}\beta)_{A}+\omegabh\beta_{A}-\yb^{B}\alpha_{AB}

\displaystyle(\nabla_{3}R)_{A4B4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left((\nabla_{B}R)_{A434}+(\nabla_{A}R)_{B434}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left((\nabla_{4}R)_{A3B4}+(\nabla_{4}R)_{B3A4}\right)

\displaystyle(\nablasl_{3}\alpha)_{AB}+2\omegabh\alpha_{AB}-4(\eta\hat{\otimes}\beta)_{AB}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left(2(\nablasl_{B}\beta)_{A}+2\zeta_{B}\beta_{A}-\chib^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{B}^{C}\alpha_{AC}-3\rho\chi_{BA}-3\sigma\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\chi_{BC}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+2(\nablasl_{A}\beta)_{B}+2\zeta_{A}\beta_{B}-\chib^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\alpha_{BC}-3\rho\chi_{AB}-3\sigma\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{B}^{C}\chi_{AC}\right)

\displaystyle+(-\nablasl_{4}\rho-2(\xi,\betab)+2(\etab,\beta))\not{g}_{AB}\,.

The term
\nablasl_{4}\rho
in the last line can be replaced by using the equation (
145
).

Remark 4.54
.

The remaining Bianchi equations can be immediately deduced from the ones derived above as explained in Remark
4.53
. The Bianchi equations for
\sigma
can be deduced by taking the Hodge dual of the Bianchi equations for
\rho
.

The derivation of the Bianchi equations of Section
4.7
is now complete.

5

The Kerr exterior manifold

In this section, we identify the Lorentzian manifold
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})
and its Levi-Civita connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
from Section
4
with the
Kerr exterior manifold

(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
and its Levi-Civita connection
\nabla_{a,M}
. After
defining
the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
, we shall discuss a particular choice of differentiable structure and null frame on
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
. For the latter, we will identify the null frame
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
from Section
4
with the
algebraically special frame

\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
and the induced horizontal distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}
with
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
. The algebraically special frame of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
will be defined and, crucially, shown to be a
non-integrable
frame for
|a|>0
.

Treating the Kerr geometry relative to the non-integrable frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
requires (and already motivates) the general formalism from Section
4
. The connection coefficients and curvature components of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
solve the nonlinear system of null structure and Bianchi equations of Section
4.7
, whereas they do
not
solve the nonlinear system of null structure and Bianchi equations of
[
14
]
.

5.1

Definition of the Kerr exterior manifold

We define the manifold-with-boundary

\mathcal{M}:=(-\infty,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}

(146)

with coordinates
\bar{t}^{*}\in(-\infty,\infty)
,
y^{*}\in[0,\infty)
and standard spherical coordinates
(\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
. As usual, the coordinates
(\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})
only cover a subset of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Nonetheless, one can extend
(\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})
as
functions
on the whole
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Similarly, the coordinate vector field
\partial_{\bar{\phi}^{*}}
can be smoothly extended to a
global
vector field on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
which vanishes at the poles of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

We define the vector fields

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle:=\partial_{\bar{t}^{*}}\,,

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle:=\partial_{\bar{\phi}^{*}}

on
\mathcal{M}
. We will denote by
\widetilde{\Phi}
the smooth extension of
\Phi
to the whole
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. The vector field
\widetilde{\Phi}
is a
global
vector field on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, and thus on
\mathcal{M}
.

We define the
(future) event horizon
as the boundary

\mathcal{H}^{+}:=\partial\mathcal{M}

of
\mathcal{M}
and
(future) null infinity
as the formal hypersurface

\mathcal{I}^{+}:=\left\{\bar{t}^{*}=\infty\right\}\,.

We note that
\mathcal{H}^{+}=\left\{y^{*}=0\right\}
.

Given real parameters
a
and
M
, with
|a|<M
, we define the positive constants

r_{\pm}:=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}

and a new coordinate
\bar{r}_{a,M}=\bar{r}_{a,M}(y^{*})
such that

\bar{r}_{a,M}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[r_{+},\infty)\,,

with
\bar{r}_{a,M}(3M)=y^{*}(3M)
. We will simply denote
\bar{r}_{a,M}
by
\bar{r}
. We define the two-spheres

\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r}}:=\left\{\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r}\right\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\,.

The coordinates

(\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r},\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})

are referred to as
Kerr coordinates
. We note that
\mathcal{H}^{+}=\left\{\bar{r}=r_{+}\right\}
. We also note that the Kerr coordinates depend on the parameters
a
and
M
, whereas the definition of the ambient manifold
\mathcal{M}
and the vector fields
T
and
\Phi
are independent of parameters.

For fixed
|a|<M
, we define the smooth scalar functions

\displaystyle\Delta(\bar{r})

\displaystyle:=(\bar{r}-r_{+})(\bar{r}-r_{-})\,,

\displaystyle\Sigma(\bar{r},\bar{\theta})

\displaystyle:=\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\bar{\theta}\,.

on
\mathcal{M}
. We note that, strictly speaking, the function
\Sigma
is well-defined on
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r}}
only on the domain of the coordinates
(\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})
. Nonetheless, one can smoothly extend
\Sigma
to the whole
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r}}
and thus understand
\Sigma
as a
global
smooth function on each of the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r}}
(and thus on
\mathcal{M}
).

We define the
Kerr family of metrics
as the two-parameter family of Lorentzian metrics
g_{a,M}
on
\mathcal{M}
such that

\displaystyle g_{a,M}=

\displaystyle-\left(1-\frac{2\,M\,\bar{r}}{\Sigma}\right){d\bar{t}^{*}}^{2}+2\,d\bar{t}^{*}\,d\bar{r}+\Sigma\,{d\bar{\theta}}^{2}+\frac{(\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta\,a^{2}\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}\,{d\bar{\phi}^{*}}^{2}

(147)

\displaystyle-2\,a\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}\,d\bar{r}\,d\bar{\phi}^{*}-\frac{4\,a\,M\,\bar{r}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\bar{\theta}\,d\bar{t}^{*}\,d\bar{\phi}^{*}\,.

The metric
g_{a,M}
in the form (
147
) is manifestly
smooth
on
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
can be checked to be a
null
hypersurface relative to
g_{a,M}
. The vector fields
T
and
\Phi
are
Killing vector fields
of
g_{a,M}
.

The smooth Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
will be referred to as the
Kerr exterior manifold
. One can check that
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
solves the vacuum Einstein equations.

5.2

Kerr-star coordinates

For fixed
|a|<M
, we define the smooth scalar function
\iota_{a,M}=\iota_{a,M}(\bar{r})
such that

\iota_{a,M}:[r_{+},\infty)\rightarrow[0,1]

and

\iota(\bar{r})=\begin{cases}1\quad r_{+}\leq\bar{r}\leq 15\,M/8\\
0\quad\bar{r}\geq 9\,M/4\end{cases}\,,

(148)

with

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}-\iota\,\left(\frac{\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}-1\right)>0\,,

\displaystyle 2-\left(1-\frac{2\,M\,\bar{r}^{2}}{\Sigma}\right)\left(\frac{\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}-\iota\,\left(\frac{\bar{r}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}-1\right)\right)>0\,.

We define
Kerr-star coordinates

(t^{*},r,\theta,\phi^{*})

(149)

on
\mathcal{M}
such that

\displaystyle t^{*}(\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r})

\displaystyle=\bar{t}^{*}-\int_{r_{0}}^{\bar{r}}(1-\iota(r^{\prime}))\,\frac{{r^{\prime}}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta(r^{\prime})}+\iota(r^{\prime})\,dr^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle r(\bar{r})

\displaystyle=\bar{r}\,,

\displaystyle\theta(\bar{\theta})

\displaystyle=\bar{\theta}\,,

\displaystyle\phi^{*}(\bar{\phi}^{*},\bar{r})

\displaystyle=\bar{\phi}^{*}-\int_{r_{0}}^{\bar{r}}(1-\iota(r^{\prime}))\,\frac{a}{\Delta(r^{\prime})}\,dr^{\prime}\quad\text{mod}\,2\pi\,,

with
r_{0}=9\,M/4
. The coordinates (
149
) are
global
coordinates on
\mathcal{M}
,
including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
48
48

48

Modulo the usual degeneration of the angular coordinates
(\theta,\phi^{*})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
We define the two-spheres

\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r}:=\left\{t^{*},r\right\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\,.

The
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r}
-spheres are spheres of constant Kerr coordinate functions
(\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r})
. The vector field
\widetilde{\Phi}
thus remains a global, degenerate (at the poles) vector field on each of the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r}
spheres (and thus on
\mathcal{M}
). Locally, we have
\widetilde{\Phi}=\partial_{\phi^{*}}
.

In Kerr-star coordinates, the Kerr metric
g_{a,M}
reads

\displaystyle g_{a,M}=

\displaystyle-\left(1-\frac{2\,M\,r}{\Sigma}\right){dt^{*}}^{2}+\iota\,\frac{4\,M\,r}{\Sigma}\,dt^{*}\,dr-\frac{4\,a\,M\,r}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\theta\,dt^{*}\,d\phi^{*}

(150)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}+\iota^{2}\,\frac{-4\,M^{2}\,r^{2}+a^{2}(r^{2}+2\,M\,r+a^{2})\sin^{2}\theta-a^{4}\,\sin^{4}\theta}{\Delta\,\Sigma}\right)\,dr^{2}

\displaystyle-\iota\,\frac{2\,a(2\,M\,r+\Sigma)\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\,dr\,d\phi^{*}+\Sigma\,{d\theta}^{2}+\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-\Delta\,a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\theta\,{d\phi^{*}}^{2}\,.

The metric
g_{a,M}
in the form (
150
) is manifestly smooth on
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
49
49

49

Note that
g_{rr}=1+2Mr/\Sigma
for
r_{+}\leq r\leq 15\,M/8
.

For any
\tau^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
, the hypersurface

\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{*}}:=\left\{t^{*}=\tau^{*}\right\}

(151)

is a
spacelike
,
50
50

50

This can be checked and exploits the careful definition of the function
\iota(\bar{r})
.
asymptotically flat hypersurface intersecting
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(see Figure
6
). The level sets of
r_{+}<r<\infty
are
timelike
hypersurfaces. The level set
\left\{r=r_{+}\right\}=\mathcal{H}^{+}
is
null
and the formal hypersurface
\left\{t^{*}=\infty\right\}=\mathcal{I}^{+}
is
null
.

\mathcal{H}^{+}
\mathcal{I}^{+}
\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{*}}

Remark 5.1
.

The coordinates
(\bar{t}^{*},\bar{r},\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})
and
(t^{*},r,\theta,\phi^{*})
can be related to
Boyer–Lindquist coordinates

(t_{\textup{bl}},r_{\textup{bl}},\theta_{\textup{bl}},\phi_{\textup{bl}})

on
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{H}^{+}
by the coordinate transformations

\displaystyle\bar{t}^{*}(t_{\textup{bl}},r_{\textup{bl}})

\displaystyle=t_{\textup{bl}}+\int_{r_{0}}^{r_{\textup{bl}}}\frac{{r^{\prime}}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta(r^{\prime})}\,dr^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{r}(r_{\textup{bl}})

\displaystyle=r_{\textup{bl}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}(\theta_{\textup{bl}})

\displaystyle=\theta_{\textup{bl}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}^{*}(\phi_{\textup{bl}},r_{\textup{bl}})

\displaystyle=\phi_{\textup{bl}}+\int_{r_{0}}^{r_{\textup{bl}}}\frac{a}{\Delta(r^{\prime})}\,dr^{\prime}\quad\textup{mod}\,2\pi

and

\displaystyle t^{*}(t_{\textup{bl}},r_{\textup{bl}})

\displaystyle=t_{\textup{bl}}+\int_{r_{0}}^{r_{\textup{bl}}}\iota(r^{\prime})\,\left(\frac{{r^{\prime}}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta(r^{\prime})}-1\right)\,dr^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle r(r_{\textup{bl}})

\displaystyle=r_{\textup{bl}}\,,

\displaystyle\theta(\theta_{\textup{bl}})

\displaystyle=\theta_{\textup{bl}}\,,

\displaystyle\phi^{*}(\phi_{\textup{bl}},r_{\textup{bl}})

\displaystyle=\phi_{\textup{bl}}+\int_{r_{0}}^{r_{\textup{bl}}}\iota(r^{\prime})\,\frac{a}{\Delta(r^{\prime})}\,dr^{\prime}\quad\textup{mod}\,2\pi\,.

In Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, the Kerr metric
g_{a,M}
reads

g_{a,M}=-\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}\,(dt_{\textup{bl}}-a\,\sin^{2}\theta_{\textup{bl}}\,d\phi_{\textup{bl}})^{2}+\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}\,dr_{\textup{bl}}^{2}+\Sigma\,d\theta_{\textup{bl}}^{2}+\frac{\sin^{2}\theta_{\textup{bl}}}{\Sigma}\,(a\,dt_{\textup{bl}}-(r_{\textup{bl}}^{2}+a^{2})\,d\phi_{\textup{bl}})^{2}\,.

(152)

Remark 5.2
.

The identities

\displaystyle(t^{*},r,\theta,\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\equiv(\bar{t}^{*}-\bar{r},\bar{r},\bar{\theta},\bar{\phi}^{*})

\displaystyle\textup{for}\quad r_{+}\leq r\leq 15\,M/8\,,

\displaystyle(t^{*},r,\theta,\phi^{*})

\displaystyle\equiv(t_{\textup{bl}},r_{\textup{bl}},\theta_{\textup{bl}},\phi_{\textup{bl}})

\displaystyle\textup{for}\quad r\geq 9\,M/4

hold up to additive constants. The metric (
150
) takes the form (
152
) for
r\geq 9\,M/4
.

5.3

The algebraically special frame

We define the
algebraically special
null vector fields
51
51

51

Later in the section, we will comment on the algebraically special nature of the vector fields (
153
)-(
154
).

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\text{as}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\left(1+\iota\,\left(1-\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\right)\,\partial_{t^{*}}+\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{r}+\frac{a}{\Sigma}(1+\iota)\,\widetilde{\Phi}\,,

(153)

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\text{as}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}\,\left(1-\iota\,\left(1-\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\right)\,\partial_{t^{*}}-\partial_{r}+\frac{a}{\Delta}\,(1-\iota)\,\widetilde{\Phi}\,.

(154)

The vector fields
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
and
e_{3}^{\text{as}}
are
global
,
52
52

52

We recall that
\widetilde{\Phi}
is a global vector field on each of the two-spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r}
(locally,
\widetilde{\Phi}=\partial_{\phi^{*}}
). The vector field coefficients are smooth functions of the coordinates, and can be smoothly extended to global functions on the whole two-spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r}
.

regular
53
53

53

We note that the coordinate components of
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
and
e_{3}^{\text{as}}
are bounded functions of regular coordinates on
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
and
non-degenerate
vector fields on the whole manifold
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. One can check that the null vector field
e^{\text{as}}_{3}
is
geodesic
, i.e. the identity

\nabla_{e^{\text{as}}_{3}}e^{\text{as}}_{3}=0

holds on
\mathcal{M}
. We also define the induced
global
,
regular
horizontal distribution

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}:=\left\langle e^{\text{as}}_{3},e^{\text{as}}_{4}\right\rangle^{\perp}

on
\mathcal{M}
. One can complete the null vector fields (
153
)-(
154
) with a
local
orthonormal basis

(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})

of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
to form the
local
null frame

\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}=(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2},e^{\text{as}}_{3},e^{\text{as}}_{4})\,,

called the
algebraically special frame
of the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
.

Remark 5.3
.

In Boyer-Lindquist coordinates, the algebraically special frame vectors (
153
) and (
154
) read

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\textup{as}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{t_{\textup{bl}}}+\frac{\Delta}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{r_{\textup{bl}}}+\frac{a}{\Sigma}\,\partial_{\phi_{\textup{bl}}}\,,

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\textup{as}}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}\,\partial_{t_{\textup{bl}}}-\partial_{r_{\textup{bl}}}+\frac{a}{\Delta}\,\partial_{\phi_{\textup{bl}}}\,.

Crucially, the frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
is
non-integrable
for
|a|>0
, with
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
a
non-integrable
distribution. This fact can be easily checked by inspecting the connection coefficients of
g_{a,M}
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
presented in Section
5.4
and noting that both the second fundamental forms possess non-trivial antitraces. As a consequence, the induced connection
\nablasl_{a,M}
over the bundle of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors is
not
the Levi-Civita connection of the induced metric
\not{g}_{a,M}
(see Remark
4.34
).

We point out three additional properties of the frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
holding along the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
:

•

We have

e_{4}^{\text{as}}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}=2\,\frac{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma(r_{+})}\,\partial_{t^{*}}+2\,\frac{a}{\Sigma(r_{+})}\,\widetilde{\Phi}\,,

(155)

i.e. the vector field
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
is tangent to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and in the span of the Killing vector fields
\partial_{t^{*}}
and
\widetilde{\Phi}
.

•

We have

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\subset T\mathcal{H}^{+}\,,

i.e. the arbitrary local frame
(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
is tangent to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
54
54

54

One way to check this fact is to exhibit an explicit choice of
(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})
, e.g.
e_{1}^{\text{as}}=(a^{2}\,\sin(2\theta)/2\,\Sigma)\,\partial_{t^{*}}+(r/\Sigma)\,\partial_{\theta}+(a\,\cot\theta/\Sigma)\,\partial_{\phi^{*}}
and

e_{2}^{\text{as}}=(a\,r\,\sin\theta/\Sigma)\,\partial_{t^{*}}-(a\,\cos\theta/\Sigma)\,\partial_{\theta}+(r\,\csc\theta/\Sigma)\,\partial_{\phi^{*}}
. Any local frame vector field of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
is a linear combination of such
e_{1}^{\text{as}}
and
e_{2}^{\text{as}}
, which are themselves manifestly tangent to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
Thus, the vector fields

(e^{\text{as}}_{4},e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})

form a local basis of
T\mathcal{H}^{+}
(and, in particular, generate the
integrable
distribution
T\mathcal{H}^{+}\subset T\mathcal{M}
).

•

We have

\nablasl_{e_{4}^{\text{as}}}e_{A}^{\text{as}}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}=0

(156)

for any local frame
(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
.
55
55

55

One can check that
\nablasl_{e_{4}^{\text{as}}}e_{A}^{\text{as}}=0

on
\mathcal{M}
for the vector fields
(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})
in footnote
54
. In view of the identity (
155
), any linear combination of such vector fields satisfies the identity (
156
).

5.4

Induced metric, connection coefficients and curvature components

The induced metric, connection coefficients and curvature components of
g_{a,M}
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
are defined as
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors
(see Definition
4.9
with the identification
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\equiv\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
). Their explicit expression in coordinate form is given below.
56
56

56

We note that both their definition and explicit expression are independent of the choice of local vector fields
(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}})
. We also note that, for the induced metric and the covariant connection coefficients, we give the explicit expression of their
canonical extension
. See Remark
5.4
.
Since
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
is a global, regular distribution on
\mathcal{M}
, the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors are defined
globally
on
\mathcal{M}
and are
regular
quantities on the whole manifold
\mathcal{M}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

In Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, the induced metric
\not{g}^{\circ}_{a,M}
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
reads

\not{g}^{\circ}_{a,M}=\frac{a^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{bl}}\,dt_{\text{bl}}^{2}-2\,\frac{a\,(r_{\text{bl}}^{2}+a^{2})}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{bl}}\,dt_{\text{bl}}\,d\phi_{\text{bl}}+\Sigma\,d\theta_{\text{bl}}^{2}+\frac{(r_{\text{bl}}^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{bl}}\,d\phi_{\text{bl}}^{2}

(157)

and the connection coefficients
\eta_{a,M}^{\circ}
,
\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}
and
\zeta_{a,M}^{\circ}
of
g_{a,M}
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
read

\displaystyle\eta_{a,M}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=-\frac{a^{2}r_{\text{bl}}}{\Sigma^{2}}\,\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{bl}}\,dt_{\text{bl}}^{2}-\frac{a^{2}\sin(2\theta_{\text{bl}})}{2\,\Sigma}\,d\theta_{\text{bl}}+\frac{a\,r_{\text{bl}}(r_{\text{bl}}^{2}+a^{2})}{\Sigma^{2}}\,\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{bl}}\,d\phi_{\text{bl}}\,,

(158)

\displaystyle\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}r_{\text{bl}}}{\Sigma^{2}}\,\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{bl}}\,dt_{\text{bl}}^{2}-\frac{a^{2}\sin(2\theta_{\text{bl}})}{2\,\Sigma}\,d\theta_{\text{bl}}-\frac{a\,r_{\text{bl}}(r_{\text{bl}}^{2}+a^{2})}{\Sigma^{2}}\,\sin^{2}\theta_{\text{bl}}\,d\phi_{\text{bl}}\,,

(159)

\displaystyle\zeta_{a,M}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=-\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}-\frac{\Sigma}{r_{\text{bl}}^{2}+a^{2}}\,\nablasl^{\circ}\left(\frac{r_{\text{bl}}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}\right)\,.

(160)

In Kerr-star coordinates, the remaining connection coefficients of
g_{a,M}
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
read

\displaystyle{\chih}_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle{\chibh}_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)_{a,M}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,a\,\Delta\cos\theta}{\Sigma^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)_{a,M}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,a\cos\theta}{\Sigma}\,,

\displaystyle(\text{tr}\chi)_{a,M}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,r\,\Delta}{\Sigma^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle(\text{tr}\chib)_{a,M}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\,r}{\Sigma}

and

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}_{a,M}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\left(a^{2}(M-r)\cos^{2}\theta+a^{2}r-Mr^{2}\right)}{\Sigma^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\omegabh_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\yb_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,.

The curvature components of
g_{a,M}
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
read

\displaystyle\alpha_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\alphab_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\betab_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\rho_{a,M}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,M\,r\left(3\,a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta-r^{2}\right)}{\Sigma^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{a,M}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,a\,M\cos\theta\left(3\,r^{2}-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\right)}{\Sigma^{3}}\,.

We note, in particular, the following identities for the connection coefficients
57
57

57

The second of the identities below is not immediately manifest from the identities (
158
) and (
160
), but can be easily checked by evaluating the identity relative to the vector fields
(e^{\text{as}}_{1},e^{\text{as}}_{2})
in footnote
54
. We also note that these identities are geometric, and thus do not depend on our choice of coordinates to write the connection coefficients explicitly.

\displaystyle\omegabh_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\eta_{a,M}-\zeta_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{a,M}=\yb_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle{\chih}_{a,M}={\chibh}_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,.

The identities for the curvature components

\displaystyle\alpha_{a,M}=\alphab_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{a,M}=\betab_{a,M}

\displaystyle=0

identify the null frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
as the algebraically special frame of the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
.

Remark 5.4
.

In the identities (
157
), (
158
), (
159
) and (
160
), we expressed the canonical extension (in the sense of Definition
4.17
, with the identification
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}\equiv\mathcal{M}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\equiv\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}}
) of the induced metric
\not{g}_{a,M}
and connection coefficients
\eta_{a,M}
,
\etab_{a,M}
and
\zeta_{a,M}
, the latter being the actual
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}}
tensors. The non-integrability of the distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}}
does not allow an explicit expression of
\not{g}_{a,M}
,
\eta_{a,M}
,
\etab_{a,M}
and
\zeta_{a,M}
in coordinate form, in that
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}}
does not admit a coordinate co-basis.

Remark 5.5
.

The connection coefficients and curvature components of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}
solve the nonlinear system of null structure and Bianchi equations of Section
4.7
.

On the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, the identities (
155
) and (
156
) imply

\displaystyle\nablasl_{4}\Gamma_{a,M}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\nablasl_{4}\psi_{a,M}|_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}

\displaystyle=0

for any connection coefficient
\Gamma_{a,M}
and curvature component
\psi_{a,M}
of
g_{a,M}
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
.

Remark 5.6
.

In the definition of the algebraically special frame
\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}
, we made a choice of scaling for the null vector fields. Our choice of scaling for
e_{3}^{\textup{as}}
and
e_{4}^{\textup{as}}
in (
153
)-(
154
) is
uniquely
determined by the requirement that the frame extends
regularly
to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and the null vector
e_{3}^{\textup{as}}
is
geodesic
. Alternative scalings that keep the frame regular on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
are of course possible, a notable one being the one setting
\nablasl\omegah_{a,M}=0
and
\etab_{a,M}+\zeta_{a,M}=0
, but now
\omegabh_{a,M}\neq 0
.

5.5

Commutation formulae

We present the commutation formulae for
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors. The formulae are obtained by specialising the general commutation formulae of Section
4.6.3
, with the identification
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}},\boldsymbol{g})\equiv(\mathcal{M}^{*},g_{a,M})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}}\equiv\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
.

For any
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-tensor
\varsigma
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
two-tensor
\theta
, we have the following commutation formulae

\displaystyle[\nablasl_{4},\nablasl]\,\varsigma=

\displaystyle\,-\chi_{a,M}\times(\nablasl\varsigma)+(\etab_{a,M}+\zeta_{a,M})\otimes(\nablasl_{4}\varsigma)

(161)

\displaystyle+(\etab_{a,M},\varsigma)\,\chi_{a,M}-(\chi^{\sharp_{2}}_{a,M}\cdot\varsigma)\otimes\etab_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle[\nablasl_{3},\nablasl]\,\varsigma=

\displaystyle\,-\chib_{a,M}\times(\nablasl\varsigma)

(162)

\displaystyle+(\eta_{a,M},\varsigma)\,\chib_{a,M}-(\chib^{\sharp_{2}}_{a,M}\cdot\,\varsigma)\otimes\eta_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle[\nablasl_{3},\nablasl_{4}]\,\varsigma=

\displaystyle\,\hat{\omega}_{a,M}\,(\nablasl_{3}\varsigma)+2\,(\nablasl\varsigma)^{\sharp_{1}}\cdot(\eta_{a,M}-\etab_{a,M})

(163)

\displaystyle+2\,(\eta_{a,M},\varsigma)\,\etab_{a,M}-2\,(\etab_{a,M},\varsigma)\,\eta_{a,M}+2\,\sigma_{a,M}({}^{\star}\varsigma)

and

\displaystyle([\nablasl_{4},\nablasl]\,\theta)_{ABC}=

\displaystyle\,-\chi^{\sharp_{2}}_{a,M}{}_{A}^{D}(\nablasl_{D}\theta)_{BC}+(\etab_{a,M}+\zeta_{a,M})_{A}(\nablasl_{4}\theta)_{BC}

(164)

\displaystyle+\chi_{a,M}{}_{AB}\etab_{a,M}^{D}\theta_{DC}-\etab_{a,M}{}_{B}\chi_{a,M}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}\theta_{DC}

\displaystyle+\chi_{a,M}{}_{AC}\etab_{a,M}^{D}\theta_{BD}-\etab_{a,M}{}_{C}\chi_{a,M}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}\theta_{BD}\,,

\displaystyle([\nablasl_{3},\nablasl]\theta)_{ABC}=

\displaystyle\,-\chib_{a,M}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}(\nablasl_{D}\theta)_{BC}

(165)

\displaystyle+\chib_{a,M}{}_{AB}\eta_{a,M}^{D}\theta_{DC}-\eta_{a,M}{}_{B}\chib^{\sharp_{2}}_{a,M}{}_{A}^{D}\theta_{DC}

\displaystyle+\chib_{a,M}{}_{AC}\eta_{a,M}^{D}\theta_{BD}-\eta_{a,M}{}_{C}\chib_{a,M}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{D}\theta_{BD}\,,

\displaystyle([\nablasl_{3},\nablasl_{4}]\,\theta)_{AB}=

\displaystyle\,\hat{\omega}_{a,M}\,(\nablasl_{3}\theta)_{AB}+2\,{(\nablasl\theta)^{\sharp_{1}}}{}^{C}_{AB}(\eta_{a,M}-\etab_{a,M})_{C}

(166)

\displaystyle+2\,\eta_{a,M}^{C}\theta_{CB}\,\etab_{a,M}{}_{A}-2\,\etab_{a,M}^{C}\theta_{CB}\,\eta_{a,M}{}_{A}+2\,\sigma_{a,M}\,\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{A}^{C}\theta_{CB}

\displaystyle+2\,\eta_{a,M}^{C}\theta_{AC}\,\etab_{a,M}{}_{B}-2\,\etab_{a,M}^{C}\theta_{AC}\,\eta_{a,M}{}_{B}+2\,\sigma_{a,M}\,\not{\varepsilon}^{\sharp_{2}}{}_{B}^{C}\theta_{AC}\,.

5.6

Outgoing principal differentiable structures

In this section, we introduce the
star-normalised outgoing principal differentiable structure
on
\mathcal{M}
, which will later serve as the background differentiable structure when formulating nonlinear perturbations of the Kerr exterior manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
(see Section
6.1
).

We start with a preliminary definition and a proposition.

Definition 5.7
.

An
outgoing principal differentiable structure
of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
is a (local) differentiable structures
(x^{1},x^{2},\vartheta^{A})
on
\mathcal{M}
, with
(\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, such that

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\textup{as}}(x^{1})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\textup{as}}(\vartheta^{A})

\displaystyle=0\,.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
(x^{1},x^{2},\vartheta^{A})
be an outgoing principal differentiable structure of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
. Then, we have the identities

\displaystyle(g_{a,M})_{x^{2}x^{2}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{x^{2}}(g_{a,M})_{x^{2}\mu}+\mathfrak{h}_{a,M}\,(g_{a,M})_{x^{2}\mu}

\displaystyle=0

for some smooth scalar function
\mathfrak{h}_{a,M}
on
\mathcal{M}
, with
x^{\mu}=(x^{1},\vartheta^{A})
.

Proof.

We have
e_{4}^{\text{as}}=h_{a,M}\partial_{x^{2}}
for some smooth scalar function
h_{a,M}
on
\mathcal{M}
. The first identity follows immediately from
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
being a null vector field. We compute

\displaystyle\nabla_{e_{4}^{\text{as}}}(g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}},\partial_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=\omegah_{a,M}\,g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}},\partial_{\mu})+h^{2}_{a,M}\,g_{a,M}(\partial_{x^{2}},\nabla_{x^{2}}\partial_{\mu})

\displaystyle=\omegah_{a,M}\,g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}},\partial_{\mu})+h^{2}_{a,M}\,g_{a,M}(\partial_{x^{2}},\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{x^{2}})

\displaystyle=\omegah_{a,M}\,g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}},\partial_{\mu})+\frac{1}{2}\,h^{2}_{a,M}\,\partial_{\mu}((g_{a,M})_{x^{2}\,x^{2}})

\displaystyle=\omegah_{a,M}\,g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}},\partial_{\mu})

and then note that the left hand side can be written as follows

\displaystyle\nabla_{e_{4}^{\text{as}}}(g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}},\partial_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{e_{4}^{\text{as}}}h_{a,M})\,g_{a,M}(\partial_{x^{2}},\partial_{\mu})+h_{a,M}\nabla_{e_{4}^{\text{as}}}(g_{a,M}(\partial_{x^{2}},\partial_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=h_{a,M}\,(\partial_{x^{2}}h_{a,M})g_{a,M}(\partial_{x^{2}},\partial_{\mu})+h^{2}_{a,M}\,\partial_{x^{2}}(g_{a,M}(\partial_{x^{2}},\partial_{\mu}))\,.

We conclude by setting
\mathfrak{h}_{a,M}=h^{-1}_{a,M}(\partial_{x^{2}}h_{a,M}-\omegah_{a,M})
.

∎

5.6.1

Star-normalised outgoing principal differentiable structure

In this section, we define the
star-normalised
outgoing principal differentiable structure of
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
.

We start by considering the manifold
\mathcal{M}
with Kerr-star differentiable structure
(t^{*},r,\theta,\phi^{*})
and the hypersurface
\mathcal{S}_{0}\subset\mathcal{M}
(recall the definition (
151
) and the related Figure
6
). The hypersurface
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is foliated by the Kerr-star two-spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{0,r}
. For any point
p\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, we define the smooth curve

\displaystyle\gamma_{p}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow J^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{0})\cap\mathcal{M}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{p}(\lambda)=(t^{*}(\lambda),r(\lambda),\theta(\lambda),\phi^{*}(\lambda))

such that
\gamma_{p}(0)=p
, with
(t^{*}(0),r(0),\theta(0),\phi^{*}(0))=(0,r(p),\theta(p),\phi^{*}(p))
, and

\displaystyle\frac{d\gamma_{p}^{t^{*}}}{d\lambda}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma_{p}^{r}{}^{2}(\lambda)+a^{2}}{\Sigma(\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda),\gamma_{p}^{\theta}(\lambda))}\,\left(1+\iota(\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda))\,\left(1-\frac{\Delta(\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda))}{\gamma_{p}^{r}{}^{2}(\lambda)+a^{2}}\right)\right)\,,

(167)

\displaystyle\frac{d\gamma_{p}^{r}}{d\lambda}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta(\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda))}{\Sigma(\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda),\gamma_{p}^{\theta}(\lambda))}\,,

(168)

\displaystyle\frac{d\gamma_{p}^{\theta}}{d\lambda}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(169)

\displaystyle\frac{d\gamma_{p}^{\phi^{*}}}{d\lambda}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\Sigma(\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda),\gamma_{p}^{\theta}(\lambda))}(1+\iota(\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda)))

(170)

for all
\lambda\geq 0
, with
\gamma_{p}^{\mu}(\lambda):=x^{\mu}(\gamma_{p}(\lambda))
.
58
58

58

The curves
\gamma_{p}
are well-defined in view of the later Proposition
5.9
.
We note that

\displaystyle\gamma_{p}^{t^{*}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\in[0,\infty)\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\in[r_{+},\infty)\,,

\displaystyle(\gamma_{p}^{\theta}(\lambda),\gamma_{p}^{\phi^{*}}(\lambda)))

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{S}^{2}

for all
\lambda\geq 0
. It is an immediate check that the curves
\gamma_{p}
are
integral curves
of
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
.

Importantly, the point
p\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
can be chosen to coincide with either of the poles of a
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{0,r}
-sphere. Although the angular coordinates
(\theta,\phi^{*})
are not defined at the poles (and thus the functions
\gamma_{p}^{\theta}(0)
and
\gamma_{p}^{\phi^{*}}(0)
in the system (
167
)-(
170
) are not well-defined at the poles), one can smoothly extend all the functions of the angular coordinates on the right hand side of (
167
)-(
170
) to the poles.
59
59

59

We note that being able to extend smoothly to the poles the right hand sides of (
167
)-(
170
) corresponds to the fact that
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
is global on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{0,r}
-spheres.

The following propositions prove that the curves
\gamma(\lambda)
induce a smooth
global
flow
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda\geq 0}
on
J^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{0})\cap\mathcal{M}
.

Proposition 5.9
.

For any
p\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, the curve
\gamma_{p}
is unique and future complete. Furthermore, for any distinct points
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, we have
\gamma_{p_{1}}(\lambda)\neq\gamma_{p_{2}}(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\geq 0
.

Proof.

The four ODEs (
167
)-(
170
) form an autonomous system of ODEs of the form

\frac{d\gamma_{p}^{\mu}}{d\lambda}(\lambda)=F^{\mu}(\gamma_{p}^{\nu}(\lambda))\,,

(171)

where each
F^{\mu}
is a
bounded
smooth function of
\gamma_{p}^{\nu}(\lambda)=(\gamma_{p}^{t^{*}}(\lambda),\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda),\gamma_{p}^{\theta}(\lambda),\gamma_{p}^{\phi^{*}}(\lambda))
on
\mathcal{M}
. We note that the right hand side of the ODEs (
167
) and (
168
) are
non-negative
smooth functions on
\mathcal{M}
, and thus
\gamma_{p}^{t^{*}}(\lambda)
and
\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda)
are
non-decreasing
functions of
\lambda
for all
\lambda\geq 0
. By standard ODE theory, there exists a unique solution
\gamma_{p}(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\geq 0
. Since
\gamma_{p}^{t^{*}}(\lambda)
and
\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda)
are non-decreasing functions of
\lambda
, the fact that the domains of
\gamma_{p}^{t^{*}}(\lambda)
and
\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda)
are lower bounded poses no issues for the completeness of
\gamma_{p}
. The global uniqueness of
\gamma_{p}
immediately implies the second part of the proposition.

∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{S}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Then, we have
\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda)=r_{+}
for all
\lambda\geq 0
.

Proof.

One can directly verify that the smooth curve

\gamma_{p}(\lambda)=\left(2\,\frac{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma(r_{+},\theta(p))}\,\lambda\,,\,r_{+}\,,\,\theta(p)\,,\,\phi^{*}(p)+2\,\frac{a}{\Sigma(r_{+},\theta(p))}\,\lambda\right)

solves the system of ODEs (
167
)-(
170
) for all
\lambda\geq 0
.

∎

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
such that
r(p)>r_{+}
. Then, we have
\gamma_{p}^{t^{*}}(\lambda)\rightarrow\infty
and
\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda)\rightarrow\infty
as
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

We note that the right hand side of the ODEs (
167
) and (
168
) are positive smooth functions on
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and thus
\gamma_{p}^{t^{*}}(\lambda)
and
\gamma_{p}^{r}(\lambda)
are increasing functions of
\lambda
for all
\lambda\geq 0
. One concludes by the future completeness of
\gamma_{p}(\lambda)
from Proposition
5.9
.

∎

Remark 5.12
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
such that
r(p)>r_{+}
. Then, the curve
\gamma_{p}
intersects future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
. This follows immediately from Proposition
5.11
.

We now consider the hypersurface

\mathcal{S}_{0}^{*}:=\mathcal{S}_{0}\cap\left\{r<r_{+}+1\right\}

and the spacetime region
\mathcal{M}^{*}\subset\mathcal{M}
such that

\mathcal{M}^{*}:=\mathcal{F}_{\lambda\geq 0}(\mathcal{S}_{0}^{*})\,.

(172)

See the earlier Figure
. With a slight abuse of notation, we will denote
\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}
by
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. We observe that, by definition, the region
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is the spacetime region of all points
q\in J^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{0})\cap\mathcal{M}
such that there exists an integral curve
\gamma_{p}
of
e_{4}^{\text{as}}
connecting
p
and
q
, with
p\in\mathcal{S}_{0}^{*}
. For later convenience, we denote such a curve by
\gamma_{p,q}
.

We define the smooth scalar function

s:\mathcal{M}^{*}\rightarrow[r_{+},r_{+}+1)

such that

s(q)=r(\gamma_{p,q}(0))

for any
q\in\mathcal{M}^{*}
. Note that
s(q)=r(q)
for any
q\in\mathcal{S}_{0}^{*}
. We also note that points
q\in\mathcal{M}^{*}
with associated
\gamma_{p,q}
such that
p
coincides with the pole of a
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{0,r}
sphere are allowed. In view of Propositions
5.9
and
5.11
, the function
s
is a
globally
well-defined
coordinate
function on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. One can thus conclude that the intersection of the level sets of
t^{*}
and
s
are (complete) two-spheres

\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},s}:=\left\{t^{*},s\right\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\,.

We note that
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{0,s}\equiv\mathbb{S}^{2}_{0,r}
, the latter understood as Kerr-star two-spheres.

Let
(\theta^{1}_{0},\theta^{2}_{0})
be arbitrary angular coordinates on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{0,s}
-spheres. For each
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},s}
-sphere, we define angular coordinates

(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}

such that

(\theta^{1}(q),\theta^{2}(q))=(\theta^{1}_{0}(\gamma_{p,q}(0)),\theta^{2}_{0}(\gamma_{p,q}(0)))

for any
q\in\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},s}
. The coordinates
(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})
only cover a subset of each
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},s}
-sphere.

Finally, we define the smooth scalar function

\underline{s}:\mathcal{M}^{*}\rightarrow[0,\infty)

such that

\underline{s}\equiv t^{*}

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and obtain the
star-normalised
outgoing principal differentiable structure

(s,\underline{s},\theta^{A})\,.

(173)

We emphasise that the coordinates
(s,\underline{s})
are
global
and
regular
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, whereas the angular coordinates
(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})
are regular but only cover a subset of the two-spheres

\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}:=\left\{s,\underline{s}\right\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\,.

We also note that the coordinates (
173
) depend on the Kerr parameters
a
and
M
.

It is an immediate check that the differentiable structure (
173
