) is an outgoing principal differentiable structure in the sense of Definition
5.7
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\text{as}}(s)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\text{as}}(\theta^{A})

\displaystyle=0

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

Let

\displaystyle k_{a,M}

\displaystyle:=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}\,\left(1+\iota\,\left(1-\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\right)\right)\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}

\displaystyle:=k_{a,M}^{-1}\,(\partial_{\underline{s}}k_{a,M}-\omegah_{a,M})

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, with
k_{a,M}

nowhere
vanishing on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. Relative to the differentiable structure (
173
), the Kerr metric takes the form

g_{a,M}=\mathfrak{a}\,ds^{2}+2\,\Omega^{2}\,ds\,d\underline{s}+2\,b_{\theta^{A}}\,ds\,d\theta^{A}+2\,\underline{b}_{\theta^{A}}\,d\underline{s}\,d\theta^{A}+\,\gamma_{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}\,d\theta^{A}d\theta^{B}

(174)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, with

\displaystyle\partial_{\underline{s}}\,\Omega^{2}+\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}\,\Omega^{2}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{\underline{s}}\,\underline{b}_{\theta^{A}}+\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}\,\underline{b}_{\theta^{A}}

\displaystyle=0

(175)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(see Proposition
5.8
), and the frame vector fields
(e_{4}^{\text{as}},e_{3}^{\text{as}})
take the form

\displaystyle e_{4}^{\text{as}}

\displaystyle=k_{a,M}\,\partial_{\underline{s}}\,,

\displaystyle e_{3}^{\text{as}}

\displaystyle=\underline{j}_{a,M}\,\partial_{s}+\underline{k}_{a,M}\,\partial_{\underline{s}}+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}_{a,M}\,\partial_{\theta^{A}}

(176)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. The quantities
\mathfrak{a}
,
\Omega^{2}
,
\underline{j}_{a,M}
and
\underline{k}_{a,M}
are smooth scalar functions on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, the quantities
b
and
\underline{b}
are one-tensors on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-spheres, the quantity
\gamma
is a symmetric two-tensor on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-spheres and the quantity
\underline{\Lambda}_{a,M}
is a vector field tangent to the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-spheres. All quantities are regular on the whole
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The Kerr metric coefficients in (
174
) and frame coefficients of
e_{3}^{\text{as}}
in (
176
) cannot be made explicit.

We conclude with some remarks.

Remark 5.13
.

In our problem, the ambient manifold
\mathcal{M}^{*}
will act as the fixed background manifold and the differentiable structure (
173
) will serve as the fixed differentiable structure on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. See Section
6.1
.

Remark 5.14
.

For what follows, it is important for us that the coordinates
(s,\theta^{A})
are transported along the integral curves of the algebraically special null vector
e_{4}^{\textup{as}}
and are regular on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. In this sense, the choice of these coordinates is somewhat rigid. On the other hand, our particular choice of the ingoing coordinate
\underline{s}
will play no fundamental role, and may be replaced. Note also that one can rescale the coordinate
\underline{s}
by a regular (on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
) weight so that
e_{4}^{\textup{as}}
becomes a coordinate vector field.

5.6.2

The
(s,\underline{s})
-foliation

The coordinates
(s,\underline{s})
induce a
global
foliation of
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. The level sets of
r_{+}<s<r_{+}+1
are
timelike
hypersurfaces intersecting the hypersurface
\mathcal{S}_{0}
and future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(see Proposition
5.11
). Such hypersurfaces are
asymptotically null
as they approach
\mathcal{I}^{+}
. In view of Proposition
5.10
, the event horizon corresponds to the level set

\mathcal{H}^{+}=\left\{s=r_{+}\right\}

and is
null
. The properties of the level sets of
\underline{s}
coincide with those of the level sets
\mathcal{S}_{\tau^{*}}
of the Kerr-star time coordinate
t^{*}
discussed in Section
5.2
. The reader should refer back to Figure
1
.

5.7

The
|a|=0
case

For
|a|=0
, we refer to
(\mathcal{M},g_{0,M})
as the
Schwarzschild exterior manifold
. The following properties hold:

•

The algebraically special frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
is integrable and such that

(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})_{p}=T_{p}\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s(p),\underline{s}(p)}

for any
p\in\mathcal{M}
.

•

The induced metric, connection coefficients and curvature components of
g_{0,M}
relative to
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
are
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-tensors in the sense of
[
14
,
13
]
and such that

\displaystyle(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi)_{0,M}=(\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib)_{0,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\eta_{0,M}=\etab_{0,M}=\zeta_{0,M}

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{0,M}

\displaystyle=0

on
\mathcal{M}
.

•

The outgoing coordinate
s
is a regular null coordinate, whereas the ingoing coordinate
\underline{s}
is a spacelike coordinate such that

\underline{s}=t_{\text{bl}}+2M\log(r_{\text{bl}}-2M)

for
2M\leq r_{\text{bl}}\leq 15M/8
and coincides with the Schwarzschild time coordinate
t_{\text{bl}}
for
r_{\text{bl}}\geq 9M/4
(see already Figure
2
).

6

Nonlinear perturbations of the Kerr exterior manifold

In this section, we formulate nonlinear perturbations of the Kerr exterior manifold. We start by introducing a one-parameter family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
on the fixed ambient manifold
\mathcal{M}^{*}
in Section
6.1
. In doing that, we will make a choice of gauge for the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
. To the family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, we associate a one-parameter family of frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
in Section
6.3
. Sections
6.2
and
6.4
will prove that our choices of gauge and frames are legitimate.

Sections
6.2
and
6.4
are independent modules and can be skipped by the reader without affecting the overall logic of the paper.

6.1

One-parameter family of metrics

We choose real parameters
a
and
M
, with
|a|<M
. We fix the manifold-with-boundary
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, which we identify with the subset of the Kerr ambient manifold (
172
) with star-normalised outgoing principal differentiable structure
(s,\underline{s},\theta^{A})
.
60
60

60

We note that the fixed differentiable structure is global (modulo the usual degeneration on two-spheres) and regular on the whole ambient manifold
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
We introduce the fixed smooth scalar functions
\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}
,
k_{a,M}
and
\omegah_{a,M}
, the fixed one-tensor
\etab^{\circ}_{a,M}
, the fixed vector fields
(e_{4}^{\text{as}},e_{3}^{\text{as}})
and the fixed distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
of the fixed coordinates on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

We prescribe a one-parameter family of smooth, non-degenerate Lorentzian metrics

\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon):=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\epsilon)\,ds^{2}+2\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)\,ds\,d\underline{s}+2\,\boldsymbol{b}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,ds\,d\theta^{A}+2\,\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,d\underline{s}\,d\theta^{A}+\,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)\,d\theta^{A}d\theta^{B}

(177)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, with

\displaystyle\partial_{\underline{s}}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{\underline{s}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0

(178)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for all
\epsilon
. For each
\epsilon
, the metric quantities
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)
are smooth scalar functions on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, the metric quantities
\boldsymbol{b}(\epsilon)
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}(\epsilon)
are one-tensors on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-spheres and the metric quantity
\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\epsilon)
is a symmetric two-tensor on the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-spheres. All quantities are regular on the whole
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. We assume that
\boldsymbol{g}(0)\equiv g_{a,M}
with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{a}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(0)

\displaystyle=\Omega^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{b}_{\theta^{A}}(0)

\displaystyle=b_{\theta^{A}}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(0)

\displaystyle=\underline{b}_{\theta^{A}}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}(0)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}\,,

i.e. the metric coefficients of
\boldsymbol{g}(0)
relative to the fixed differentiable structure coincide with the Kerr metric coefficients relative to the star-normalised, outgoing principal differentiable structure of Kerr from (
174
) in Section
5.6
. We further assume that

\sum_{0\leq|I|\leq 10}|\partial^{I}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{g}}(\epsilon)-\partial^{I}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{g}}(0)|\leq\epsilon

(179)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, with

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{g}}(\epsilon)=\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{b}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)\right\}

and
I
a multi-index for partial derivatives relative to the fixed differentiable structure.

We remark that there exists a sufficiently small constant
\epsilon_{0}>0
(which is chosen once and for all in Section
6.2
), depending only on the fixed background parameters
a
and
M
, such that, for all
0\leq|\epsilon|\leq\epsilon_{0}
, the form (
177
) of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, together with the additional identities (
178
), correspond to a choice of gauge for the family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, called
outgoing principal gauge
. See Section
6.2
.

From now on, without loss of generality,
we restrict to
0\leq|\epsilon|\leq\epsilon_{0}
. When we write
for all
\epsilon
, this restriction is implicitly assumed.

Remark 6.1
.

Since the fixed differentiable structure is regular on the whole ambient manifold
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
), formulating the smoothness of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
does not require the slightly more technical characterisation from Section 5.1.1 of
[
17
]
.

6.2

Outgoing principal gauge

In this section, we prove the following proposition. Proposition
6.2
implies that the form (
177
) of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
introduced in Section
6.1
, together with the additional identities (
178
), correspond to a choice of gauge for the family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
provided that
|\epsilon|
is sufficiently small. We refer to such a gauge as an
outgoing principal gauge
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
a
and
M
be real parameters, with
|a|<M
. Let
(\mathcal{M},g_{a,M})
be the Kerr exterior manifold. Then, there exists a sufficiently small constant
\epsilon_{0}>0
, depending only on
a
and
M
, such that the following statement holds true.

Consider any one-parameter family of smooth, non-degenerate Lorentzian metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{M}
, with
0\leq|\epsilon|\leq\epsilon_{0}
and such that
\boldsymbol{g}(0)\equiv g_{a,M}
and

\sum_{0\leq|I|\leq 10}|\partial^{I}\boldsymbol{g}_{\mu\nu}(\epsilon)-\partial^{I}g_{a,M}{}_{\mu\nu}|\leq\epsilon

(180)

on
\mathcal{M}
, where the metric components in (
180
) are relative to the Kerr-star differentiable structure on
\mathcal{M}
. For any
p\in\mathcal{M}
, there exist an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{R}_{p}\subset\mathcal{M}
of
p
and a one-parameter family of differentiable structures

(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\epsilon}^{A})

on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
, with
(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon})
global coordinates on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
and
(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\epsilon}^{1},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\epsilon}^{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
subject to the usual degeneration on two-spheres, such that

•

Curves of constant
(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\epsilon}^{A})
are null geodesics of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)

For
0\leq|\epsilon|\leq\epsilon_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}\,\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}\mu}(\epsilon)+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{h}}^{\epsilon}_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}\mu}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0

(181)

on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
, with
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{h}}^{\epsilon}_{a,M}
a one-parameter family of smooth scalar functions such that

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{h}}^{\epsilon}_{a,M}=h_{a,M}^{-1}(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}h_{a,M}-\omegah_{a,M})

for some smooth, nowhere vanishing scalar function
h_{a,M}
independent of
\epsilon
and
x^{\mu}=(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\epsilon}^{A})
.

•

Curves of constant
(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{0}^{A})
are outgoing, principal null geodesics of
\boldsymbol{g}(0)

For
\epsilon=0
, the coordinates

(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{0},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{0},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{0}^{A})

are an outgoing principal differentiable structure of
\boldsymbol{g}(0)
on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
.

The gauge construction builds upon the definition of an auxiliary one-parameter family of local, null (relative to
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
) vector fields
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)
. The preliminary definition of this family is presented in Section
6.2.1
, whereas the gauge construction proper is carried out in Section
6.2.2
. In Section
6.2.3
, we prove the gauge identities (
181
).

Remark 6.3
.

The assumption (
180
) is needed in the present section. In particular, it is exploited in the initial step of the construction of Section
6.2.1
.

6.2.1

An auxiliary one-parameter family of vector fields

We consider the set-up stated in Proposition
6.2
.

In view of the assumption (
180
), there exists a real constant
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that, for all
0\leq|\epsilon|\leq\epsilon_{0}
, the inequalities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{1}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle>0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(e_{2}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle>0

(182)

hold
everywhere
on
\mathcal{M}
for any choice of local frame
(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}})
of the fixed distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
on
\mathcal{M}
. See the related Remark
6.3
and note that, for the existence of the constant
\epsilon_{0}
, a
C^{0}
-version of the assumption (
180
) would suffice. The inequalities (
182
) imply that the distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
is everywhere
spacelike

relative to
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{M}
.
61
61

61

Meaning that, at each point
p\in\mathcal{M}
,
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}},\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)|_{\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}})_{p}
is diffeomorphic to the two-dimensional Euclidean space
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\delta)_{p}
.
For the rest of the section, we restrict to
0\leq|\epsilon|\leq\epsilon_{0}
.

We define the distribution

\boldsymbol{V}(\epsilon):=(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})^{\perp_{\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)}}

(183)

on
\mathcal{M}
,
62
62

62

Recall that both
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
and
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
are global on
\mathcal{M}
, and thus
\boldsymbol{V}(\epsilon)
is globally well-defined on
\mathcal{M}
.
i.e. the bundle of all vector fields
\boldsymbol{X}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{X}(\epsilon),e_{A}^{\text{as}})=0
for any local frame
(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}})
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
. For
any

p\in\mathcal{M}
, by Sylvester’s law of inertia,
63
63

63

By definition, we have
\boldsymbol{V}_{p}(\epsilon)\oplus(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})_{p}=T_{p}\mathcal{M}
. Since the metric
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
has Lorentzian signature on
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
and is positive definite on
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})_{p}
, then
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
has Lorentzian signature on
\boldsymbol{V}_{p}(\epsilon)
.
we have that
(\boldsymbol{V}(\epsilon),\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)|_{\boldsymbol{V}(\epsilon)})_{p}
is diffeomorphic to the two-dimensional Minkowski space
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\eta)_{p}
. Thus, there exist exactly two distinct
null
(relative to
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
) directions in
\boldsymbol{V}_{p}(\epsilon)
. We choose
(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon))_{p}
as the unique vector at
p
such that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon))_{p}

\displaystyle\in\boldsymbol{V}_{p}(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}_{p}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}_{p}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),e_{3}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=-2\,.

Note that, by the definition (
183
), we have

\boldsymbol{g}_{p}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),e_{A}^{\text{as}})=0

for any local frame
(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}})
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
.

From the chosen point
p\in\mathcal{M}
, we propagate
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)

locally
such that

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)}\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)=\omegah_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)\,,

(184)

with
\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\epsilon)
the Levi-Civita connection of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
and
\omegah_{a,M}=\omegah_{a,M}(r,\theta)
smooth scalar function of the Kerr-star coordinates on
\mathcal{M}
. The local existence and uniqueness of
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)
away from
p
is a classical local existence and uniqueness result from ODE theory. It is an easy check that, propagating via (
184
), one obtains a local vector field
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)
which remains
null
(relative to
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
) along its integral curve emanating from
p
.

Let

\mathcal{B}_{p}:=\bigcup_{r(p)-\delta<r<r(p)+\delta}\,\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*}(p),r}\,,

with sufficiently small real constant
\delta>0
. The construction of the local vector field
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)
can be repeated starting from
any
64
64

64

In particular, the point
q\in\mathcal{B}_{p}
can coincide with either of the poles of a
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{t^{*},r}
-sphere foliating
\mathcal{B}_{p}
.
point
q\in\mathcal{B}_{p}
.
65
65

65

For
\delta
sufficiently small, one can check that, for any
q\in\mathcal{B}_{p}
, the vector
(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon))_{q}
is not tangent to
\mathcal{B}_{p}
.
The assumption (
180
) guarantees that the construction yields a null vector field
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)
on an open neighbourhood
\mathcal{R}_{p}\subset\mathcal{M}
of
p
. We note that, for this step of the construction, a
C^{0}
-version of the assumption (
180
) would
not
suffice. The integral curves of
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)
induce a flow on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
.

Remark 6.4
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{M}
. For
\epsilon=0
, we have
\boldsymbol{V}(0)\equiv\left\langle e_{3}^{\textup{as}},e_{4}^{\textup{as}}\right\rangle
and

\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\textup{aux}}(0)\equiv e_{4}^{\textup{as}}

(185)

on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
. Indeed, one can check that
e_{4}^{\textup{as}}
is the unique null (relative to
g_{a,M}
) vector field on
\mathcal{B}_{p}
such that
g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\textup{as}},e_{A}^{\textup{as}})=0
and
g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\textup{as}},e_{3}^{\textup{as}})=-2
, and thus
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\textup{aux}}(0)\equiv e_{4}^{\textup{as}}
on
\mathcal{B}_{p}
. Recalling that
\nabla_{e_{4}^{\textup{as}}}e_{4}^{\textup{as}}=\omegah_{a,M}\,e_{4}^{\textup{as}}
and by uniqueness from ODE theory, one concludes that
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\textup{aux}}(0)\equiv e_{4}^{\textup{as}}
on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
.

Remark 6.5
.

As we shall see in the next section, it is crucial that the family of vector fields
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\textup{aux}}(\epsilon)
satisfies the identities (
184
) and (
185
) on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
. On the other hand, the choice of the family of vector fields
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\textup{aux}}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{B}_{p}
can differ from ours as long as
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\textup{aux}}(0)\equiv e_{4}^{\textup{as}}
on
\mathcal{B}_{p}
.

6.2.2

The gauge

For any
p\in\mathcal{M}
, we consider the open sets
\mathcal{B}_{p}
and
\mathcal{R}_{p}
of Section
6.2.1
. We define the scalar functions

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{R}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{R}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

such that
\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon}\equiv r
and
\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}\equiv t^{*}
on
\mathcal{B}_{p}
and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=h_{a,M}

(186)

on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
, with
h_{a,M}
an arbitrary smooth,
nowhere vanishing
scalar function of the Kerr-star coordinates on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
which is independent of
\epsilon
. Since the integral curves of
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)
induce a flow on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
, the functions
(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon})
are
coordinate
functions on the whole
\mathcal{R}_{p}
.

We define the two-spheres

\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}\right\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}

and angular coordinates

(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{2}_{\epsilon})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}

such that
(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{2}_{\epsilon})
are arbitrary angular coordinates on any two-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}}\subset\mathcal{B}_{p}
and

\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{A}_{\epsilon})=0

(187)

on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
, obtaining the differentiable structure

(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{A}_{\epsilon})

on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
. As usual, the angular coordinates
(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{2}_{\epsilon})
only cover a subset of the two-spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}
.

6.2.3

The gauge identities

For any
p\in\mathcal{M}
, we consider the differentiable structure
(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}^{A}_{\epsilon})
on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
of Section
6.2.2
. Let

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{h}}^{\epsilon}_{a,M}:=h_{a,M}^{-1}(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}h_{a,M}-\omegah_{a,M})\,.

(188)

The following proposition proves the gauge identities (
181
), whereas the second point of Proposition
6.2
immediately follows from the identities (
185
), (
186
) and (
187
).

Proposition 6.6
.

We have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}\,\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}\mu}(\epsilon)+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{h}}^{\epsilon}_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}\mu}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0

(189)

on
\mathcal{R}_{p}
, with
x^{\mu}=(\boldsymbol{x}^{1}_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\epsilon}^{A})
.

Proof.

The first of the identities (
189
) is easy to check, in that
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)
takes the coordinate form

\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)=h_{a,M}\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}

from (
186
) and (
187
) and is null relative to
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
by construction. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}},\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}})

\displaystyle=h_{a,M}^{-2}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=0\,.

To check the second set of identities (
189
), we compute

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)}(\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),\partial_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=\omegah_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),\partial_{\mu})+\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)}\partial_{\mu})

\displaystyle=\omegah_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),\partial_{\mu})+h^{2}_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}},\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\mu}\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}})

\displaystyle=\omegah_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),\partial_{\mu})+\frac{1}{2}\,h^{2}_{a,M}\,\partial_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{g}(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}},\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}))

\displaystyle=\omegah_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),\partial_{\mu})\,,

where in the last equality we used the first of the identities (
189
). The left hand side can be written as follows

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)}(\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon),\partial_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)}h_{a,M})\,\boldsymbol{g}(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}},\partial_{\mu})+h_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\text{aux}}(\epsilon)}(\boldsymbol{g}(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}},\partial_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=h_{a,M}\,(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}h_{a,M})\,\boldsymbol{g}(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}},\partial_{\mu})+h^{2}_{a,M}\,\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}}(\boldsymbol{g}(\partial_{\boldsymbol{x}^{2}_{\epsilon}},\partial_{\mu}))\,,

yielding the second set of identities (
189
).

∎

6.3

One-parameter family of frames

To the one-parameter family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
introduced in Section
6.1
, we associate a one-parameter family of local frames

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)=(\eo(\epsilon),\etw(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon))

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
such that

\displaystyle\ef(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=k_{a,M}\,\partial_{\underline{s}}

\displaystyle\et(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{s}+\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\underline{s}}+\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\theta^{A}}\,.

(190)

For each
\epsilon
, the frame quantities
\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)
are smooth scalar functions on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and the frame quantity
\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}(\epsilon)
is a vector field tangent to the
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}
-spheres. All quantities are regular on the whole
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. For each
\epsilon
and any
(s,\theta^{A})
, the frame quantities are
defined
implicitly as solutions to the system of ODEs

\displaystyle k\,\frac{d\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)}{d\underline{s}}=

\displaystyle\,-2\left(\frac{\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{A}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)}{\Omega^{2}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{B}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)}\right)(\Omega^{2}\boldsymbol{g}^{ss}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{C}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{s\theta^{C}}(\epsilon))

(191)

\displaystyle-(\omegah+k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{s\underline{s}}^{s}(\epsilon))\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\underline{s}\theta^{A}}^{s}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{ss}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{A}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{s\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle k\,\frac{d\,\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)}{d\underline{s}}=

\displaystyle\,-2\left(\frac{\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{A}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)}{\Omega^{2}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{B}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)}\right)(\Omega^{2}\boldsymbol{g}^{s\underline{s}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{C}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\underline{s}\theta^{C}}(\epsilon))

(192)

\displaystyle+(k\,\mathfrak{k}-\omegah)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{s\underline{s}}^{\underline{s}}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\underline{s}\theta^{A}}^{\underline{s}}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{s\underline{s}}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{A}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\underline{s}\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle k\,\frac{d\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)}{d\underline{s}}=

\displaystyle\,-2\left(\frac{\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{B}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)}{\Omega^{2}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{C}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{C}}(\epsilon)}\right)(\Omega^{2}\boldsymbol{g}^{s\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{D}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\theta^{A}\theta^{D}}(\epsilon))

(193)

\displaystyle-\omegah\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\underline{s}\theta^{B}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)-k\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{s\underline{s}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{s}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{s\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{\eta}^{\circ}_{\theta^{B}}\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\theta^{A}\theta^{B}}(\epsilon)

in the variable
\underline{s}
, with initial data

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)|_{\underline{s}=0}=\left.-\frac{2\,\underline{j}}{k\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon))}\,\right\rvert_{\underline{s}=0}\,,

(194)

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)|_{\underline{s}=0}=\left.\frac{\underline{j}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{j}\,\underline{k}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{j}\,\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\boldsymbol{b}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+2\,\underline{k}\,\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)+|\underline{\Lambda}|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\epsilon)}}{k\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon))^{2}}-\frac{2\,\underline{k}}{k\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon))}\,\right\rvert_{\underline{s}=0}\,,

(195)

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)|_{\underline{s}=0}=\left.-\frac{2\,\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}}{k\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon))}\,\right\rvert_{\underline{s}=0}\,,

(196)

where
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\mu\nu}^{\sigma}(\epsilon)
denote the Christoffel symbols of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the fixed differentiable structure on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. A choice of the frame vector fields
(\eo(\epsilon),\etw(\epsilon))
is not required. In Section
6.4
, we prove that the family of frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
is well-defined.

As a result of their definition, the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
satisfy the following properties, whose proof is addressed in Section
6.4
.

•

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are null frames

The frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are null relative to the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon
.

•

\ef(\epsilon)
and
\et(\epsilon)
are global, regular and non-degenerate

The frame vector fields (
190
) are global, regular and non-degenerate vector fields on the whole
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, including on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The one-parameter family of horizontal distributions

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}:=\left\langle\et(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon)\right\rangle^{\boldsymbol{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)}}

is globally well-defined on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

•

\ef(\epsilon)
is fixed

The frame vector field
\ef(\epsilon)
remains fixed on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for all
\epsilon
and satisfies the identity

\ef(\epsilon)\equiv e_{4}^{\text{as}}

(197)

for all
\epsilon
.

•

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)
is the algebraically special frame

We have

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)\equiv\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}

with

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(0)

\displaystyle=\underline{j}_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(0)

\displaystyle=\underline{k}_{a,M}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(0)

\displaystyle=\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}_{a,M}\,,

(198)

i.e. the frame coefficients of
\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{3}}(0)
relative to the fixed differentiable structure coincide with the frame coefficients of the vector field
e_{3}^{\text{as}}
relative to the star-normalised, outgoing principal differentiable structure of Kerr from Section
5.6
. In particular, we have

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)}\equiv\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}\,.

•

\boldsymbol{\omegah}(\epsilon)
,
\boldsymbol{\xi}(\epsilon)
and
\underline{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\epsilon)
are fixed

The connection coefficients and curvature components

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omegah}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\omegabh}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\eta}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\etab}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\xi}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\yb}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chih}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\chibh}(\epsilon)\,,\,(\boldsymbol{\textbf{tr}\chi})(\epsilon)\,,\,(\textbf{tr}\boldsymbol{\chib})(\epsilon)\,,\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi})(\epsilon)\,,\,(\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chib})(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{K}}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\rho}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\beta}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\betab}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\alphab}(\epsilon)

of the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
relative to the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are global and regular
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. We have the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omegah}(\epsilon)-\omegah_{a,M}

\displaystyle\equiv 0\,,

(199)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\equiv 0\,,

(200)

\displaystyle\underline{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\epsilon)-\underline{\eta}{}_{a,M}

\displaystyle\equiv 0

(201)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for all
\epsilon
. The geometric quantity
\underline{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\epsilon)
in the identity (
201
) is a global, regular
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-tensor on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
that one can uniquely associate to the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensor
\underline{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\epsilon)
(see Section
7
).

For
\epsilon=0
, all the perturbed geometric quantities coincide with the corresponding connection coefficients and curvature components of the Kerr metric
g_{a,M}
relative to the algebraically special frame
\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}
from Section
5.4
.

Remark 6.7
.

We recall that the vector fields
(e_{3}^{\textup{as}},e_{4}^{\textup{as}})
are global and regular on the whole
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(see Section
5.3
), which makes the identities (
198
) consistent with the property that the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are global and regular on the whole
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. We also point out that, consistently with the identity (
201
), we will have
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\etab}}(0)\equiv\boldsymbol{\etab}(0)
(see Remark
7.36
), and thus
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\etab}}(0)\equiv\etab_{a,M}
.

Remark 6.8
.

Each of the frame properties is independent of the choice of one-parameter family of local frames
(\eo(\epsilon),\etw(\epsilon))
of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
.

For
|a|>0
, the one-parameter family of distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
is a family of
non-integrable
distributions for all
\epsilon
(see Section
6.4
for a proof). Given the Levi-Civita connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\epsilon)
of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
, the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(\epsilon)
over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
is
not
the Levi-Civita connection of the induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
(see the related Remark
4.34
). For
|a|=0
, the distribution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)}
is integrable and the induced connection
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}(0)
over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)}
is the Levi-Civita connection of the induced metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(0)
. See the related comments in Section
1.3.3
.

We introduce the following definition.

Definition 6.9

(Variable distributions)
.

A one-parameter family of
variable distributions

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}(\epsilon)
relative to a fixed distribution
\mathfrak{D}
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is a one-parameter family of distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
such that

•

\dim(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}(\epsilon))_{p}=\dim(\mathfrak{D})_{p}
for all
|\epsilon|\geq 0
and all
p\in\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

•

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}(0)\equiv\mathfrak{D}
.

•

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}(\epsilon)\not\equiv\mathfrak{D}
for all
|\epsilon|>0
.

The one-parameter family of distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is a family of
variable
distributions relative to the fixed distribution
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(0)}\equiv\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
and, for
|\epsilon|>0
, there exists a choice of local frame
(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}})
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
such that at least one of the relations

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\ef(\epsilon),e_{A}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle\neq 0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\et(\epsilon),e_{A}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle\neq 0

holds on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
(see Section
6.4
for a proof). The property persists for
|a|=0
.

We conclude the section with the following definition.

Definition 6.10

(Frame coefficients)
.

We define the
frame coefficients
of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
as the one-parameter families of smooth scalar functions

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\mathfrak{f}}}_{3}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\mathfrak{f}}}_{4}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{3}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{4}(\epsilon)

such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\mathfrak{f}}}_{3}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(\ef(\epsilon),e_{3}^{\textup{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\mathfrak{f}}}_{4}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(\ef(\epsilon),e_{4}^{\textup{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{3}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(\et(\epsilon),e_{3}^{\textup{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{4}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(\et(\epsilon),e_{4}^{\textup{as}})\,,

the one-parameter families of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensors

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{4}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{3}(\epsilon)

such that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{4})_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\textup{as}},\ea(\epsilon))\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{3})_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}(e_{3}^{\textup{as}},\ea(\epsilon))

and the one-parameter families of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}}
one-tensors

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\mathfrak{f}}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{\boldsymbol{1}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{\boldsymbol{2}}(\epsilon)

such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\mathfrak{f}}}_{A}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=g_{a,M}(\ef(\epsilon),e_{A}^{\textup{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{A}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=g_{a,M}(\et(\epsilon),e_{A}^{\textup{as}})\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{\boldsymbol{A}})_{B}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=g_{a,M}(\ea(\epsilon),e_{B}^{\textup{as}})\,.

The following proposition is immediate.

Proposition 6.11
.

For all
\epsilon
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\mathfrak{f}}}_{3}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=1\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\mathfrak{f}}}_{4}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\mathfrak{f}}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0\,.

For
\epsilon=0
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{3}(0)

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{4}(0)

\displaystyle=1\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{4}(0)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{3}(0)=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}}(0)

\displaystyle=0

and
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{\not{\mathfrak{f}}}}_{\boldsymbol{A}})_{B}(0)=\delta_{AB}
.

6.4

Frame construction

Consider the set-up of Section
6.1
. Let

\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}}:=\bigcup_{0\,\leq\,\underline{s}\,\leq\,\underline{s}_{0}}\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,\underline{s}}

for some constant
\underline{s}_{0}>0
. In this section, we prove the following proposition, which implies that the frames introduced in Section
6.3
are well-defined and satisfy the properties stated.

Proposition 6.12
.

There exists a constant
\underline{s}_{0}>0
such that the following statement holds true. For any
(s,\theta^{A})
, the system of ODEs (
191
)-(
193
), with initial data (
194
)-(
196
), admits a unique one-parameter family of smooth (in
\underline{s}
) solutions

(\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon),\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon),\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}(\epsilon))

for all
0\leq\underline{s}\leq\underline{s}_{0}
. By smooth dependence on the initial data, the solutions are smooth in
(s,\theta^{A})
. The resulting one-parameter family of vector fields

\displaystyle\ef(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=k_{a,M}\,\partial_{\underline{s}}

\displaystyle\et(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:=\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{s}+\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\underline{s}}+\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\theta^{A}}

can be completed to a one-parameter family of frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
satisfying all the properties listed in Section
6.3
on
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}}
. Furthermore, the family of distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
is a family of non-integrable and variable distributions.

The section is divided into two parts. In Section
6.4.1
, we prove the abstract local existence and uniqueness result for solutions to the system of ODEs (
191
)-(
193
) claimed by the first part of Proposition
6.12
. In Section
6.4.2
, we establish that the family of frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
defined in Proposition
6.12
satisfies all the properties listed in Section
6.3
, thus proving the second part of the proposition.

6.4.1

The system of ODEs (
191
)-(
193
)

In this section, we prove the following proposition.

Proposition 6.13
.

There exists a real constant
\underline{s}_{0}>0
such that the following statement holds true. For any
(s,\theta^{A})
, the system of ODEs (
191
)-(
193
), with initial data (
194
)-(
196
), admits a unique one-parameter family of smooth (in
\underline{s}
) solutions

(\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon)\,,\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}(\epsilon))

for all
0\leq\underline{s}\leq\underline{s}_{0}
. By smooth dependence on the initial data, the solutions are smooth in
(s,\theta^{A})
.

Proof.

For each
\epsilon
and any
(s,\theta^{A})
, the strategy of the proof is to apply Picard–Lindelöf theorem to the system of ODEs (
191
)-(
193
), with initial data (
194
)-(
196
). To do that, the right hand sides of the equations (
191
)-(
193
) need to be Lipschitz continuous in the unknowns in an open neighbourhood of the initial data (
194
)-(
196
) (recall that the smooth scalar function
k_{a,M}
is nowhere vanishing on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, so the left hand sides of the equations (
191
)-(
193
) pose no issues). To check that, it suffices to show that the denominators

\Omega^{2}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{A}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)

are nowhere vanishing for
\underline{s}=0
. Using the initial data (
194
)-(
196
), we compute

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon)+\underline{b}_{\theta^{A}}\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)|_{\underline{s}=0}

\displaystyle=\left.-2\,\frac{\Omega^{2}\,\underline{j}+\underline{b}_{\theta^{B}}\,\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{B}}}{k\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{C}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{C}}(\epsilon))}\,\right\rvert_{\underline{s}=0}

\displaystyle=\left.-2\,\frac{g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}},e_{3}^{\text{as}})}{k^{2}\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{C}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{C}}(\epsilon))}\,\right\rvert_{\underline{s}=0}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{4}{k^{2}\,(\underline{j}\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)+\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{C}}\underline{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\theta^{C}}(\epsilon))}\,\right\rvert_{\underline{s}=0}

\displaystyle\neq 0\,.

One can thus conclude by Picard–Lindelöf theorem.
∎

Proposition
6.13
proves the first part of Proposition
6.12
.

6.4.2

Frame properties

Consider the one-parameter family of frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}}
defined in Proposition
6.12
. We prove the following proposition.

Proposition 6.14
.

The one-parameter family of frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
satisfies all the properties listed in Section
6.3
on
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}}
. Furthermore, the family of distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
is a family of non-integrable and variable distributions.

Proof.

Let
\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}
be the canonical extension of the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
one-tensor
\etab_{a,M}
(recall Definition
4.17
), i.e. let

\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}\in\Gamma((T\mathcal{M}^{*})^{\star})

such that

\displaystyle\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}(e_{A}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=\etab_{a,M}(e_{A}^{\text{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}(e_{4}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}(e_{3}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=0\,.

Here and throughout the proof, the vector fields
(e_{4}^{\text{as}},e_{3}^{\text{as}})
denote the fixed vector fields (
176
).

The central observation of the proof is that
(\underline{\boldsymbol{j}}(\epsilon),\underline{\boldsymbol{k}}(\epsilon),\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}(\epsilon))
is the unique one-parameter family of solutions to the system of ODEs (
191
)-(
193
) for all
0\leq\underline{s}\leq\underline{s}_{0}
, with initial data (
194
)-(
196
), if and only if the one-parameter family of frame vector fields
\et(\epsilon)
is the unique one-parameter family of solutions to the ODE

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}(\epsilon)}\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{3}}(\epsilon)=\,2\,\boldsymbol{\Xi}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}(\epsilon)-\omegah_{a,M}\et(\epsilon)

(202)

on
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}}
with initial datum

\et(\epsilon)=(\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\text{as}}_{3},e^{\text{as}}_{4}))^{-2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\text{as}}_{3},e^{\text{as}}_{3})\,e^{\text{as}}_{4}-2\,(\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\text{as}}_{3},e^{\text{as}}_{4}))^{-1}\,e^{\text{as}}_{3}

(203)

on
\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}
, with
\boldsymbol{\Xi}(\epsilon)\in\Gamma((T\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}})^{\star})
defined as

\boldsymbol{\Xi}(\epsilon):=\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}-\frac{\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}(\et(\epsilon))}{g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}},\et(\epsilon))}\,g_{a,M}(e_{4}^{\text{as}},\cdot)

(204)

and
\boldsymbol{\Xi}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}(\epsilon)\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}})
such that

\boldsymbol{\Xi}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}(\epsilon)=\boldsymbol{g}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Xi}(\epsilon),\cdot)\,.

This fact is easy (but lengthy) to check by plugging
\et(\epsilon)
in the form (
176
) into the ODE (
202
) and the initial datum (
203
) to obtain the system of ODEs (
191
)-(
193
) with initial data (
194
)-(
196
) respectively. We note the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Xi}(e_{A}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=\etab_{a,M}(e_{A}^{\text{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Xi}(\ef(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Xi}(\et(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=0

and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\Xi}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\Xi}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=0\,.

(205)

We also note the identity
\boldsymbol{\Xi}(0)=\etab_{a,M}^{\circ}
.

We now prove the properties satisfied by the frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
. The observation that
\et(\epsilon)
satisfies the ODE (
202
) is repeatedly exploited.

•

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
are null frames

The identity
\boldsymbol{g}(\ef(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon))=0
on
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}}
is immediate to check. To check the identities

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\ef(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=0\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\et(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=0

on
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}}
, we first compute the Christoffel symbols

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\underline{s}\,\underline{s}}^{\mu}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}^{\mu\sigma}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\underline{s}}\,\boldsymbol{g}_{\underline{s}\sigma}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}\,\delta_{\underline{s}}^{\mu}\,,

with
x^{\mu}=(s,\underline{s},\theta^{A})
. We have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}(\epsilon)}\boldsymbol{e}_{\boldsymbol{4}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=k^{2}_{a,M}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{\underline{s}\,\underline{s}}^{\mu}(\epsilon)\,\partial_{\mu}+k_{a,M}\,(\partial_{\underline{s}}\,k_{a,M})\,\partial_{\underline{s}}

\displaystyle=-k_{a,M}\,\mathfrak{k}_{a,M}\,\ef(\epsilon)+(\partial_{\underline{s}}\,k_{a,M})\ef(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\omegah_{a,M}\,\ef(\epsilon)\,.

(206)

We then combine (
206
) with the equation (
202
) to compute

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\ef(\epsilon)}(\boldsymbol{g}(\ef(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon)))

\displaystyle=2\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\Xi}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}(\epsilon),\ef(\epsilon))-\omegah_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\ef(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))+\omegah_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\ef(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=0\,,

which, using
\boldsymbol{g}(\ef(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))=-2
on
\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}
(easy check left to the reader following from (
203
)), allows to conclude

\boldsymbol{g}(\ef(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))=-2

(207)

on
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}}
. We also compute

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\ef(\epsilon)}(\boldsymbol{g}(\et(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon)))

\displaystyle=4\,\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{\Xi}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp}}(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))-2\omegah_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\et(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))

\displaystyle=-2\omegah_{a,M}\,\boldsymbol{g}(\et(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))\,,

which, using
\boldsymbol{g}(\et(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))=0
on
\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}
(easy check left to the reader following from (
203
)), allows to conclude

\boldsymbol{g}(\et(\epsilon),\et(\epsilon))=0

on
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{s}_{0}}
.

•

\ef(\epsilon)
and
\et(\epsilon)
are global, regular and non-degenerate

This is immediate for
\ef(\epsilon)
. The property that
\et(\epsilon)
is global follows from Proposition
6.13
and the fact that the initial data (
194
)-(
196
) are smooth functions of the coordinates
(s,\theta^{A})
and smoothly extend to global functions on the whole
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,0}
-spheres. In particular,
\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\theta^{A}}(\epsilon)|_{\underline{s}=0}(s,\theta^{B})=0
for some
(s,\theta^{B})
if and only if
\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}|_{\underline{s}=0}(s,\theta^{B})=0
, and thus the vector field
\underline{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}(\epsilon)|_{\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}}
smoothly extends to a global vector field on the whole
\mathbb{S}^{2}_{s,0}
-spheres (as
e_{3}^{\text{as}}|_{\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}}
, and thus
\underline{\Lambda}|_{\mathcal{S}^{*}_{0}}
, do). To prove that
\et(\epsilon)
is non-degenerate, it suffices to observe that the initial data (
194
)-(
196
) all vanish for some
(s,\theta^{B})
if and only if the vector field
e_{3}^{\text{as}}
degenerates at a point
(s,0,\theta^{B})
(i.e.
\underline{j}|_{\underline{s}=0}(s,\theta^{B})=\underline{k}|_{\underline{s}=0}(s,\theta^{B})=\underline{\Lambda}^{\theta^{A}}|_{\underline{s}=0}(s,\theta^{B})=0
). The initial data (
194
)-(
196
) are therefore nowhere trivial, giving rise to a nowhere vanishing local solution from Proposition
6.13
.

•

\ef(\epsilon)
is fixed

This immediately follows from the definition of
\ef(\epsilon)
.
