7

A morphism between horizontal tensor bundles

We consider the fixed ambient manifold
\mathcal{M}^{*}
and the one-parameter family of metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
and associated frames
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
introduced in Sections
6.1
and
6.3
. Since
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
is a family of
variable
distributions (see Definition
6.9
and Proposition
6.12
),
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
covariant tensors and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
covariant tensors are smooth sections of
distinct
abstract tensor bundles on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for
|\epsilon|>0
.

In this section, we prescribe a geometric procedure to uniquely identify any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
covariant tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
with a
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
covariant tensor, which we shall denote by
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}(\epsilon)
. The procedure may be seen as prescribing, for each
\epsilon
, a
bundle morphism
between the bundle of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
covariant tensors and the bundle of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
covariant tensors.
68
68

68

As we shall see, the procedure also yields, for each
\epsilon
, a bundle morphism between the horizontal distributions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
.

The geometric procedure is nonlinear and, in its abstract formulation of this section, does not assume that the metrics
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
solve the vacuum Einstein equations for
|\epsilon|>0
. The procedure will prove fundamental to formulate the
renormalised
vacuum Einstein equations (see Section
8
), where
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
covariant tensors and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
covariant tensors are to be compared.

7.1

Overview

Our geometric procedure starts by considering a global, regular
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
covariant tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
for some
\epsilon
and consists of
three
steps, each step producing a new geometric quantity. The first step, dubbed
extension
, applies Definition
4.17
to obtain the
canonical extension

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\epsilon)
of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
, which is, by definition, a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
-horizontal tensor.
69
69

69

We recall Remark
4.20
for the notion of a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
(and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
)-horizontal (covariant) tensor.
The second step is the
projection
of
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\epsilon)
over the horizontal bundle
\otimes(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})^{\star}
, thus obtaining a
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
-horizontal tensor
(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})(\epsilon)
. The last step is the
restriction
of
(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})(\epsilon)
to a
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensor
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}(\epsilon)
. The final quantity
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}(\epsilon)
is a
global
,
regular

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensor on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
. For
\epsilon=0
, we have
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}(0)\equiv\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(0)
, i.e. our geometric procedure acts as the identity map over the bundle of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
covariant tensors. See Table
1
for a schematic summary of the procedure.

Step

Notation

Tensor bundle

\boldsymbol{1}

Extension

\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\epsilon)

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
-horizontal tensors

\boldsymbol{2}

Projection

(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})(\epsilon)

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
-horizontal tensors

\boldsymbol{3}

Restriction

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}(\epsilon)

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors

Table 1
:

Each of the Sections
7.2
,
7.3
and
7.4
corresponds to one of the three steps of the procedure. The projection step of Section
7.3
is the most intricate. The final result of our procedure is Proposition
7.47
.

The two main technical difficulties of the procedure are outlined below. As we shall describe, both these difficulties are overcome by introducing some
auxiliary
geometric quantities induced
directly
by the
spacetime
geometry of
(\mathcal{M}^{*},\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon))
over
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
. As a result, one has to deal with a delicate mix between the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
quantities arising from applying our geometric procedure to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
quantities and the auxiliary
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
quantities.

•

For later convenience, one needs to apply our geometric procedure to
covariant
and
Lie derivatives
of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors,
70
70

70

Products and contractions of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors are also considered, but they pose less severe technical issues.
i.e. to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors of the form

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma})(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma})(\epsilon)

for some
\boldsymbol{X}(\epsilon)\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M}^{*})
, and be able to relate the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}})(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}})(\epsilon)

(210)

produced by our procedure to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors of the form

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}})(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}})(\epsilon)\,,

(211)

with
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(\epsilon)
a suitable linear connection over the bundle of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\epsilon)
a suitable differential operator over the bundle of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors. The definition of the connection
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(\epsilon)
and the derivation of the formulae relating the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors (
210
) and (
211
) will be rather technical. These are mainly addressed at the level of the
projection
step of the procedure (see Section
7.3.3
), to then obtain the correct formulae in the final restriction step (see, for instance, the formulae in Proposition
7.47
).

It is already worth pointing out that the
auxiliary
connection
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(\epsilon)
may be thought as the connection induced by the
spacetime
Levi-Civita connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\epsilon)
of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
over the bundle of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensors. The connection
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(\epsilon)
is such that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(0)\equiv\nablasl_{a,M}
.

Some of the terms in the final formulae relating the geometric quantities (
210
) and (
211
) will be referred to as
nonlinear terms
and will not be explicitly computed (see Proposition
7.47
). These terms contain products of two or more quantities which identically vanish for
\epsilon=0
.
71
71

71

We note that such a notion of nonlinear terms depends, of course, on our choice of gauge and frames from Section
6
.
We will comment on the structure of the omitted nonlinear terms when relevant.

•

We will have to deal with two distinct metric quantities on
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
. One will be the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
tensor
72
72

72

Note the slight abuse of notation in (
212
) and (
213
), where the tilded quantity (
213
) is an auxiliary quantity and
not
the product of our procedure (which is double-tilded instead).

\widetilde{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}}(\epsilon)

(212)

obtained by applying our procedure to the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
metric tensor
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
, the other will be an
auxiliary
symmetric
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
two-tensor

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(\epsilon)\,,

(213)

which may be thought as the metric induced by the
spacetime
metric
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
over
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
. The quantity (
213
) is such that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(0)\equiv\not{g}_{a,M}
. The auxiliary metric quantity is introduced at the level of the
projection
step of the procedure (see Section
7.3.1
, in particular Definition
7.9
), to then obtain the correct metric quantity (
213
) in the final step of the procedure (see Definition
7.37
).

The quantity (
213
) comes with a crucial computational advantage, in that it can be
explicitly
related to the aforementioned connection
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(\epsilon)
over
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
. Indeed, although
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(\epsilon)
is
not
the Levi-Civita connection of either
\widetilde{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}}(\epsilon)
or
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(\epsilon)
,
73
73

73

We recall that, being
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
a non-integrable distribution, any given metric on
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
admits no Levi-Civita connection, in that all connections over
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
fail to be torsion-free. However, the obstruction is, in this case, even more severe. Indeed, the connection
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(\epsilon)
is not even
compatible
with the metric
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(\epsilon)
(see Remark
7.32
).
the connection
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(\epsilon)
and the metric
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(\epsilon)
are induced by the spacetime connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\epsilon)
and metric
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
respectively, the former being
Levi-Civita
of the latter. This gives the hope that the induced quantities
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\nablasl}}(\epsilon)
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(\epsilon)
inherit some induced version of the well-known explicit formulae relating
\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
.
74
74

74

One instance of these formulae is the explicit expression for the Christoffel symbols of
\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\epsilon)
in terms of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
.
As we shall see, this is indeed the case.

Another fundamental observation will be that the two quantities (
212
) and (
213
) only differ by
nonlinear terms
(see Proposition
7.41
, and Section
7.1.1
for the notion of nonlinear terms), and thus the latter quantity can replace the former in all the formulae
up to nonlinear terms
. The only metric quantity over
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
appearing in our final formulae will be
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(\epsilon)
(see Proposition
7.47
).

Before moving to some more technical remarks, we point out that, in principle, there is no canonical bundle morphism between
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
covariant tensors and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
covariant tensors. Our geometric procedure represents one of the many possible ways to construct a morphism between the two abstract tensor bundles on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.
75
75

75

There are, for instance, ways to extend
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
-horizontal tensors which differ from the one implemented in our procedure. See the related Remark
4.19
.
The fundamental aspect underpinning our particular choice of construction is that our bundle morphism

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{B}_{q}(\epsilon))_{p}\,:\,(\otimes_{q}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)})^{\star},|\cdot|_{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)})_{p}

\displaystyle\rightarrow(\otimes_{q}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})^{\star},|\cdot|_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(\epsilon)})_{p}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{p}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varsigma}}_{p}(\epsilon)

is an
isometry
between normed vector spaces at each
p\in\mathcal{M}^{*}
, with
|\cdot|_{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)}
and
|\cdot|_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(\epsilon)}
the natural pointwise norms induced by
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}(\epsilon)
over the respective tensor bundles (see Proposition
7.47
and, in particular, the identity (
247
)). This fact, discussed in Section
7.5
, guarantees that the later application of the geometric procedure to our problem is legitimate.

7.1.1

Preliminary technical remarks

Some technical remarks before we start:

•

The steps of our geometric procedure apply to global, regular, totally covariant
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensors. The mixed covariant-contravariant tensors appearing in the section (e.g.
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}(\epsilon)
) should be always thought as suitable contractions between the inverse metric
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\epsilon)
and totally covariant tensors. This observation includes the volume form
\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}(\epsilon)
, which is always treated as a totally covariant tensor.

•

Both bolded and unbolded horizontal frame indices will appear, i.e.
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B},\ldots
and
A,B,\ldots
. Bold indices refer to the vector fields
(\eo,\etw)
and are raised/lowered by
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
(and its inverse
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\epsilon)
), unbolded indices refer to the vector fields
(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}})
and are raised/lowered by
\not{g}_{a,M}
(and its inverse
\not{g}^{-1}_{a,M}
). When repeated, indices are either both bolded or both unbolded. The bolded flat symbol
\boldsymbol{\flat}
refers to lowering relative to
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
, the unbolded flat symbol
\flat
refers to lowering relative to
\not{g}_{a,M}
. The choice of the vector fields
(\eo(\epsilon),\etw(\epsilon))
and
(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}})
is arbitrary throughout the section.

•

All the bolded quantities depend on
\epsilon
and may be interpreted as one-parameter families of geometric quantities. All the identities involving
\epsilon
hold for all
\epsilon
. To keep the notation lighter, the
\epsilon
will be often removed.

7.2

Extension

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
, we canonically extend
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
to a
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
-horizontal tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\epsilon)
. The canonical extension is intended as defined in Definition
4.17
.

We define the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
-horizontal tensor

\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(\epsilon)

on
\mathcal{M}^{*}
as the canonical extension of the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
tensor
\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\epsilon)
. We have the identity

\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)=-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\ef})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}(\epsilon)\otimes(\boldsymbol{\et})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}(\epsilon)-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\et})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}(\epsilon)\otimes(\boldsymbol{\ef})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}(\epsilon)+\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(\epsilon)\,.

(214)

Definition 7.1
.

For any
\boldsymbol{f}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}^{*})
, any
\boldsymbol{X}\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M}^{*})
and any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, we define the smooth scalar function
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\boldsymbol{f}
such that

\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\boldsymbol{f}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\boldsymbol{f}\,,

the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
-horizontal one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{f}
such that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{f})(\ea)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{f})(\ea)\,,

the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
-horizontal
k
-tensors
\boldsymbol{\nablasl^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma^{\circ}}
and
\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma^{\circ}}
such that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})

and the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
-horizontal
(k+1)
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\varsigma^{\circ}}
such that

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K+1}}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K+1}}})\,.

We have the following proposition.

Proposition 7.2
.

For any
\boldsymbol{f}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}^{*})
, any
\boldsymbol{X}\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M}^{*})
and any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, we have

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\otimes\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\boldsymbol{\circ}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\boldsymbol{\circ}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\boldsymbol{\circ}}

and

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{X}}}\,\boldsymbol{f})^{\boldsymbol{\circ}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}}\,\boldsymbol{f}\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}\,\boldsymbol{f})^{\boldsymbol{\circ}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl^{\circ}}\,\boldsymbol{f}\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma})^{\boldsymbol{\circ}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma^{\circ}}\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl\varsigma})^{\boldsymbol{\circ}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\varsigma^{\circ}}\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma})^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma^{\circ}}

and similarly for the extension of all differential operators defined in Section
4
.

Proof.

The proof is immediate.

∎

The following is an immediate corollary.

Corollary 7.3
.

For any
\boldsymbol{f}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}^{*})
, any
\boldsymbol{X}\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M}^{*})
and any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, we have

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{f})(\ea)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}\boldsymbol{f})(\ea)

and

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\,\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})\,,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K+1}}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K+1}}})\,.

Proof.

This follows from simply computing, for instance,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\nablasl^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma^{\circ}})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma})^{\circ}(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\nablasl_{\boldsymbol{X}}\varsigma})(\boldsymbol{e_{A_{1}}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{A_{K}}})\,,

where the first equality follows from Proposition
7.2
and the second equality from Definition
4.17
.

∎

7.3

Projection

We start with the main definition of this step of our geometric procedure.

Definition 7.4
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, we define the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}}
-horizontal
k
-tensor
\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}
such that

(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})(e_{A_{1}}^{\textup{as}},\ldots,e_{A_{k}}^{\textup{as}})=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(e_{A_{1}}^{\textup{as}},\ldots,e_{A_{k}}^{\textup{as}})\,.

For later convenience, we note that any local frame
(e_{1}^{\text{as}},e_{2}^{\text{as}})
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
induces a local frame
(\boldsymbol{G}_{1}(\epsilon),\boldsymbol{G}_{2}(\epsilon))
of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
, with

\boldsymbol{G}_{A}(\epsilon)=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{A}(\epsilon),\cdot)\,,

(215)

where
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{A}(\epsilon)
is the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensor such that
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{A}(\eb(\epsilon))=\boldsymbol{g}(e_{A}^{\text{as}},\boldsymbol{e_{B}})
. Note that
\boldsymbol{G}_{A}(0)=e_{A}^{\text{as}}
.

We state the following propositions.

Proposition 7.5
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

k
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, we have

(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})(e_{A_{1}}^{\textup{as}},\ldots,e_{A_{k}}^{\textup{as}})=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\boldsymbol{G}_{A_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{G}_{A_{k}})\,.

Proof.

Immediate from Definition
7.4
.

∎

Proposition 7.6
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
, we have

(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})(\epsilon)=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\epsilon)+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(e_{3}^{\textup{as}})(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\,,

with
(e_{I}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}=g_{a,M}(e_{I}^{\textup{as}},\cdot)
.

Proof.

It is easy to check that

(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})(\epsilon)=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\epsilon)+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(e_{3}^{\text{as}})(e_{4}^{\text{as}})_{\flat}+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(e_{4}^{\text{as}})(e_{3}^{\text{as}})_{\flat}\,.

Crucially, for our choice of frames, we have
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(e_{4}^{\text{as}})=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}(\ef(\epsilon))=0
.

∎

Proposition 7.7
.

We have

\displaystyle(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(\epsilon)=\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(e^{\textup{as}}_{3},e^{\textup{as}}_{A})(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{A}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(e^{\textup{as}}_{A},e^{\textup{as}}_{3})(e_{A}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(e^{\textup{as}}_{3},e^{\textup{as}}_{3})(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\,.

Proof.

Easy check, analogous to the proof of Proposition
7.6
.

∎

The following definition will be useful to write some of the upcoming formulae in a compact fashion.

Definition 7.8
.

We define the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
-horizontal one-tensors
\Pi(\ef)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}
,
\Pi(\et)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}
and
\Pi(\ea)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}
such that

\displaystyle\Pi(\ef)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}(e_{A}^{\textup{as}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\ef,e_{A}^{\textup{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\Pi(\et)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}(e_{A}^{\textup{as}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\et,e_{A}^{\textup{as}})\,,

\displaystyle\Pi(\ea)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}(e_{B}^{\textup{as}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(\ea,e_{B}^{\textup{as}})\,.

7.3.1

Metric quantities

We start with the definition of an
auxiliary
metric quantity
, which arises from projecting the spacetime metric
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
onto
\otimes_{2}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}})^{\star}
. For some preliminary comments on this part of the section, see the overview in Section
7.1
.

Definition 7.9
.

We define the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}}
-horizontal two-tensor
(\Pi\boldsymbol{g})(\epsilon)
such that

(\Pi\boldsymbol{g})(e_{A}^{\textup{as}},e_{B}^{\textup{as}})=\boldsymbol{g}(e_{A}^{\textup{as}},e_{B}^{\textup{as}})\,.

We have the following proposition.

Proposition 7.10
.

We have

\displaystyle(\Pi\boldsymbol{g})(\epsilon)=\,\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\textup{as}}_{3},e^{\textup{as}}_{A})(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{A}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\textup{as}}_{A},e^{\textup{as}}_{3})(e_{A}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}-\frac{1}{4}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\textup{as}}_{3},e^{\textup{as}}_{3})(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\textup{as}}_{4},e^{\textup{as}}_{A})(e_{3}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{A}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}+\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\textup{as}}_{A},e^{\textup{as}}_{4})(e_{A}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{3}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}-\frac{1}{4}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\textup{as}}_{3},e^{\textup{as}}_{4})(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{3}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e^{\textup{as}}_{4},e^{\textup{as}}_{3})(e_{3}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\otimes(e_{4}^{\textup{as}})_{\flat}\,.

Note that we have the four distinct spacetime tensors

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi\boldsymbol{g})(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(\epsilon)\,,

with

\displaystyle(\Pi\boldsymbol{g})(0)

\displaystyle\equiv\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(0)\equiv(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(0)\equiv\not{g}_{a,M}^{\circ}\,.

Note that
(\not{g}_{a,M}^{\circ})_{AB}=(g_{a,M})_{AB}
, but
\not{g}_{a,M}^{\circ}\not\equiv g_{a,M}
.

We have the following proposition, which will be crucial.

Proposition 7.11
.

We have

(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(\epsilon)=(\Pi\boldsymbol{g})(\epsilon)+\textup{nonlinear terms}\,.

(216)

Proof.

It suffices to show that the two quantities agree, up to nonlinear terms, when evaluated relative to an arbitrary choice of
(e_{A}^{\text{as}},e_{B}^{\text{as}})
. Using the identity (
214
), we have

\displaystyle(\Pi\boldsymbol{g})(e_{A}^{\text{as}},e_{B}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{g}(e_{A}^{\text{as}},e_{B}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(e_{A}^{\text{as}},e_{B}^{\text{as}})-\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e_{A}^{\text{as}},\boldsymbol{e_{3}})\,\boldsymbol{g}(e_{B}^{\text{as}},\boldsymbol{e_{4}})-\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e_{A}^{\text{as}},\boldsymbol{e_{4}})\,\boldsymbol{g}(e_{B}^{\text{as}},\boldsymbol{e_{3}})

\displaystyle=(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e_{A}^{\text{as}},e_{B}^{\text{as}})-\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e_{A}^{\text{as}},\boldsymbol{e_{3}})\,\boldsymbol{g}(e_{B}^{\text{as}},\boldsymbol{e_{4}})-\frac{1}{2}\,\boldsymbol{g}(e_{A}^{\text{as}},\boldsymbol{e_{4}})\,\boldsymbol{g}(e_{B}^{\text{as}},\boldsymbol{e_{3}})\,,

where the last two terms in the last line are nonlinear.

∎

We shall now define a key contravariant tensor. To do that, let
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{1}(\epsilon),\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{2}(\epsilon))
be the local co-frame of
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)})^{\star}
relative to
(\boldsymbol{G}_{1}(\epsilon),\boldsymbol{G}_{2}(\epsilon))
from (
215
), i.e.

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{B}(\boldsymbol{G}_{A})

\displaystyle=\delta_{A}^{B}\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{G}_{C}^{\boldsymbol{B}}\,\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{C}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\boldsymbol{B}}\,.

Note that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{A}(0)=\not{g}_{a,M}(e_{A}^{\text{as}},\cdot)
.

Definition 7.12
.

We define the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}}
-horizontal (contravariant) tensor

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})(\epsilon)

(217)

such that

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ}=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{A},\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{B})\,e_{A}^{\textup{as}}\otimes e_{B}^{\textup{as}}

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})(g_{a,M}(e_{A}^{\textup{as}},\cdot),g_{a,M}(e_{B}^{\textup{as}},\cdot))=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{A},\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{B})\,.

We adopt the notation

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})(g_{a,M}(e_{A}^{\text{as}},\cdot),g_{a,M}(e_{B}^{\text{as}},\cdot))

\displaystyle=(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})^{AB}\,.

We have the following proposition, which allows to interpret the tensor (
217
) as the inverse of
(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(\epsilon)
in a suitable sense.
76
76

76

Strictly speaking, the tensor
(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(\epsilon)
is not invertible.
Moreover, the tensor (
217
) may be interpreted as the inverse of
(\Pi\boldsymbol{g})(\epsilon)
in a suitable sense, up to nonlinear terms.

Proposition 7.13
.

We have

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})^{BC}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})_{AC}

\displaystyle=\delta_{A}^{B}\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})^{BC}(\Pi\boldsymbol{g})_{AC}

\displaystyle=\delta_{A}^{B}+\textup{nonlinear terms}\,.

Proof.

From Proposition
7.5
, we note that

\displaystyle(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e_{A}^{\text{as}},e_{B}^{\text{as}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{G}_{A},\boldsymbol{G}_{B})\,.

We compute

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})^{BC}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})_{AC}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{B},\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{C})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}(\boldsymbol{G}_{A},\boldsymbol{G}_{C})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{B}(\boldsymbol{G}_{A})

\displaystyle=\delta_{A}^{B}\,.

This proves the first identity. For the second identity, we apply Proposition
7.11
.

∎

The following proposition is useful to treat the projected volume form.

Proposition 7.14
.

We have

\displaystyle(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}^{\boldsymbol{\circ}})(e_{A}^{\textup{as}},e_{B}^{\textup{as}})=

\displaystyle\,(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e^{\textup{as}}_{A},\boldsymbol{e_{1}})\cdot(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e^{\textup{as}}_{B},\boldsymbol{e_{2}})-(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e^{\textup{as}}_{A},\boldsymbol{e_{2}})\cdot(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e^{\textup{as}}_{B},\boldsymbol{e_{1}})

\displaystyle+\textup{nonlinear terms}\,.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}^{\boldsymbol{\circ}})(e_{A}^{\textup{as}},e_{B}^{\textup{as}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}^{\boldsymbol{\circ}}(e_{A}^{\textup{as}},e_{B}^{\textup{as}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(e^{\textup{as}}_{A},\boldsymbol{e_{1}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(e^{\textup{as}}_{B},\boldsymbol{e_{2}})-\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(e^{\textup{as}}_{A},\boldsymbol{e_{2}})\,\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}(e^{\textup{as}}_{B},\boldsymbol{e_{1}})

\displaystyle=(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e^{\textup{as}}_{A},\boldsymbol{e_{1}})\,(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e^{\textup{as}}_{B},\boldsymbol{e_{2}})-(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e^{\textup{as}}_{A},\boldsymbol{e_{2}})\,(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(e^{\textup{as}}_{B},\boldsymbol{e_{1}})+\text{nonlinear terms}\,.

∎

7.3.2

Projection of products and contractions of tensors

We start with the following proposition.

Proposition 7.15
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

k_{1}
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
and any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}

k_{2}
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\epsilon)
, we have

\Pi(\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\otimes\boldsymbol{\theta})^{\circ}=(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})\otimes(\Pi\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\,.

Proof.

This is immediate by using the identity for tensor products from Proposition
7.2
.

∎

We give two definitions for products of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
-horizontal tensors. The first one allows for some immediate considerations (see Propositions
7.18
and
7.19
), the second one will be applied later on (see, for instance, Proposition
7.33
). Note that, in Definition
7.17
, the replacement of
(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ})(\epsilon)
by
(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{g})(\epsilon)
is intentional and will be convenient in the sequel.

Definition 7.16
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensors
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\epsilon)
, we define the product

\displaystyle(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ},\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})_{\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}}

\displaystyle:=(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})^{AB}(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})_{A}(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})_{B}\,.

Definition 7.17
.

We define the product

\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{I}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}\,\widehat{\otimes}_{\Pi\,\boldsymbol{g}}\,\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{J}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}:=\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{I}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}\otimes\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{J}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}+\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{J}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}\otimes\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{I}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}-(\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{I}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}},\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{J}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}})_{\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}}\,(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{g})\,.

The following convenient proposition holds.

Proposition 7.18
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensors
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
two-tensor
\boldsymbol{\omega}(\epsilon)
, we have the identity between smooth scalar functions

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})^{AB}(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})_{A}(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})_{B}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\,,

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})^{AB}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\circ})_{AB}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,.

In particular,

\displaystyle(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ},\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})_{\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}\,,

\displaystyle|\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}|^{2}_{\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}}

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{\varsigma}|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\not{g}}}\,.

(218)

Analogous formulae hold for inner products of higher rank tensors and for more general full contractions.

Proof.

We use Proposition
7.5
and Definition
7.12
to compute

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ})^{AB}(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})_{A}(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})_{B}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{A},\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{B})\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\boldsymbol{G}_{A})\,\boldsymbol{\theta}(\boldsymbol{G}_{B})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\,,

where the second identity holds because tensor contractions are independent of the choice of basis.

∎

We have the following proposition for partial contractions.

Proposition 7.19
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
two-tensor
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\epsilon)
, we have

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\boldsymbol{\circ}})^{BC}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})_{AC}(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})_{B}

\displaystyle=(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\theta}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\varsigma})^{\circ})_{A}\,.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\boldsymbol{\circ}})^{BC}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})_{AC}(\Pi\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})_{B}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{B},\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}{}^{C})\,\boldsymbol{\theta}(\boldsymbol{G}_{A},\boldsymbol{G}_{C})\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\boldsymbol{G}_{B})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{BC}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta}(\boldsymbol{G}_{A},\boldsymbol{e_{C}})\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\,,

where the second identity holds because tensor contractions are independent of the choice of basis. By Proposition
7.5
, we have

(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\theta}{}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\varsigma})^{\circ})_{A}=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{BC}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta}(\boldsymbol{G}_{A},\boldsymbol{e_{C}})\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\,.

∎

Remark 7.20
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensors
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}(\epsilon)
, Propositions
7.18
and
7.19
allow to treat quantities of the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\boldsymbol{\wedge}\boldsymbol{\theta}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\,.

Indeed, one first writes

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\boldsymbol{\wedge}\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{AC}}\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\boldsymbol{BD}}\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{CD}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}{}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\,\boldsymbol{\theta}{}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\,,

\displaystyle{}^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}

and then, by applying Propositions
7.18
and
7.19
, gets

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ}){}^{AC}(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ}){}^{BD}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}^{\circ}})_{CD}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}){}_{A}\,(\Pi\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}){}_{B}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varsigma}\boldsymbol{\wedge}\boldsymbol{\theta}

\displaystyle(\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{-1})^{\circ}){}^{BC}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{\varepsilon}^{\circ}})_{AC}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}){}_{B}

\displaystyle=(\Pi({}^{\boldsymbol{\star}}\boldsymbol{\varsigma})^{\circ})_{A}\,.

7.3.3

Projection of covariant and Lie derivatives

In this section, we present some technical propositions to treat the projection of covariant and Lie derivatives. The formulae in the propositions relate the raw projection of the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
-horizontal tensors obtained in the extension step of the procedure, e.g. the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
-horizontal tensor

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\circ}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})\,,

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\not{\mathcal{L}}}^{\circ}_{X}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})\,,

to more convenient
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
-horizontal tensors, e.g.
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\text{as}}}
-horizontal tensors of the form

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{4}}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}))\,,

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{X}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}))\,,

with
\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\epsilon)
the spacetime Levi-Civita connection of
\boldsymbol{g}(\epsilon)
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{X}(\epsilon)
the spacetime Lie derivative. We recall that all the formulae are independent of the choice of the one-parameter family
(\eo(\epsilon),\etw(\epsilon))
. For some preliminary comments on this part of the section, see the overview in Section
7.1
.

We give a brief outline of the propositions addressing the projection of the
covariant derivatives
. Propositions
7.21
and
7.22
state the main formulae for the covariant derivatives of scalar functions and one-tensors respectively. The proof of Proposition
7.22
is the most technical part of the section. Proposition
7.27
states the formulae for the covariant derivative of one-tensors in a special case. Proposition
7.28
states the relevant formulae for the covariant derivative of higher-rank tensors. The set of propositions of the present section can be combined with those from Sections
7.3.1
and
7.3.2
to write formulae for the projection of all the differential operators defined in Section
4
. We give an example for the projection of a more complicated differential operator at the end of this section, in Proposition
7.33
. The treatment of the projection of the
Lie derivatives
is somewhat less involved and is limited to Proposition
7.30
.

We are now ready to start.

Proposition 7.21
.

For any
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}^{*})
, we have

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nabla}f)=

\displaystyle\,\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}f)-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{3}}f)\,\Pi(\ef)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{4}}f)\,\Pi(\et)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}\,,

(219)

where we recall Definition
7.8
of the
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{N}_{\textup{as}}}
-horizontal one-tensors
\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{I}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}(\epsilon)
.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}f

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}f-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{3}}f)(\ef)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{4}}f)(\et)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}f-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{3}}f)(\ef)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}_{\boldsymbol{4}}f)(\et)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}\,,

where the second identity uses Definition
7.1
. We then take the projection of both the left and right hand sides.

∎

Proposition 7.22
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{D}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)}
one-tensor
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(\epsilon)
, we have

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{4}}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}))=

\displaystyle\,\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{4}}^{\circ}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})+(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ},\Pi\boldsymbol{\etab}^{\circ})_{\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}}\cdot\Pi(\ef)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}

(220)

\displaystyle+\textup{nonlinear terms}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{3}}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}))=

\displaystyle\,\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{3}}^{\circ}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})+(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ},\Pi\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\circ})_{\Pi\boldsymbol{\not{g}}^{\circ}}\cdot\Pi(\et)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}-g_{a,M}^{-1}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}^{\circ})\cdot(\Pi\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\circ})

(221)

\displaystyle+\textup{nonlinear terms}\,,

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{A}}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}))=

\displaystyle\,\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\circ}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})+\frac{1}{2}\left({\boldsymbol{\chib}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}}{}_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\boldsymbol{B}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\right)\Pi(\ef)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}+\frac{1}{2}\left({\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}}{}_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\boldsymbol{B}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\right)\Pi(\et)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}

(222)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,g_{a,M}^{-1}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ},\boldsymbol{\underline{\mathfrak{f}}}^{\circ})\cdot{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\boldsymbol{\sharp_{2}}}}{}_{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\boldsymbol{B}}\,\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{B}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}

\displaystyle+\textup{nonlinear terms}

and

\displaystyle\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\Pi\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ}))=

\displaystyle\,\Pi(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\varsigma}^{\circ})+\frac{1}{2}\left({\boldsymbol{\chib}}{}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\right)\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{A}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}\otimes\Pi(\ef)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}+\frac{1}{2}\left({\boldsymbol{\chi}}{}^{\boldsymbol{AB}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma}_{\boldsymbol{B}}\right)\Pi(\boldsymbol{e_{A}})_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}\otimes\Pi(\et)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}

(223)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{4}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma})^{\boldsymbol{A}}\,\Pi(\et)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}\otimes\Pi(\ea)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}-\frac{1}{2}\,(\boldsymbol{\nablasl}_{\boldsymbol{3}}\,\boldsymbol{\varsigma})^{\boldsymbol{A}}\,\Pi(\ef)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}\otimes\Pi(\ea)_{\boldsymbol{\flat}}
